(k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
, there exist PSD matrices
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{k_{N}\in{\cal N}}W_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N}\},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq{\cal K}_{n}\subsetneq{\cal N},\,\forall\,(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\in{\cal N}\backslash{\cal K}_{n},\\[2.84526pt]
\ \ \Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}\!\!W_{({\cal K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\!=\!\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!W_{\!({\cal K}_{n}\backslash\{k_{n}\},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\!\otimes\!\mathbbm{1}^{\!A_{k_{n}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})\in{\cal N},\ \Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}W_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}=q_{\pi}\,\mathbbm{1}^{P},\\[8.53581pt]
\hskip 128.0374ptq_{\pi}\geq 0,\,\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}=1.\end{array}\right.

This
QC-supFO
class is the quantum-controlled analogue of the classically and non-dynamically-controlled
QC-convFO
, in the same way as QC-QCs are the quantum-controlled analogue of QC-CCs. The characterisations and the notations we use reflect this: Eq. (
III.3.2
) above involves matrices
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, while the characterisation of
QC-convFO
in Eq. (
III.2.2
) involved matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
; in both cases, the subscripts include the first
n
parties of a given order (forgetting about the order of the first
n-1
parties, in the QC-QC or QC-supFO case), while the superscript (not present in the general QC-CC and QC-QC decompositions) display the ordered list of remaining parties. We note also that just like the
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
’s, the matrices
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
here can be seen to be valid process matrices (up to normalisation).
Comparing the two classes, one has
\textup{{QC-convFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-supFO}}
, the inclusion being strict (in general) for
N\geq 2
if
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is nontrivial, or for
N\geq 3
if
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is trivial.

It is interesting to observe, also, that the characterisation of QC-supFOs above displays some form of symmetry with that of general QC-CCs, Eq. (
III.2.1
): for QC-CCs the total causal order is not encoded in the decomposition at the “lower levels” (for small
n
), but it appears in the matrices involved in the decomposition as we go “up” (to larger
n
) in the decomposition. For QC-supFOs it is the opposite: the causal order is encoded in the decomposition at the lower levels, but gets erased as we go up in the decomposition.
This symmetry can also be seen in the equations: the sums appear on the left-hand side of the constraints in Eq. (
III.2.1
), and on the right-hand side of the constraints in Eq. (
III.3.2
) (notice that for QC-QCs they appear on both sides). In Appendix
D.4
we propose a graphical representation of the SDP constraints for our different classes, which allows one, in particular, to clearly visualise the symmetry between the decompositions of QC-CCs and of QC-supFOs.

Comparing it to the full set of QC-QCs, it can clearly be seen that
QC-supFO
is a subclass of
QC-QC
:
\textup{{QC-supFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-QC}}
, the inclusion being in general strict for
N\geq 2
with a nontrivial
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, or for
N\geq 3
with a trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P}
(see Appendix
D.3.4
; the situation is thus similar to that of
QC-convFO
vs
QC-CC
). It contains on the other hand the subclass of QC-convFOs:
\textup{{QC-convFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-supFO}}
, the inclusion being in general strict for
N\geq 2
with a nontrivial
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, or for
N\geq 3
with a trivial
\mathcal{H}^{F}
(see also Appendix
D.3.4
; here the situation is similar to that of
QC-CC
vs
QC-QC
).
Furthermore, building on the proof that QC-QCs produce causal correlations, we show in Appendix
E.2.2
that QC-supFOs produce correlations in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, which is consistent with the claim that these processes feature no dynamical causal order; see Fig.
6
.

The definition and characterisation of QC-supFOs allows us to now properly formalise the claim that some processes can have
both indefinite and dynamical causal order
. Indeed, this can be said of any QC-QC that is not a QC-CC,
12
12

12

We recall that it remains an open question, whether for
N\geq 4
, all causally separable processes are QC-CCs
Wechs et al. 2019
;
Wechs et al. 2021
. Here we overlook this subtlety for the case of quantum circuits, and consider all those that are not QC-CCs to have indefinite causal order.

but also not a QC-supFO—i.e., whose process matrix is in
\textup{{QC-QC}}\setminus(\textup{{QC-CC}}\cup\textup{{QC-supFO}})
. Explicit examples will be given below.

To finish with the presentation of QC-supFOs, let us mention that another somewhat related class was introduced in Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
(just called
Sup
there), which may have also looked like a natural candidate to characterise processes with non-dynamical superpositions of causal orders. The idea was to impose that if one traces out the global future system in
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, the reduced circuit should end up in just a convex mixture of fixed-order processes (in
QC-convFO
)—as is indeed the case for the “static” quantum switch. However, we argue that such a constraint is too restrictive. It also has some undesirable features, e.g. that if several parties have no output Hilbert space and could be considered to be in the global future, then the definition crucially depends on whose Hilbert space is called
\mathcal{H}^{F}
. We discuss this in more details in Appendix
D.3.2
. In fact, we show that by generalising the
Sup
class in an adequate way, so as precisely to avoid the unwanted features, one naturally obtains the same characterisation as in Eq. (
III.3.2
). This alternative perspective lends further support to the claim that our
QC-supFO
class appropriately characterises quantum circuits with non-dynamical quantum control of causal order.

III.3.3
The
QC-NIQC
subclass

Let us finally introduce one last class: the quantum-controlled counterpart of QC-NICCs, namely
quantum circuits with non-influenceable quantum control of causal order
(QC-NIQCs).

Recall that for QC-NICCs, although the (classical) state of the control could be dynamical (if it cannot be fixed in advance), we imposed that it should not depend on the choice of parties’ TP operations (it must be non-influenceable). One may be tempted to impose the same condition, now for the quantum control system. However, this would not quite correspond to what we want. To see this, consider the “static” quantum switch, where a control qubit, initialised in the fixed state
{|+\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({|0\rangle}+{|1\rangle})
, coherently controls the order between two operations applied on a target system
Chiribella et al. 2013
(see also Eq. (
57
) below for its process matrix description). This example is clearly a QC-supFO, so we do not want to say that its quantum control features any form of dynamicality. However, the reduced state of the control system
does
depend on the two external operations: e.g. the output state of the control qubit is
{|+\rangle}
if (the Kraus operators of) the two external operations commute, or
{|-\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({|0\rangle}-{|1\rangle})
if these anti-commute—this is in fact at the very heart of the best-known application of the quantum switch
Chiribella 2012
.
What
does not
depend on the external operations in this example are the
classical weights
corresponding to each fixed order in the quantum-controlled superposition of orders—that is, the diagonal elements of the state of the control system, when written in the basis that defines the controllisation (the
\{{|0\rangle},{|1\rangle}\}
basis here).

This is precisely what we shall impose here to define QC-NIQCs, extending it to the general form of the control system as defined in QC-QCs:

QC-NIQCs are the QC-QCs for which at any intermediate time step, the classical weights of each term in the basis
\{{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}}\}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}
that defines the controllisation—i.e. the diagonal elements of the state of the control system in that basis—are independent of the choice of previously applied external operations and of the potential initial state preparation in the global past.

We denote by
QC-NIQC
the set of process matrices that describe QC-NIQCs. We show in Appendix
D.3.3
that the above constraint implies the following characterisation:
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})
is in
QC-NIQC
if and only if
W\geq 0
and it has a
QC-QC
decomposition in terms of PSD matrices
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
as in Eq. (
III.3.1
), with the additional constraint that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r}\text{all}\ W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\text{'s are valid process matrices}\\
\text{(up to normalisation).}\end{array}

This additional constraint is thus quite analogous to the one we obtained for QC-NICCs, Eq. (
III.2.3
).
Note however that, contrary to QC-NICCs, it is not sufficient here to verify the validity for only the “highest” terms
W_{({\cal K}_{N-1},k_{N})}
to ensure that Eq. (
III.3.3
) is satisfied.

We show in Appendix
D.3.4
that for
N\leq 3
,
QC-NIQC
coincides with
QC-supFO
, but for
N\geq 4
it defines a class in between
QC-supFO
and
QC-NIQC
, as depicted in Fig.
6
:

\displaystyle\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-QC}}}.

(56)

It can also easily be seen that
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}
(the inclusion being again generally strict for
N\geq 2
if
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is nontrivial, or for
N\geq 3
if
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is trivial, as in the previous cases of
QC-CC
vs
QC-QC
, and of
QC-convFO
vs
QC-supFO
).

Considering now the correlations generated by QC-NIQCs, one can show (see Appendix
E.2.3
) that these are necessarily in the polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
of causal correlations with “non-influenceable coarse-grained order”, which we pre-emptively introduced in Sec.
II.3
. This can be understood by recalling that the control state in a QC-QC only encodes, at any given time step, the “coarse-grained” order
(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})
(with
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
containing all “past” parties, grouped together). When imposing the non-influenceability of (the diagonal elements of) the control state, it is then natural to expect that the distribution of
p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})\big|\vec{x}\big)
obtained from a causal decomposition of some induced correlation would not depend on the choice of external operations.

One may then wonder whether the stronger statement holds, that the non-coarse-grained probabilities
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\big|\vec{x}\big)
also do not depend on the external operations, i.e. that the correlations created by QC-NIQCs are in the smaller polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
. We leave this as an open question: we could not show this in general, but neither did we find any QC-NIQC correlation in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
(see Sec.
IV
).

III.3.4
Examples

We now provide examples of process matrices that belong to each of the
three classes with quantum control of causal order introduced above.
13
13

13

For each of the three examples detailed below, we provide in Appendix
D.3.5
an explicit decomposition, which matches the characterisation of the corresponding class.

Again, our claims that a given process matrix does
not
belong to a given class can be verified through SDP (see Footnote
9
).

First, the quantum switch with a fixed initial state of the control system
Chiribella et al. 2013
—the “static”, or “non-dynamical” quantum switch (already referred to at the beginning of Sec.
III.3.3
)—is an example of a process in
\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\setminus\text{{{QC-convFO}}}
for
N=2
parties (with a trivial global past space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
but a nontrivial future space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
). Considering that the future Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{F}=\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{t}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{c}}}
receives the target (in
\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{t}}}
) and control (in
\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{c}}}
) systems, its process matrix is defined as

\displaystyle W_{\textup{supFO}}={|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{supFO}}|}\ \ \text{with}

\displaystyle{|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{|\psi\rangle}^{A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|0\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36967pt+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{|\psi\rangle}^{B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|1\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}.

(57)

This process corresponds to a coherent superposition of two branches where
A\prec B
or
B\prec A
, with the target system being sent in each branch to the first party and forwarded through identity channels to the second party and then to
\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{t}}}
, and with a control qubit initially in the fixed state
{|+\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({|0\rangle}+{|1\rangle})
and finally sent to
\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{c}}}
.

Our second example here is of a process with dynamical but non-influenceable quantum control of causal order, i.e. in
\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\setminus(\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cup\text{{{QC-NICC}}})
, for the
N=4
case with trivial spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
. Let us first define the ket vectors

\displaystyle{|\Phi_{00}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}({|0,0\rangle}+{|1,1\rangle}),

\displaystyle{|\Phi_{11}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}({|0,0\rangle}-{|1,1\rangle}),

\displaystyle{|\Phi_{01}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}({|0,0\rangle}+i{|1,1\rangle}),

\displaystyle{|\Phi_{10}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}({|0,0\rangle}-i{|1,1\rangle}),

(58)

and the matrices

\displaystyle W_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b}{|\Phi_{ab}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{ab}|}^{A^{I}B^{I}}\otimes{|a,b\rangle\!\langle a,b|}^{A^{O}B^{O}},

\displaystyle\quad W_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b}{|\Phi_{ba}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{ba}|}^{A^{I}B^{I}}\otimes{|a,b\rangle\!\langle a,b|}^{A^{O}B^{O}},

(59)

which can both be shown to be bipartite causally nonseparable process matrices in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{IO}B^{IO}})
.
We then define
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
as:

\displaystyle W_{\textup{NIQC}}=

\displaystyle\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{0}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{C^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{C^{O}D^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D^{O}}

\displaystyle+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{1}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{D^{I}}\!\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{D^{O}C^{I}}\!\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C^{O}}.

(60)

As for any QC-QC, one can find an implementation of this process by constructing internal operations from its QC-QC decomposition, through a procedure detailed in Appendix
D.3.3

Wechs et al. 2021
.
The process matrix
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
has a similar structure to
W_{\textup{NICC}}
, and one can also find an implementation compatible with the coarse-grained causal order
\{A,B\}\prec\{C,D\}
, which is graphically represented in Fig.
7
. However, in contrast to
W_{\textup{NICC}}
, the order between
A
and
B
is now coherently controlled using a control qubit initialised in the state
{|+\rangle}
. Then,
\{E_{+},E_{-}\}
, with
E_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}[\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 3}\pm\frac{1}{2}(XX+YY)Y]
and implicit tensor products, defines a POVM (no longer a projective measurement) performed on the output spaces of
A
and
B
, along with the auxiliary and control spaces, whose outcome controls classically the causal order between
C
and
D
(
C\prec D
for outcome
+
and
D\prec C
for outcome
-
). One can explain why
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
has dynamical but non-influenceable causal order with the same reasoning as for
W_{\textup{NICC}}
: dynamicality relates to the fact that
W_{0/1}
are causally nonseparable, while non-influenceability is due to the fact that the probabilities for the outcomes
\pm
of the POVM
\{E_{+},E_{-}\}
are independent of the operations performed by
A
and
B
(these probabilities are
\frac{1}{2}
for both outcomes). The only different qualitative feature between these two processes is the coherent control of causal order between
A
and
B
in
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
. This raises questions concerning other potential features that would differentiate
QC-NICC
from
QC-NIQC
; we will comment on this in the outlook below.

Figure 7:
Circuit-like representation of
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
. The causal order between
A
and
B
is coherently controlled (illustrated by the overlap between gates
A
and
B
) by a control qubit prepared in the state
{|+\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({|0\rangle}+{|1\rangle})
. The first party to act (be it
A
or
B
) is given half of the state
{|\Phi^{+}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({|0,0\rangle}+{|1,1\rangle})
. An internal unitary, composed of a Swap and a Controlled-
Z
gate, is applied between the first and the second operations in both branches of the causal superposition between
A
and
B
. The output state coming from the output Hilbert space of the second operation, the auxiliary space and the control Hilbert spaces are then measured by the POVM defined by
\{E_{+},E_{-}\}
(see main text).
As in the implementation of
W_{\textup{NICC}}
shown on Fig.
4
, a new system in the state
{|\psi\rangle}
is then, according to the measurement result, either sent to
C
then forwarded to
D
, or sent to
D
then forwarded to
C
—the two corresponding branches of the circuit being exclusive.

Next, the “dynamical quantum switch” (as opposed to the static one), where the qubit that coherently controls the causal order between two parties
A
and
B
is not fixed
a priori
, but is left to be prepared (in any possible state) in the past Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, is an example of a process with influenceable quantum control of causal order in
\textup{{QC-QC}}\setminus(\textup{{QC-NIQC}}\cup\textup{{QC-CC}})
(for
N=2
, with now both
\mathcal{H}^{P}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}
being nontrivial). Considering again that the target and control systems are ultimately sent to the future Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{F}=\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{t}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{c}}}
, its process matrix is defined here as

\displaystyle W_{\textup{QS}}={|w_{\textup{QS}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{QS}}|}\ \ \text{with}

\displaystyle{|w_{\textup{QS}}\rangle}={|0\rangle}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle}^{A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|0\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt+{|1\rangle}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle}^{B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|1\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}.

(61)

Compared to
{|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle}
in Eq. (
57
), here the past is “open”, so that the input state of the control system in
\mathcal{H}^{P}
can influence the weight of the two branches, corresponding to
A\prec B
and
B\prec A
.

The other new explicit example of a QC-QC presented in
Wechs et al. 2021
(dubbed the “Grenoble process” in Ref.
Vanrietvelde et al. 2022
) is also an example of a process in
\textup{{QC-QC}}\setminus(\textup{{QC-NIQC}}\cup\textup{{QC-CC}})
, for
N=3
. The “double quantum switch” from Ref.
Salzger 2023
is yet another example in the same class, now for
N=4
. This process is obtained by composing two quantum switches, where the control qubit of the first quantum switch (between
A
and
B
, controlled in the
\{{|0\rangle},{|1\rangle}\}
basis) is transmitted in order to control the second quantum switch (between
C
and
D
), in the complementary
\{{|+\rangle},{|-\rangle}\}
basis.
Since the projection onto the
\{{|\pm\rangle}\}
basis of the control qubit after the first quantum switch depends on the operations performed by
A
and
B
(e.g., it is
{|+\rangle}
if those operations commute, or
{|-\rangle}
if they anti-commute, as already discussed
Chiribella 2012
), then
A
and
B
can indeed influence the causal order between
C
and
D
.

IV
Correlations with dynamical causal order from quantum circuits

We have already clarified, for each class of quantum circuits introduced in the previous section, the type of correlations they may generate—i.e., in which of the polytopes introduced in Sec.
II
their correlations can be found (see Figs.
3
and
6
). One may now wonder whether the circuits from a given class can reach
all
correlations in the corresponding polytopes (and if not, whether some further restrictions can be evidenced) and whether one can exhibit correlations that are outside of a smaller polytope.
This can be done, in particular, by considering the quantity
I_{4}
from Eq. (
17
)—which we introduced precisely to separate our four polytopes of correlations—and by looking at the maximal value that processes in a given class, with appropriate quantum instruments for the different parties, can reach.

As one may have expected, it can be shown that any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
can be obtained from a quantum (and even a classical) circuit in
QC-convFO
—hence also from circuits in larger classes, in particular in
QC-supFO
—and that any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
can be obtained from a quantum (and even a classical) circuit in
QC-CC
—hence also from circuits in
QC-QC
; see Appendix
E.3
.
Thus, quantum circuits in
QC-convFO
and
QC-supFO
(which both only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
) saturate the convFO bound of
I_{4}\leq_{\text{convFO}}\frac{15}{16}
; while quantum circuits in
QC-CC
and
QC-QC
can reach the trivial upper bound of
I_{4}\leq_{\text{causal}}1
, thereby violating the bounds of Eqs. (
18
)–(
20
) for
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
,
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}}
and
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}
. In Appendix
E.3
we present explicit process matrices, together with choices of instruments, that can be used to reach these different bounds.

The situation is more subtle for the circuits with non-influenceable classical or quantum control of causal order. Recall that we showed that circuits in
QC-NICC
and
QC-NIQC
only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}}
and
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}
, respectively.
It is however less clear
a priori
whether the whole polytopes can be obtained.
Indeed while the correlation with non-influenceable order that reaches the bound
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}
(Eq. (
C.2
) in Appendix
C.2
), for instance, can be obtained by a QC-CC and appropriate instruments, that QC-CC is not a QC-NICC: other choices of instruments give correlations with influenceable order (see again Appendix
E.3
).

To investigate this question further, we tried to optimise numerically the value of
I_{4}
over the classes
QC-NICC
and
QC-NIQC
using a see-saw approach
Werner and Wolf 2001
;
Branciard et al. 2015
—i.e., iteratively optimising (via SDP) over the class of quantum circuits in question and the instruments of the parties, one at a time.
The highest value of
I_{4}
we found was
0.9455
for
QC-NICC
and
0.9534
for
QC-NIQC
.
14
14

14

As none of the corresponding process matrices and quantum instruments thus obtained could be easily interpreted, we do not present them explicitly here.

Both of these values violate the convFO bound
I_{4}\leq_{\text{convFO}}\frac{15}{16}=0.9375
. However, they are far from saturating the NIO and NIO
′
bounds
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}=0.96875
and
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}\frac{47}{48}\simeq 0.9792
; see Fig.
8
.
Although the see-saw approach only gives lower bounds on the possible values attainable for
QC-NICC
and
QC-NIQC
, it thus seems plausible that these classes cannot cover the whole polytopes
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}}
and
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}
. Understanding how exactly these correlations are further restricted remains an open problem.

Figure 8:
Different bounds on the quantity
I_{4}
defined in Eq. (
17
), attainable by circuits in the different classes under considerations. QC-convFOs and QC-supFOs can only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
and saturate the corresponding bound
I_{4}\leq_{\text{convFO}}\frac{15}{16}
from Eq. (
18
). Similarly, general QC-CCs and QC-QCs can only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
and saturate the corresponding (trivial) bound
I_{4}\leq_{\text{causal}}1
from Eq. (
21
). On the other hand, the largest values we found for QC-NICCs and QC-NIQCs—obtained numerically with a see-saw algorithm—do not reach the corresponding bounds for
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}}
and
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}
,
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}
and
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}\frac{47}{48}
from Eqs. (
19
)–(
20
).

A related question is whether QC-NIQCs can give correlations outside of
{\cal P}_{\text{NIO}}
, or if for some reason their correlations are also constrained to be in that polytope. Despite the fact that we did not find any violation of the NIO bound on
I_{4}
with a QC-NIQC, this question remains open.

First, as already noted, the see-saw approach does not necessarily find a global maximum, and it could be in principle that a larger value of
I_{4}
can be obtained with a QC-NIQC.
It could also be that the violation of an NIO bound can only be witnessed by a different quantity than
I_{4}
.
Since we were unable to characterise the polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
in terms of its vertices or facets due to its high-dimensionality (even in the simplest nontrivial scenario), we have no easy method to obtain other inequalities that bound these correlations, and hence study their potential violation.
Finally, numerically we only looked for violations using processes where the quantum systems exchanged between the parties are qubits (i.e., the dimension of input and output Hilbert spaces for each party is 2).
It could be that larger violations can be obtained with higher-dimensional systems, potentially violating the NIO bound using QC-NIQCs.
In this respect, one may also wonder whether the techniques of
Liu and Chiribella 2025
could be adapted to obtain upper bounds on the possible violation of the inequalities we considered. We leave the exploration of these various directions for future work.

Adopting a complementary perspective, one may also wonder whether one could define any other subclass of
QC-NIQC
, larger than
QC-NICC
, for which one can prove that the correlations produced are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
. We formally define such a class in Appendix
E.2.4
, even if its definition is somewhat ad hoc and lacking—for now, at least—a clear physical motivation.

Finally, we considered whether our explicit examples of circuits with dynamical but non-influenceable control of causal order,
W_{\text{NICC}}\in\textup{{QC-NICC}}\setminus\textup{{QC-convFO}}
and
W_{\text{NIQC}}\in\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\setminus(\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cup\text{{{QC-NICC}}})
from Eqs. (
43
) and (
60
), could provide violations of some inequality for non-dynamical causal orders. Although we did not find violations of Eq. (
18
), we were able to find another inequality that bounds
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
and can be violated by
W_{\text{NICC}}
. We did not find such a violation with
W_{\text{NIQC}}
, but we found another similar process in
\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\setminus(\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cup\text{{{QC-NICC}}})
that also provides an analytical violation of the same inequality as for
W_{\text{NICC}}
; see Appendix
E.4
.

V
Outlook

As soon as
N\geq 3
parties establish correlations in a well-defined causal structure, there may
be some dynamical causal order, which cannot be explained in terms of a (probabilistic) fixed causal order between the parties.
Previously, all the examples of causal structures with dynamical causal order have been understood as the influence of past parties on the causal order of future parties. In this work, we have critically reassessed this assertion and shown it to be incomplete.
We identified a new way for the causal order to be dynamical without any influence of the past parties on the causal order of the future parties, and which arises as soon as
N\geq 4
.
We termed this dynamical but non-influenceable causal order, and we showed that the set of causal correlations with non-influenceable order forms a subpolytope
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
of the causal polytope
{\cal P}_{\textup{causal}}
, that reduces to the convex mixture of fixed orders polytope
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}
for
N\leq 3
.
Yet, for
N\geq 4
,
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
is strictly larger than
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, and there exist causal correlations with dynamical but non-influenceable order. We also proposed a relaxed form of non-influenceable causal order, namely causal correlations with non-influenceable coarse-grained orders.

We then built on the formalism of quantum circuits with classical and quantum control of causal order
Wechs et al. 2021
to define various classes of processes inspired by the different classes of causal correlations presented. We are thus able to clearly distinguish between the classes of quantum circuits with non-dynamical, dynamical but non-influenceable and dynamical and influenceable classical control of causal order (
QC-convFO
,
QC-NICC
and
QC-CC
), and analogously in the case of quantum circuits with quantum control of causal order (with the classes
QC-supFO
,
QC-NIQC
and
QC-QC
respectively). We summarise the inclusion relations between these classes as follows (see also Fig.
6
):

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\text{{{QC-convFO}}}&\subset&\text{{{QC-NICC}}}&\subset&\text{{{QC-CC}}}\\[2.84526pt]
\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\subset$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\subset$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\subset$}\\[2.84526pt]
\text{{{QC-supFO}}}&\subset&\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}&\subset&\text{{{QC-QC}}}\end{array}\ .

In general, for
N\geq 4
, the inclusions are all strict, while for
N\leq 3
,
QC-NICC
coincides with
QC-convFO
and
QC-NIQC
coincides with
QC-supFO
.
From the inclusion relations defined in Eq. (
V
), one may wonder whether there remain nontrivial and interesting intersections between the classes, as denoted by the question marks in Fig.
6
, or whether it is the case that (some of) these intersections are empty, so that
\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cap\text{{{QC-NICC}}}=\text{{{QC-convFO}}}
,
\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cap\text{{{QC-CC}}}=\text{{{QC-convFO}}}
, and/or that
\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\cap\text{{{QC-CC}}}=\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
? We leave these as open questions.

All the classes of quantum circuits considered in this work were characterised in terms of SDP constraints on positive semidefinite matrices. We propose in Appendix
D.4
a graphical representation of these constraints which highlights the structural differences between the considered classes. Considering new graphical structures could inspire the definition of new classes of circuits. It would also be interesting to explore the connection between this graphical representation with other graphical understandings of the causal structure that underlies process matrices. One can for instance find similar structures in the graph that represents the SDP constraints for the
QC-QC
class (see Fig.
13
in Appendix
D.4
) as in the so-called “branch graph”
Vanrietvelde et al. 2022
that one can build from a possible generic routed quantum circuit representation of QC-QCs
Grothus et al. 2025
. We leave all this for future work.

We exhibited in particular two examples of quantum circuits with dynamical but non-influenceable classical or quantum control of causal order, characterised by their process matrices
W_{\textup{NICC}}
and
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
(Eqs. (
43
) and (
60
)).
Admittedly, for these two examples the dynamical behaviour has a strong classical flavour: a measurement is performed after the action of
A
and
B
, whose classical outcome controls the order between
C
and
D
. One may wonder whether there exist other examples of quantum circuits with non-influenceable causal order, where the dynamical behaviour has a “more quantum” flavour. How this question could be formalised, and whether a scenario with more parties involved would be required—so that for instance a quantum system that coherently controls the causal order between
C
and
D
is sent to a future party—is left for further work.

A corollary of the existence of dynamical but non-influenceable causal structures is that the two notions of dynamicality and influenceability are in general (for
N\geq 4
) inequivalent, both at the level of causal correlations and of quantum circuits with classical or quantum control of causal order. An interesting follow-up question would be to clarify under which conditions dynamicality and influenceability are found to be equivalent notions. For example, one may wonder whether dynamicality without influenceability may still be found in “pure” QC-QCs
Araújo et al. 2017
, that is, QC-QCs that transform unitary maps into a unitary map from a global past Hilbert space to a global future Hilbert space. Indeed, we note that the property of dynamicality without influenceability of the processes
W_{\textup{NICC}}
and
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
presented above crucially relies on the maximally entangled pair of qubits initially prepared. If one purifies these processes (in the sense of
Araújo et al. 2017
), the system initially prepared will then depend on the action of the global past, in turn allowing the parties’ actions to—in general—influence the causal order.
Along this line, an alternative path worth exploring further is to study whether the possibility of causal structures with dynamical but non-influenceable causal order can be understood as some sort of fine-tuning that prevents the parties from influencing the causal order at the statistical level, even if such an influence is in principle possible at the level of the underlying causal structure.

More generally, this work also opens several follow-up questions to better understand causal structures between
N
parties. One first idea could be to refine the analysis performed in this work in order to define “partially dynamical” causal structures, so that one has a clear understanding of which parties contribute to the dynamical behaviour of a process and which do not, allowing one to quantify some notion of “depth” of dynamicality.
Another interesting perspective is to look whether any fundamentally new feature could occur if one studies scenarios with
N\geq 5
parties, in the same way that the tripartite scenario gives rise to dynamical properties, and the fourpartite scenario gives rise to dynamical but non-influenceable causal order.

An important question that remains open is whether one could formalise a notion of dynamical causal order beyond the class of QC-QCs, as there are processes—such as the Lugano (or “AF/BW”) process
Baumeler et al. 2014
;
Baumeler and Wolf 2016
—which cannot be expressed as a QC-QC and whose dynamicality remains ambiguous. This would be rather challenging as the formalisation of the notion of dynamical causal order presented in this present work crucially relies on the notion of a quantum control system that dictates the causal order between the parties. Yet, no clear physical interpretation of process matrices that go beyond
QC-QC
has been proposed so far, which makes it unclear what the notion of dynamical causal order could mean in general. Nevertheless, a potential track to tackle this problem could be to consider the framework of routed quantum circuits
Vanrietvelde et al. 2021
;
Vanrietvelde et al. 2022
, that can model process matrices beyond QC-QCs, and in which a graphical criterion to detect the dynamical causal order in the “Grenoble process” was hinted at, in terms of “bifurcation choices" of the parties
Vanrietvelde et al. 2022
. One could first try to see whether this graphical criterion captures the same notion of dynamical causal order for QC-QCs as ours (or perhaps of influenceable order) and then generalise it to processes beyond QC-QCs.

From a more practical point of view, the study of indefinite causal order has aroused some particular interest for the potential information processing advantages that they offer (see
Rozema et al. 2024
for an extensive review of these).
Whether dynamical causal order, both classically or coherently controlled, can provide such kind of advantages thus arises as a natural question.
A positive answer to this question for classically controlled dynamical causal order was recently obtained in Ref.
Bavaresco et al. 2024
. The authors showcased an advantage of QC-CCs over QC-convFOs in a tripartite task, where the goal is to simulate the quantum switch with two queries to
A
’s operation and one to
B
’s operation.
However, as the notion of coherent control of non-dynamical causal order was not yet formalised, an analogous advantage in the case of coherently controlled dynamical causal order, that is, an advantage of QC-QCs over QC-supFOs, has not yet been exhibited.
In a recent work
Mothe et al. 2024
, we presented an advantage of QC-QCs over a particular class of quantum circuits with non-dynamical control of causal order (the
Sup
class from Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
, which turns out to be strictly included in
QC-supFO
) for a tripartite task in quantum metrology. However, the present work, and in particular the formalisation of
QC-supFO
, helps to prove that the origin of the advantage observed in
Mothe et al. 2024
cannot be attributed to dynamical causal order, since the optimal QC-QC strategy in fact belongs to
QC-supFO
and thus does not have dynamical causal order.
Further work thus remains to be done to show whether dynamical causal order combined with indefinite causal order is indeed a useful resource in quantum information.
In this context, we note that the characterisations of the various classes of processes proposed in this work, in terms of linear constraints on positive semidefinite matrices, provide a readily usable way to compare the performances of quantum circuits with dynamical control of causal order with those of quantum circuits with non-dynamical causal order, for any task that could be solved or optimised through semidefinite programming techniques.

acknowledges the support from the Alexander von Humboldt Foundation. For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright license to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.

Appendices

Appendix A
Multipartite causal correlations: equivalence between the different definitions

A.1
Abbott
et al.
’s definition

In Ref.
Abbott et al. 2016
Abbott
et al.
proposed the following recursive definition for causal correlations:
an
N
-partite probability distribution
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is causal if and only if
N=1
, or it has a decomposition as

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{k_{1}}q_{k_{1}}\,p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})\,p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}),

(65)

with
q_{k_{1}}\geq 0
,
\sum_{k_{1}}q_{k_{1}}=1
, where for each
k_{1}\in\mathcal{N}
,
p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})
is a probability distribution for the single party
A_{k_{1}}
, and where for each
k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}
,
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})
is a conditional probability distribution for the remaining
N-1
parties in
\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}
, which is itself causal (according to the present definition).

Our definition in Eq. (
3
) can be seen as an “unravelling” of this definition. Let us indeed prove by induction that the two are equivalent.
This is clearly the case for
N=1
(they are both trivial); let us then assume they are equivalent for
N-1\ (\geq 1)
:

(69)

the decomposition of Eq. (
3
) can then be written in exactly the same form as in Eq. (
65
).

Clearly
q_{k_{1}}\geq 0
,
\sum_{k_{1}}q_{k_{1}}=1
, and
p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})
is a valid (nonnegative and normalised) probability distribution. It remains to verify that
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})
is a causal
(N-1)
-partite probability distribution (according to Abbott
et al.
’s definition).

To see this, notice that the last line in Eq. (
69
) above gives precisely a decomposition of
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})
of the form of Eq. (
3
) for
(N-1)
-partite distributions, with a sum over all permutations
\pi^{\prime}=(k_{2},\ldots,k_{n})
of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}
. Consider some
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
and some subsequence
(k_{2},\ldots,k_{n+1})
of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. One then has

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}\big((k_{2},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{2},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}\big)=p\big((k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\big/p\big((k_{1}),a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big).

(70)

According to Eq. (
3
), which we assumed for

, the numerator in the right-hand-side above does not depend on

(and neither does the denominator). Hence, the left-hand-side does not depend on

satisfies the constraint of Eq. (
3
) for
(N-1)
-partite distributions. It is thus causal according to that definition, and therefore, by our induction hypothesis, it is causal according to Abbott
et al.
’s definition, as required.

A.2
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi’s definition

The first general definition for multipartite causal correlations that appeared in the literature was in fact proposed earlier by Oreshkov and Giarmatzi
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi 2016
. It says that causal correlations are those compatible with a causal structure described as a Strict Partial Order (SPO), rather than a full permutation as in our definition of Eq. (
3
).

More specifically: an SPO between
N
parties
A_{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{N}\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,N\}
) is a binary relation
\prec
which is irreflexive, transitive and antisymmetric. An SPO can in general be defined by the (consistent
16
16

16

Some consistency constraints are implied by the irreflexivity, transitivity and antisymmetry of the SPO. E.g., the pairwise relations
A_{1}\prec A_{2}
,
A_{2}\prec A_{3}
,
A_{3}\prec A_{1}
would be inconsistent.
) list of all pairwise relations for all
i\neq j
:
17
17

17

When writing such pairwise relations between
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
, we will always assume
i\neq j
, without necessarily specifying it explicitly.

either
A_{i}\prec A_{j}
(understood as
A_{i}
being in the causal past of
A_{j}
),
A_{j}\prec A_{i}
(
A_{j}
being in the causal past of
A_{i}
), or
A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{j}
(neither party being in the causal past of the other one). We shall generically denote by
\kappa=\kappa(\mathcal{N})
an SPO for the parties in
\mathcal{N}
. Note that any such SPO
\kappa(\mathcal{N})
induces an SPO
\kappa(\mathcal{K})
on any subset
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{N}
.

Rephrasing it slightly, Oreshkov and Giarmatzi’s definition (Definition 2.3 in
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi 2016
) states that a correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is causal if and only if

\displaystyle\exists\,p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\text{and}\ \forall\,\mathcal{K}\subsetneq\mathcal{N},\ \forall\,\ell\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K},\ \forall\,\bar{\kappa}=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\text{ for which }A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K},

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptp\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\text{ does not depend on }x_{\ell},

(71)

where
p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is a probability distribution (so that
p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})\geq 0
,
\sum_{\kappa,\vec{a}}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=1
) in which the random variable
\kappa=\kappa(\mathcal{N})
takes values in all possible SPOs on
\mathcal{N}
, where the shorthand notation
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}
means
A_{\ell}\nprec A_{k}
(i.e.,
A_{k}\prec A_{\ell}
or
A_{k}\nprec\nsucc A_{\ell}
) for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
, and where the last line involves the marginal distributions
p\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
obtained from
p(\kappa,\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}|\vec{x})
(
=\sum_{\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}}}p(\kappa,\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}},\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}}|\vec{x})
) by summing over all SPOs
\kappa=\kappa(\mathcal{N})
which induce the SPO
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
on the subset
\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}
: that is,
p\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\sum_{\kappa(\mathcal{N})\text{ s.t. }\kappa(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})}p\big(\kappa(\mathcal{N}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
.

That this definition is equivalent to that of Abbott
et al.
, Eq. (
65
) above, was in fact already proven by Oreshkov and Giarmatzi in
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi 2016
.
Let us for convenience propose here a new version of this proof.

•

Proof that (
65
)
\Rightarrow
(
71
) [or (
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
71
)]:
Assume Eq. (
65
). We have just shown in the previous subsection that this is equivalent to assuming that
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
has a decomposition as in Eq. (
3
).
Now, clearly a total order defined by some permutation
\pi
is a particular case of an SPO
\kappa
, defined by the pairwise relations
A_{i}\prec A_{j}
iff
i
comes before
j
in
\pi
(for all
i\neq j
). Hence, the decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
, with
p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=0
as soon as
\kappa
contains a pairwise relation of the form
A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{j}
. It then remains to prove that the second and third lines of Eq. (
71
) are indeed satisfied.

Consider
\mathcal{K}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}
,
\ell\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}
, and some SPO
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
for which
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}
. If
\bar{\kappa}
contains any relation of the form
A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{j}
, then so does any SPO
\kappa(\mathcal{N})
that induces
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
on the subset
\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}
, and hence
p\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=0
, so that the last line of Eq. (
71
) is trivially satisfied. Otherwise, if
\bar{\kappa}
contains no relation of the form
A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{j}
, then it can be equivalently described as a permutation
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)
of
\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}
(where
n
is the cardinality of
\mathcal{K}
, and noting that
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}
implies that
\ell
must necessarily come last). The SPOs
\kappa=\kappa(\mathcal{N})
that contribute to the decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
and that induce
\bar{\kappa}\equiv(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)
on the subset
\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}
are then the permutations
\pi
of
\mathcal{N}
that contain
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)
as a subsequence—which we write
\pi\supset(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)
—so that

\displaystyle p\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{\pi\supset(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)}p(\pi,\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\qquad=\hskip-8.53581pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},\ell)\\
\hskip 8.19447pt\supset(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69054ptp\big((i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},\ell),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\ =\hskip-5.69054pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},\ell)\\
\hskip 8.19447pt\supset(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{i}:\\
\forall\,i\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}\end{subarray}}p\big((i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},\ell),\vec{a}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}\big|\vec{x}\big),

(72)

where the second sum above is over all supersequences
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},\ell)
of
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},\ell)
(with
n\leq m<N
), and the last sum is over the outcomes
a_{i}
of all parties with indices
i\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}
that were not already in
\mathcal{K}
.
The assumption of Eq. (
3
) implies that each probability in the last sums above do not depend on
x_{\ell}
, which implies that
p\big(\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
itself does not depend on
x_{\ell}
—which indeed means that the last line of Eq. (
71
) is satisfied.

•

Proof that (
71
)
\Rightarrow
(
65
):

We prove this by induction on
N
. This clearly holds for
N=1
(both conditions are trivial); let us then assume that Eq. (
71
) implies Eq. (
65
) for
(N-1)
-partite distributions (with
N-1\geq 1
), and consider an
N
-partite distribution
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
that admits a decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfying Eq. (
71
).

The first task, for each SPO
\kappa
that appears in the decomposition, is to identify which party
A_{k_{1}}
can be “taken to come first”, so as to provide a decomposition as in Eq. (
65
). For this let us denote, for any given
\kappa=\kappa(\mathcal{N})
, by
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}
the (nonempty) set of parties that have no other party in their causal past—the “first consecutive set” according to the terminology of Ref.
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi 2016
.
We will first show that the independence condition of Eq. (
71
) also holds when one further restricts the probabilities to SPOs with a given first consecutive set
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.:
18
18

18

Here we adopt the notation that, for a given property
[\cdot]
that a SPO may or may not satisfy, we write
p([\cdot],\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa\text{ s.t.}\,[\cdot]}\,p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
. E.g. in Eq. (
73
),
p(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa\text{ s.t.}\,\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\kappa(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})}\,p(\kappa,\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}|\vec{x})
.

In the proof that follows, the notation
\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{j}
means that
\exists\,i\in\mathcal{S}
,
A_{i}\prec A_{j}
; the notation
\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}
means that
\forall\,i\in\mathcal{S}
,
A_{i}\nprec A_{j}
.

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{N},\ \forall\,\mathcal{K}\subsetneq\mathcal{N},\ \forall\,\ell\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K},\ \forall\,\bar{\kappa}=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\text{ for which }A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K},

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptp\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\text{ does not depend on }x_{\ell}.

(73)

Proof of Eq. (
73
):

If
\ell\in\mathcal{S}
, then using the inclusion–exclusion principle one can write:
19
19

19

More specifically, we use the fact that for some events
E
and
\{F_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{I}}
(for some finite set of indices
\mathcal{I}
), one has

\displaystyle p\Big(E,\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{I}}F_{j}\Big)=p\big(E\big)-p\Big(E,\bigvee_{j\in\mathcal{I}}(\neg F_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle=p\big(E\big)-p\Big(\bigvee_{j\in\mathcal{I}}(E\wedge\neg F_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle=p\big(E\big)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|-1}\,p\Big(\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(E\wedge\neg F_{j})\Big)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|}\,p\Big(E,\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(\neg F_{j})\Big),

where the first equality on the second line is a direct application of the inclusion–exclusion principle.

\displaystyle p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=p\Bigg(\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\!\!\big(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}\big),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}}\!\!\!\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{j}\Big),\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigg|\vec{x}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|}\ p\Bigg(\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\!\!\big(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}\big),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\Big),\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigg|\vec{x}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K})\text{ s.t. }\\[-2.84526pt]
\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\big),\\
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\end{subarray}}p\big(\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big).

(74)

Now, recalling that
\ell\in\mathcal{S}
here, one can see that
\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}})
and
\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\big)
imply that
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}(\mathcal{S}\backslash\ell)
and
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{J}
, respectively. The constraint that
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
implies that the condition
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}
, which was imposed on
\bar{\kappa}
, also holds for
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}
. Hence all the
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}
’s considered in the sum above satisfy
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}\backslash\ell)
.
From Eq. (
71
) (with
\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}\backslash\ell
, after marginalising over some of the outputs
a_{k}
) it then follows that the terms
p\big(\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
that appear in the sum, and therefore
p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
itself, do not depend on
x_{\ell}
.

Otherwise if
\ell\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}
, then using again the inclusion–exclusion principle one can write:

\displaystyle p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=p\Bigg(\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\!\!\big(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}\big),\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{\ell}\Big),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}\backslash\ell}\!\!\!\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{j}\Big),\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigg|\vec{x}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}\backslash\ell}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|}\ p\Bigg(\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\!\!\big(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}\big),\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{\ell}\Big),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\Big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\Big),\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigg|\vec{x}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{S}\backslash\ell}(-1)^{|\mathcal{J}|}\hskip-19.91692pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\text{ s.t. }\\
\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}}),\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{\ell}\big),\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\big),\\
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\end{subarray}}\hskip-19.91692ptp\big(\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big).

(75)

Now, one can see that
\bigwedge_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(A_{i}\nprec\nsucc A_{i^{\prime}})
and
\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{\ell}\big)
together imply that
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{S}
, and that
\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\prec}}A_{\ell}\big)
and
\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big(\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}A_{j}\big)
together imply that
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{J}
.
The constraint that
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
again implies that the condition
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}
also holds for
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}
. Hence all the
\bar{\bar{\kappa}}
’s considered in the sum above satisfy
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K})
.
From Eq. (
71
) (with now
\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}
, after marginalising over some of the outputs
a_{k}
) it then follows that the terms
p\big(\bar{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
that appear in the sum, and therefore
p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
itself, do not depend on
x_{\ell}
—which concludes the proof of Eq. (
73
).

Now that we have this in place, let us introduce some arbitrary choice function
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}
which, to each nonempty subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
, assigns one of its elements,
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S})\in\mathcal{S}
(which will be “taken to come first”); and for any SPO
\kappa
, let us define
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}\coloneqq\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}})
(where
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}
is the “first consecutive set” of
\kappa
, as introduced above).

Notice that for
\mathcal{K}=\emptyset
, Eq. (
73
) implies that
\forall\,\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
,
p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}\big|\vec{x}\big)
does not depend on any
x_{\ell}
, i.e., it does not depend on
\vec{x}
at all; and that for
\mathcal{K}=\{k_{1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
(so that
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}
implies
A_{\ell}\nprec A_{k_{1}}
for any
\ell\neq k_{1}
), Eq. (
73
) implies that
\forall\,\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
,
\forall\,k_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
,
p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
does not depend on any
x_{\ell}
with
\ell\neq k_{1}
, i.e., it only depends on
x_{k_{1}}
.
It then follows that for any given
k_{1}\in\mathcal{N}
,

\displaystyle p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{S}\text{ s.t. }\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S})=k_{1}}p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ does not depend on }\vec{x}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{S}\text{ s.t. }\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S})=k_{1}}p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ only depends on }x_{k_{1}}.

(76)

One can then define
20
20

20

Again we assume for simplicity, in these definitions, that the denominators are nonzero. The terms for which these are zero do not contribute to the argument (see Footnote
15
).

\displaystyle q_{k_{1}}\coloneqq p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1}\big),\quad p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})\coloneqq\frac{p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)}{q_{k_{1}}},

\displaystyle\text{for any SPO }\kappa^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}),\quad

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\kappa^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})\coloneqq\frac{p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},\kappa^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)}{p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)},

and

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})\coloneqq\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1})}p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\kappa^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}),

(77)

so that the decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
can be written as

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}}p(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{k_{1}}q_{k_{1}}\,p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})\,p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}),

(78)

i.e., in exactly the same form as in Eq. (
65
).
Clearly
q_{k_{1}}\geq 0
,
\sum_{k_{1}}q_{k_{1}}=1
, and
p_{k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})
is a valid probability distribution. It remains to verify that each
(N-1)
-partite probability distribution
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})
is causal (according to Abbott
et al.
’s definition).

To see this, consider
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}
,
\ell\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, and
\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}=\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\})
for which
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. Defining
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\{k_{1}\}\cup\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, one has

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}\big(\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}\big)\ =\ \frac{p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)}{p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\text{ s.t. }\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S})=k_{1},\\
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})\text{ s.t. }\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\})\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636ptp\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\Bigg/p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big).

(79)

Now,
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\})=\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\})
implies that
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
is also such that
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}
, with
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S})=k_{1}
, then
k_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}
and therefore
A_{\ell}\nprec A_{k_{1}}
for all
\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\})
that contribute to the sum.
All in all, these thus satisfy
A_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\forall}}{{\nprec}}\{k_{1}\}\cup\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}
.
From Eq. (
73
) it follows that the terms
p\big(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S},\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
in the sum above do not depend on
x_{\ell}
; given that the same holds for the denominator
p\big(\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa}=k_{1},a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)
, we obtain that the term
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}\big(\bar{\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\cup\{\ell\}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}\big)
itself does not depend on
x_{\ell}
, i.e.,
p_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1},x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}}}(\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{1}})
satisfies Eq. (
71
). By our induction hypothesis, it also satisfies Eq. (
65
), i.e., it is causal according to Abbott
et al.
’s definition—which concludes the proof that Oreshkov and Giarmatzi’s definition is equivalent to that of Abbott
et al.

By unravelling our inductive proof that (
71
)
\Rightarrow
(
65
) above, one can see that each SPO
\kappa
gets extended to a total order (a permutation)
\pi_{\kappa}
of
\mathcal{N}
that is “compatible” with
\kappa
, i.e., that preserves all pairwise relations of the form
A_{i}\prec A_{j}
.
Specifically,
\pi_{\kappa}=(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})
is obtained inductively as follows, using the choice function
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}
:
k_{1}=\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa(\mathcal{N})}^{\textsc{I}})
,
k_{2}=\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa(\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1}\})}^{\textsc{I}})
,
k_{3}=\underline{k}_{\textsc{I}}(\mathcal{S}_{\kappa(\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},k2\})}^{\textsc{I}})
, etc. It can be seen that the decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
thus provides a new decomposition

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}\sum_{\kappa\text{ s.t. }\pi_{\kappa}=\pi}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x}),

(80)

with
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})\coloneqq\sum_{\kappa\text{ s.t. }\pi_{\kappa}=\pi}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfying Eq. (
3
) (as it follows from the proof that (
65
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) in the previous subsection).

Note that compatible extensions of a given SPO to a total order are in general not unique.
For the proof above to work, one could however not just take any arbitrary extension, independently for each SPO
\kappa
. Indeed it was important that the choice of the “first party” (when
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}
was not a singleton) only depended on the set
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}
, rather than on
\kappa
directly—i.e., for two SPOs
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
with the same first consecutive set
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\textsc{I}}
, the (otherwise arbitrary) choice of the first party
\underline{k}_{\textsc{I},\kappa^{(\prime)}}
must be the same. (That choice could also be probabilistic, but with probabilities that do not depend on the settings
\vec{x}
.) Otherwise one can construct counterexamples, for which extending each SPO independently to a total order leads to a decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
as in Eq. (
80
) that does not satisfy Eq. (
3
).
21
21

21

For an explicit such counterexample: consider for instance 3 parties
A,B,C
with the two SPOs
\kappa_{0}=(A\nprec\nsucc B,A\prec C,B\nprec\nsucc C)
and
\kappa_{1}=(A\nprec\nsucc B,A\prec C,B\prec C)
, which both have the same first consecutive set
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_{0}}^{\textsc{I}}=\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_{1}}^{\textsc{I}}=\{A,B\}
, and the distribution (with no output for any party and only an input
x=0,1
for
A
)
p(\kappa|x)=\delta_{\kappa,\kappa_{x}}
, which satisfies Eq. (
71
).
If one were to extend
\kappa_{0}
to
\pi_{\kappa_{0}}=(A,B,C)
and
\kappa_{1}
to
\pi_{\kappa_{1}}=(B,A,C)
, i.e. with different choices for the first party, then this would result in
p(\pi|x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{\pi,(A,B,C)}&\text{if}\ x=0\\
\delta_{\pi,(B,A,C)}&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.
, which does not satisfy Eq. (
3
) (as for instance
p((k_{1})=(A)|x)=\delta_{x,0}
then depends on
x
).

A.3
Two more equivalent characterisations of causal correlations

Here we provide two additional equivalent characterisations of multipartite causal correlations. The first one is that a correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is causal if and only if it can be decomposed in terms of (well-defined, normalised) probability distributions
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)
as

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with}\quad p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big),

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\text{s.t. }\forall\,n,\ \sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\text{ does not depend on }x_{k_{n}}.

(81)

The last characterisation we provide is that a correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is causal if and only if it can be decomposed in terms of (well-defined, normalised) probability distributions
\hat{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)
—which reduce to just
\hat{p}\big((k_{1})\big)
for
n=0
and to
\hat{p}\big(a_{k_{N}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{x},\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}\big)
for
n=N
—as

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with}\quad p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\prod_{n=0}^{N}\hat{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big).

(82)

Each term
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)
in Eq. (
81
) corresponds to the probability for the “next” (
n^{\text{th}}
) party to be
A_{k_{n}}
and to output
a_{k_{n}}
, given that the previous parties were (in that order) those labelled
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})
, given all previous parties’ as well as
A_{k_{n}}
’s inputs
\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}
, and given all previous parties’ outputs
\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}
. Somewhat analogously, each term
\hat{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)
in Eq. (
82
) corresponds to the probability that party
A_{k_{n}}
outputs
a_{k_{n}}
and that the next (
(n+1)^{\text{th}}
) party will be
A_{k_{n+1}}
, given that the order of the parties so far was
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, given all of those parties’ inputs
\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}
, and given all previous parties’ outputs
\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}
.
Both decompositions thus translate—in two slightly different manners—the idea that the causal order can be established step by step, with each of the parties’ outputs and the choice of the “next” party depending on all previous available information (the order of the previous parties, all inputs so far, and all previous outputs).

As we will see, the characterisation of Eq. (
81
) will be useful in particular in our proofs that QC-QCs only generate causal correlations, and reciprocally that any causal correlation can be obtained from a QC-QC; see Appendices
E.2.1
and
E.3.2
.

•

Proof that (
81
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
):
For a distribution
p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
that factorises as in Eq. (
81
), one easily obtains, recursively (from
n=N-1
down to
n=0
), that

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{p}\big((k_{j}),a_{k_{j}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}}\big)\Big)\,\tilde{p}\big((k_{n+1})\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big).

(83)

Clearly the first product within brackets does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
; the last term
22
22

22

In order to lighten the notations we sometimes write
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n+1}}\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)
for
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n+1}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big)
.

\tilde{p}\big((k_{n+1})\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)\coloneqq\sum_{a_{k_{n+1}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n+1}}\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)
also clearly does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1}\}}
, and is further required (from Eq. (
81
)) not to depend on
x_{k_{n+1}}
. All in all,
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
, as required in Eq. (
3
).

•

Proof that (
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
81
):
From a decomposition as in Eq. (
3
), one can define
\tilde{p}\big((k_{1}),a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)\coloneqq p\big((k_{1}),a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
(which indeed only depends on
x_{k_{1}}
) and (for
n=2,\ldots,N
)
23
23

23

We once again assume for simplicity that the denominators are nonzero; the terms for which these are zero do not contribute to the decomposition of
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
.

\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\coloneqq p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)/p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
(which indeed only depends on the inputs
\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}
). It is easy to see that these are normalised probability distributions whose product gives
p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
as in the second line of Eq. (
81
), and such that (for each
n
)
\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)
does not depend on
x_{k_{n}}
.

The proofs that (
82
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) and (
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
82
) are quite similar: from Eq. (
82
) one easily obtains recursively that
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\prod_{j=0}^{n}\hat{p}\big((k_{j+1}),a_{k_{j}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{j}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}}\big)
, which clearly does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
; from Eq. (
3
), one can define the normalised probability distributions
\hat{p}\big((k_{1})\big)\coloneqq p\big((k_{1})\big|\vec{x}\big)
(which indeed does not depend on
\vec{x}
),
\hat{p}\big((k_{n+1}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\coloneqq p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)/p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
(which indeed only depends on the inputs
\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}
), and
\hat{p}\big(a_{k_{N}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{x},\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}\big)\coloneqq p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)/p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
, whose product reproduces Eq. (
82
).

A.4
Equivalent definitions for correlations with non-influenceable causal orders

Recall that we defined correlations with non-influenceable causal order (Eq. (
15
)) by referring to our definition of causal correlations based on decompositions of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
.

One could however also refer to Oreshkov and Giarmatzi’s definition, namely by defining correlations with NIO as those with a decomposition of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
, as in (
71
), which further satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\,\kappa,\ p(\kappa|\vec{x})\ \text{does not depend on }\vec{x}.

(84)

It is however easy to see, following the previous subsection, that the two definitions are equivalent. Indeed a total order
\pi
is a particular case of an SPO
\kappa
, so that if a decomposition of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
(satisfying Eq. (
3
)) with
p(\pi|\vec{x})
being independent of
\vec{x}
exists, then this readily provides a decomposition of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
(satisfying Eq. (
71
), as we have seen before) with
p(\kappa|\vec{x})
that does not depend on
\vec{x}
.
Conversely, if a decomposition of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
(satisfying Eq. (
71
)) exists, then we have just seen above how to extend each SPO
\kappa
to a total order
\pi
, and obtain a decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
as in Eq. (
80
), with
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\kappa\text{ s.t. }\pi_{\kappa}=\pi}p(\kappa,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfying Eq. (
3
); if
p(\kappa|\vec{x})
does not depend on
\vec{x}
, then neither does the distribution
p(\pi|\vec{x})
thus obtained.

Appendix B
Tripartite causal correlations

B.1
The “simplest” tripartite scenario with dynamical causal order

Consider a tripartite scenario in which the three parties
A,B,C
have binary inputs
x,y,z=0,1
, party
A
has no output, while parties
B
and
C
have a binary output
b,c=0,1
only when their input is 1 (
B
and
C
are in the so-called “lazy scenario”). This scenario is simple enough that the polytopes of causal correlations (
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
), of correlations with static causal order (
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
), as well as of all valid probability distributions (
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{all}}
) can be fully characterised:

•

The causal polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
has 144 vertices: 112 of them are compatible with a fixed causal order—of the form
p(b,c|x,y,z)=\delta_{b,y\beta_{x}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{x,y}}
or
p(b,c|x,y,z)=\delta_{b,y\beta_{x,z}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{x}}
, or both, for some
\beta_{x(,z)},\gamma_{x(,y)}=0,1
—and 32 of them feature dynamical causal order—of the form
p(b,c|x,y,z)=\delta_{x,0}\delta_{b,y\beta_{0}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{0,y}}+\delta_{x,1}\delta_{b,y\beta_{1,z}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{1}}
or
p(b,c|x,y,z)=\delta_{x,1}\delta_{b,y\beta_{1}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{1,y}}+\delta_{x,0}\delta_{b,y\beta_{0,z}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{0}}
, with
\beta_{x,z}
and
\gamma_{x,y}
having a nontrivial dependence on
z
and
y
, resp.

Solving the facet enumeration problem, we find that
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
has 8 facets of the “Lazy Guess Your Neighbour’s Input” (LGYNI)
Branciard 2016
form
24
24

24

Here we only write explicitly a single representative for each type of facet. The other facets “of the same form” are obtained by exchanging the parties
B
and
C
, by flipping
A
’s input
x
, and/or by flipping
B
and/or
C
’s outputs when their input is 1.

\displaystyle p(b=c=yz|x)\underset{\text{causal}}{\leq}\frac{3}{4}

(85)

(for some fixed value
x
, with
p(b=c=yz|x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{y,z}p(b=c=yz|x,y,z)
), in addition to 8 trivial facets of the form
p(b|x,y=1,z)\geq 0
and 8 trivial facets of the form
p(b,c|x,y=z=1)\geq 0
.

•

The polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
only has the 112 vertices compatible with a fixed order given above.
We find that it has the same facets as
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
, plus 8 facets of the form
