\displaystyle I_{3}\coloneqq p\big(x=0,c=yz\big)+p\big(x=1,b=yz\big)\underset{\text{convFO}}{\leq}\frac{7}{8},

(86)

as in Eqs. (
6
)–(
7
) of the main text.

Let us present here a direct proof of the latter inequality.
Consider a fixed order in which
B
comes before
C
, so that
p(b|x,y,z)
does not depend on
z
: we shall write
p(b|x,y,z)=p(b|x,y,\cancel{z})
. We then have

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\underbrace{p\big(x=0,c=yz\big)}_{\leq p(x=0)=\frac{1}{2}}+\underbrace{p\big(x=1,b=yz\big)}_{=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{yz}p(b=yz|x=1,y,z)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}\sum_{z=0,1}\big[\underbrace{p\big(b=0|x=1,y=0,\cancel{z}\big)}_{\leq 1}+p\big(b=z|x=1,y=1,\cancel{z}\big)\big]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}=\frac{7}{8}.

(87)

The same bound holds, by symmetry, for any fixed order in which
C
comes before
B
, and by linearity, for any convex combination of fixed orders:
I_{3}\underset{\text{convFO}}{\leq}\frac{7}{8}
.

•

The polytope
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{all}}
of all valid probability distributions is only delimited by the 16 trivial facets of the form
p(b|x,y=1,z)\geq 0
and
p(b,c|x,y=z=1)\geq 0
.
Solving the vertex enumeration problem, we find that it has the same 144 vertices as
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
, plus 112 non-causal vertices that feature 2-way signalling, of the form
p(b,c|x,y,z)=\delta_{b,y\beta_{x,z}}\delta_{c,z\gamma_{x,y}}
, with both
\beta_{0,z}
and
\gamma_{0,y}
, and/or both
\beta_{1,z}
and
\gamma_{1,y}
, having a nontrivial dependence on
z
and
y
, resp.

Notice that the scenario considered here is the “smallest” one—in the sense of minimising the number of parties and the number of inputs and outputs for each party
individually
25
25

25

Note however that one could also consider even “smaller” scenarios where certain
combinations
of inputs, for the different parties, are further restricted. In fact, the expression
I_{3}
does not involve (in any nontrivial way) terms with
(x,z)=(0,0)
, nor with
(x,y)=(1,0)
. Hence one can already distinguish dynamical orders in scenarios where these combinations of inputs never appear.
—that can feature dynamical causal order. With two parties causal correlations indeed reduce to convex mixtures of the two possible orders as in Eq. (
1
); if one of the three parties had no input then it could always be considered to come last,
26
26

26

More specifically: if one party, say
A_{N}
, has no input, then starting from a probability distribution
p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
one can define
p^{\prime}\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1},N),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},N,k_{i},\ldots,k_{N-1}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
, such that party
A_{N}
always comes last in
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1},N)
.

Similarly, if one party, say
A_{1}
, has no output, then starting from
p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
one can define
p^{\prime}\big(\pi=(1,k_{2},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}p\big(\pi=(k_{2},\ldots,k_{i},1,k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
, such that party
A_{1}
always comes first in
\pi=(1,k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})
.

One can verify that in both cases, if the original distribution
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfies the causal constraints of Eq. (
3
), then so does
p^{\prime}(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
; if
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfies the NIO constraints of Eq. (
15
), then so does
p^{\prime}(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
; and if
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfies the NIO
′
constraints of Eq. (
16
), then so does
p^{\prime}(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
.

and causal correlations would reduce to convex mixtures of the two possible orders for the other two parties; and if a second party had no output then all probability distributions (for just the single remaining output) could be obtained causally, with the two parties with no output coming first.
It is in fact rather remarkable that the dynamical property appears already in a tripartite scenario where one of the parties has no output.

B.2
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
reduce to
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
for
N=3

While, as just seen, a tripartite scenario can exhibit dynamical causal order, we show here that it cannot exhibit “dynamical but non-influenceable causal order”—that is, correlations with non-influenceable causal order (in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
) reduce to convex mixtures of correlations with fixed orders (i.e., they are in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
).

To see this, notice that for
N=3
, the causality conditions of Eq. (
3
) imply in particular that for all
\pi=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
,
27
27

27

Notice that the following argument does not work in general for
N\geq 4
—unless one party has no input or one party has no output, using the observation made in the previous footnote—because
p\big((k_{1},k_{2}),a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
cannot be readily identified with
p(\pi,a_{k_{1}}|\vec{x})
as we do in Eq. (
88
), and the terms
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x},\pi)
in Eq. (
89
) are in general not compatible with the causal order
\pi
.

As an explicit counter-example, one can take for instance the NIO correlation in Eq. (
C.2
) of the next appendix, with its causal decomposition as proposed just below Eq. (
C.2
). For these one gets
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t,\pi{=}(A,B,C,D))=\delta_{a=b=xy}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}
and

p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t,\pi{=}(A,B,D,C))=\delta_{a,x\bar{y}}\,\delta_{b,\bar{x}y}\,\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}
(with
\bar{x}\coloneqq 1-x
,
\bar{y}\coloneqq 1-y
), which are not compatible with any fixed causal order.

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},k_{2}),a_{k_{1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=p(\pi,a_{k_{1}}|\vec{x})\ \text{does not depend on }\vec{x}_{k_{2},k_{3}}

and

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),a_{k_{1}},a_{k_{2}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=p(\pi,a_{k_{1}},a_{k_{2}}|\vec{x})\ \text{does not depend on }x_{k_{3}}.

(88)

Assuming the non-influenceability condition of Eq. (
15
) and writing

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi)\,p(\vec{a}|\vec{x},\pi),

(89)

we then have that, for
\pi=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
,

\displaystyle p(a_{k_{1}}|\vec{x},\pi)=\frac{p(\pi,a_{k_{1}}|\vec{x})}{p(\pi)}\ \text{does not depend on }\vec{x}_{k_{2},k_{3}},

\displaystyle p(a_{k_{1}},a_{k_{2}}|\vec{x},\pi)=\frac{p(\pi,a_{k_{1}},a_{k_{2}}|\vec{x})}{p(\pi)}\ \text{does not depend on }x_{k_{3}},

(90)

so that
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x},\pi)
is compatible with the fixed causal order defined by
\pi
, and Eq. (
89
) indeed provides a convex decomposition of
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
onto correlations compatible with fixed causal orders.

We further note that for
N=3
, the “non-influenceable coarse-grained order” condition of Eq. (
16
) reduces to the mere “non-influenceable order” condition of Eq. (
15
) (since Eq. (
16
) implies that
p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\big|\vec{x}\big)=p\big((k_{1},k_{2})\big|\vec{x}\big)=p\big((\{k_{1}\},k_{2})\big|\vec{x}\big)
does not depend on
\vec{x}
). Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
also reduces in the tripartite case to
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
.

Appendix C
Proofs of the different causal bounds on
I_{4}\coloneqq p\big(a=b=xy,d=zt\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big)

We prove in this Appendix the various causal bounds on the expression
I_{4}
that we introduced in the main text to distinguish between correlations in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{causal}}
.

Recall that we consider here a 4-partite scenario with parties denoted
A,B,C,D
, with binary inputs
x,y,z,t=0,1
, each party having a trivial output
a,b,c,d=0
if their input is 0, or a binary output
a,b,c,d=0,1
if their input is 1 (the so-called ‘‘lazy scenario’’).
This is the ‘‘smallest’’ scenario (with the minimal number of parties, and of inputs and outputs for each party individually
28
28

28

Note, as in Footnote
25
, that one can in fact still further restrict the number of allowed
combinations
of inputs for the different parties. E.g., the expression
I_{4}
does not involve (in any nontrivial way) terms with
(z,t)=(0,0)
, nor with
(x,y,z,t)=(0,0,1,0)
. Hence one can already distinguish correlations with dynamical but non-influenceable causal order in scenarios where these combinations of inputs never appear.
Restricting to such smaller spaces of correlations (and perhaps also restricting to the probabilities of certain combinations of outputs only, e.g. only those that appear nontrivially in
I_{4}
), may help to get a better characterisation of the different polytopes of correlations under consideration.
) in which one can find correlations with dynamical but non-influenceable causal order: if one of the four parties has no output or no input at all, then using the observation from Footnote
26
, a similar argument to that for
N=3
parties (just above) shows that correlations in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
are necessarily convex combinations of correlations with fixed order (cf also Footnote
27
).

C.1
For (convex mixtures of) fixed orders:
I_{4}\leq_{\text{convFO}}\frac{15}{16}

By linearity and convexity, to obtain the bound on
I_{4}
for correlations in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, it is sufficient to calculate the bound for each possible fixed order independently.
Consider therefore a correlation
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)
compatible with a given fixed order
\pi
(described as a permutation of the parties).
We will first distinguish whether
C
comes before or after
D
in
\pi
:

•

if
C
comes before
D
in
\pi
, then let us write

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=p\big(a=b=xy,d=zt\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big)

\displaystyle\leq p\big(a=b=xy\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,z,t=0,1}\Big[p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big|x,y,z,t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,t}\Big[p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=0,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),\cancel{c=0}\big|x,y,z=0,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=t\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,t}\Big[1+p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=t\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)\Big],

(91)

where we used the fact that for an input
0
(here,
z=0
), the output is necessarily
0
(
c=0
).

Let us further distinguish here the different cases according to which party comes last in
\pi
:

–

If
D
comes last in
\pi
, then
29
29

29

In the proofs we shall indicate that a probability does not depend on a given input, according to some causality condition, by striking out the corresponding input; on the subsequent lines we will simply not write that input anymore.

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,t}\Big[1+p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1,\cancel{t}\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=t\big|x,y,z=1,\cancel{t}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y}\Big[2+2\,p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy)\big|x,y,z=1\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y}\Big[3+p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1\big)\Big]=\frac{1}{4}\Big(3+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{x,y}p\big(a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1\big)\Big)\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big(3+\frac{3}{4}\Big)=\frac{15}{16},

(92)

where the last inequality just follows from the LGYNI inequality
Branciard 2016
for
A
and
B
(for which conditioning on the input
z=1
of another party does not change the causal bound).

–

If
B
comes last in
\pi
, then

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}\Big[4+\underbrace{p\big(a=b=0\big|x=y=0,z=1,t\big)}_{1}+\underbrace{p\big(\neg(a=b=0),c=t\big|x=y=0,z=1,t\big)}_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+p\big(a=b=0\big|x=0,y=1,z=1,t\big)+\underbrace{p\big(\neg(a=b=0),c=t\big|x=0,y=1,z=1,t\big)}_{\leq\,p(\neg(a=b=0)|x=0,y=1,z=1,t)}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\underbrace{p\big(a=b=0\big|x=1,y=0,z=1,t\big)}_{p(a=0|x=1,\cancel{y=0},z=1,t)}+\underbrace{p\big(\neg(a=b=0),c=t\big|x=1,y=0,z=1,t\big)}_{p(a=1,c=t|x=1,\cancel{y=0},z=1,t)}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\underbrace{p\big(a=b=1\big|x=y=1,z=1,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=1),c=t\big|x=y=1,z=1,t\big)}_{p(a=b=1,c=\bar{t}|x=y=1,z=1,t)+p(c=t|x=\cancel{y}=1,z=1,\cancel{t})}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}\Big[6+p\big(a=0\big|x=z=1,t\big)+p\big(a=1,c=t\big|x=z=1,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\underbrace{p\big(a=b=1,c=\bar{t}\big|x=y=z=1,t\big)}_{\leq\,p(a=1,c=\bar{t}|x=\cancel{y}=z=1,t)}+\,p\big(c=t\big|x=z=1\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}\Big[7+p\big(c=t\big|x=z=1\big)\Big]=\frac{15}{16}

(93)

(where we used the notation
\bar{t}\coloneqq 1-t
).

–

If
A
comes last in
\pi
, then by symmetry (with respect to
A\leftrightarrow B
), we also get the bound
I_{4}\leq\frac{15}{16}
.

•

if
D
comes before
C
in
\pi
, then let us write (considering the case where
x=y=0
separately, and noting that
a=b=xy
always holds in this case):

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=p\big(a=b=xy,d=zt\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\Bigg[\sum_{z,t=0,1}p\big(d=zt\big|x=y=0,\cancel{z},t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,z,t=0,1:\\
(x,y)\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}\Big[\underbrace{p\big(a=b=xy,d=zt\big|x,y,z,t\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big|x,y,z,t\big)}_{\leq\,1}\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\Big[\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{z}}\,p\big(d=0\big|x=y=0,t=0\big)}_{2}+\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{z}}\,p\big(d=z\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)}_{1}+12\Big]=\frac{15}{16}.

(94)

All in all, for any fixed causal order, and by linearity for any convex combination of fixed causal orders, we obtained the bound

\displaystyle I_{4}\underset{\text{convFO}}{\leq}\frac{15}{16},

(95)

as in Eq. (
18
).

This bound can be reached for instance by the correlation (compatible with any fixed order where
C
comes before
D
)
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)=\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}
.

C.2
For correlations with non-influenceable causal orders:
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}

Consider a causal correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
, for which there exists
p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
that satisifes Eq. (
3
). One can then write

\displaystyle I_{4}=\sum_{\pi}I_{\pi}\quad\text{with}\quad I_{\pi}\coloneqq p\big(\pi,a=b=xy,d=zt\big)+p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big).

(96)

Following similar calculations as in the different cases with fixed order above, we see that:

•

If
C
comes before
D
in
\pi
, then (similarly to Eq. (
91
))

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,t}\Big[p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z=0,t\big)+p\big(\pi,a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)+p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=xy),c=t\big|x,y,z=1,t\big)\Big].

(97)

–

If
D
comes last in
\pi
, then it follows from the causality constraints of Eq. (
3
) that
p(\pi,a,b,c|x,y,z,t)
, and hence
p(\pi,a,b|x,y,z,t)
and
p(\pi|x,y,z,t)
, do not depend on
t
. We then have (similarly to Eq. (
92
))

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,t}\Big[p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z=0,\cancel{t}\big)+p\big(\pi,a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1,\cancel{t}\big)+p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=xy),c=t\big|x,y,z=1,\cancel{t}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y}\Big[2\,p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z=0\big)+p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z=1\big)+p\big(\pi,a=b=xy\big|x,y,z=1\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{x,y,z}p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y}p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=xy)\big|x,y,z=1\big).

(98)

If, in addition,
A
comes before
B
in
\pi
, then we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y}p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=xy)\big|x,y,z=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad=p\big(\pi,a=1\big|x=1,y=0,z=1\big)+\underbrace{p\big(\pi,b=1\big|x=0,y=1,z=1\big)}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{p\big(\pi,\neg(a=b=1)\big|x=1,y=1,z=1\big)}_{\geq p(\pi,a=0|x=1,y=1,z=1)}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sum_{y}p\big(\pi,a=\bar{y}\big|x=1,y,z=1\big),

(99)

so that

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\sum_{x,y,z}p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{y}p\big(\pi,a=\bar{y}\big|x=1,y,z=1\big).

(100)

Similarly, if
B
comes before
A
in
\pi
instead (still in addition to
D
coming last), then

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\sum_{x,y,z}p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x}p\big(\pi,b=\bar{x}\big|x,y=1,z=1\big).

(101)

–

If
B
comes last in
\pi
, then
p(\pi,a,c|x,y,z,t)
does not depend on
y
and we have (similarly to Eq. (
93
))

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}\Big[p\big(\pi\big|x=0,\cancel{y=0},z=0,t\big)+p\big(\pi\big|x=0,\cancel{y=0},z=1,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+p\big(\pi\big|x=0,\cancel{y=1},z=0,t\big)+p\big(\pi\big|x=0,\cancel{y=1},z=1,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+p\big(\pi\big|x=1,\cancel{y=0},z=0,t\big)+p\big(\pi,a=0\big|x=1,\cancel{y=0},z=1,t\big)+p\big(\pi,a=1,c=t\big|x=1,\cancel{y=0},z=1,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+p\big(\pi\big|x=1,\cancel{y=1},z=0,t\big)+p\big(\pi,a=1,c=\bar{t}\big|x=1,\cancel{y=1},z=1,t\big)+p\big(\pi,c=t\big|x=1,\cancel{y=1},z=1,t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}\Big[2\,p\big(\pi\big|x=z=0,t\big)+2\,p\big(\pi\big|x=0,z=1,t\big)+2\,p\big(\pi\big|x=1,z=0,t\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt+p\big(\pi\big|x=z=1,t\big)+p\big(\pi,c=t\big|x=z=1,t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{x,z,t}p\big(\pi\big|x,z,t\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|x=z=1,t\big).

(102)

–

If
A
comes last in
\pi
, then by symmetry the same bound holds (just replacing
x
by
y
in the above expression):

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\sum_{y,z,t}p\big(\pi\big|y,z,t\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{t}p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|y=z=1,t\big).

(103)

•

If
D
comes before
C
in
\pi
, then (similarly to Eq. (
94
))

\displaystyle I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\Big[\sum_{z=0,1}\big[p\big(\pi\big|x=y=0,z,t=0\big)+p\big(\pi,d=z\big|x=y=0,z,t=1\big)\big]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,z,t=0,1:\\
(x,y)\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z,t\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,z,t}p\big(\pi\big|x,y,z,t\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{z}p\big(\pi,d=\bar{z}\big|x=y=0,z,t=1\big).

(104)

Now, it follows from the causality constraints of Eq. (
3
) that
p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C,d|x,y,z,t)\coloneqq\sum_{\pi{:}D{\prec}C}p(\pi,d|x,y,z,t)
and
p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C|x,y,z,t)\coloneqq\sum_{\pi{:}D{\prec}C}p(\pi|x,y,z,t)
do not depend on
z
.
30
30

30

As indicated by the conditions ‘
\pi{:}D{\prec}C
’, the sums
\sum_{\pi{:}D{\prec}C}
are over all permutations in which
D
comes before
C
. To see that
p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C,d|x,y,z,t)
does not depend on
z
, one can write
p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C,d|x,y,z,t)=p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=(A,B,D),d\big|x,y,\cancel{z},t\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=(B,A,D),d\big|x,y,\cancel{z},t\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,D),d\big|x,\cancel{y},\cancel{z},t\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,D),d\big|\cancel{x},y,\cancel{z},t\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(D),d\big|\cancel{x},\cancel{y},\cancel{z},t\big)
, where all independencies (struck-out inputs) follow from Eq. (
3
).

We then have (again, similarly to Eq. (
94
))

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi{:}D{\prec}C}I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\sum_{x,y,z,t}p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x,y,\cancel{z},t\big)-\frac{1}{16}\sum_{z}p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C,d=\bar{z}\big|x=y=0,\cancel{z},t=1\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{x,y,t}p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x,y,t\big)-\frac{1}{16}p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big).

(105)

All in all, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{4}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi}I_{\pi}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
A\prec B\end{subarray}}I_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
B\prec A\end{subarray}}I_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
A\text{ last}\end{subarray}}I_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
B\text{ last}\end{subarray}}I_{\pi}+\sum_{\pi{:}D{\prec}C}I_{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\!\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
A\prec B\end{subarray}}}$}\!\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{x,y,z}}$}\,p(\pi|x,y,z)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{y}}$}\,p(\pi,a=\bar{y}|x=1,y,z=1)\Big]\!+\!\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
B\prec A\end{subarray}}}$}\!\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{x,y,z}}$}\,p(\pi|x,y,z)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{x}}$}\,p(\pi,b=\bar{x}|x,y=z=1)\Big]

\displaystyle+\!\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
B\text{ last}\end{subarray}}}$}\!\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{x,z,t}}$}\,p(\pi|x,z,t)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{t}}$}\,p(\pi,c=\bar{t}|x=z=1,t)\Big]+\!\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
A\text{ last}\end{subarray}}}$}\!\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{y,z,t}}$}\,p(\pi|y,z,t)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{t}}$}\,p(\pi,c=\bar{t}|y=z=1,t)\Big]

\displaystyle+{\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\displaystyle\sum_{x,y,t}}$}\,p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C|x,y,t)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C|x=y=0,t=1)

\displaystyle\quad=1-\frac{1}{16}\Big(J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)\Big),

(106)

with

\displaystyle J_{C\prec D}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
A\prec B\end{subarray}}\sum_{y}p\big(\pi,a=\bar{y}\big|x=1,y,z=1\big)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi:D\text{ last},\\
B\prec A\end{subarray}}\sum_{x}p\big(\pi,b=\bar{x}\big|x,y=z=1\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
B\text{ last}\end{subarray}}\sum_{t}p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|x=z=1,t\big)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi{:}C{\prec}D,\\
A\text{ last}\end{subarray}}\sum_{t}p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|y=z=1,t\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\underbrace{p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D),a=\bar{y}\big|x=1,y,\cancel{z=1}\big)}_{\geq\,p(\pi=(A,B,C,D)|x=1,y)-p((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B),a=y|x=1,\cancel{y})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(A,C,B,D),\\
(C,A,B,D)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{y}}\,p\big(\pi,a=\bar{y}\big|x=1,\cancel{y},z=1\big)}_{p(\pi|x=z=1)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{x}\underbrace{p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D),b=\bar{x}\big|x,y=\cancel{z}=1\big)}_{\geq\,p(\pi=(B,A,C,D)|x,y=1)-p((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A),b=x|\cancel{x},y=1)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(B,C,A,D),\\
(C,B,A,D)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{x}}\,p\big(\pi,b=\bar{x}\big|\cancel{x},y=z=1\big)}_{p(\pi|y=z=1)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{t}p\big(\pi=(C,D,A,B),c=\bar{t}\big|\cancel{x}=z=1,t\big)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(C,A,D,B),\\
(A,C,D,B)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{t}}\,p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|x=z=1,\cancel{t}\big)}_{p(\pi|x=z=1)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{t}p\big(\pi=(C,D,B,A),c=\bar{t}\big|\cancel{y}=z=1,t\big)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(C,B,D,A),\\
(B,C,D,A)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{t}}\,p\big(\pi,c=\bar{t}\big|y=z=1,\cancel{t}\big)}_{p(\pi|y=z=1)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{y}p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big|x=1,y\big)+\sum_{x}p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big|x,y=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\big|x=1\big)-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)\big|y=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\big|x=\cancel{z}=1\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(C,A)\big|\cancel{x}=z=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,C)\big|y=\cancel{z}=1\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(C,B)\big|\cancel{y}=z=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{t}}\,p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(C,D),c=\bar{t}\big|z=1,\cancel{t}\big)}_{p((k_{1},k_{2})=(C,D)|z=1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big|x=1,y\big)+\sum_{x}p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big|x,y=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\big|x=1\big)-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)\big|y=1\big)

\displaystyle\quad+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\big|x=1\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,C)\big|y=1\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big|\cancel{z=1}\big),

(107)

where we repeatedly used the causality constraints of Eq. (
3
) to get rid of some dependencies and simplify the expressions.

Note that we have not used the non-influenceable order (NIO) assumption of Eq. (
15
) yet: the derivations above hold for all causal correlations.
Assuming it now—that is, assuming
p(\pi|x,y,z,t)=p(\pi)
—we then have:

\displaystyle J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big)+p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big)-\frac{1}{2}p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\big)-\frac{1}{2}p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\big)+\frac{1}{2}p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,C)\big)+\frac{1}{2}p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big)+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\underbrace{p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\lor(B,A,C,D)\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\lor(B,C)\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big)+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)}_{=\,1}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{1}{2}\Big[\underbrace{p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\lor(B,A)\lor(A,C)\lor(B,C)\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big)}_{\leq 1}\Big]\geq\frac{1}{2},

(108)

where we used the shorthand notations
p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\lor(B,A,C,D)\big)=p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big)+p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big)
,
p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\lor(B,C)\big)=p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,C)\big)
etc. Recalling Eq. (
106
), we then obtain, for correlations with NIO:

\displaystyle I_{4}\leq 1-\frac{1}{16}\Big(J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)\Big)\underset{\text{NIO}}{\leq}\frac{31}{32},

(109)

as in Eq. (
19
).

This bound can be reached for instance by the correlation

\displaystyle p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{a,0}&\text{if}\ x=0\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.}_{p(a|x)}\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{b,0}&\text{if}\ y=0\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if}\ y=1,x=0\\
\delta_{b,a}&\text{if}\ y=1,x=1\end{array}\right.}_{p(b|x,y,a)}\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ x=y=0\\[2.84526pt]
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ a=b=xy\\
\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ \neg(a=b=xy)\end{array}\right.&\text{if}\ \neg(x=y=0)\end{array}\right.}_{p(c,d|x,y,z,t,a,b)},

which can easily be seen to be causal. In fact, an explicit causal decomposition as in Eq. (
3
) can be obtained by taking
p(\pi,a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)
to be of the same form as
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)
above, after just multiplying the terms
\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}
by
\delta_{\pi,(A,B,C,D)}
and the terms
\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}
by
\delta_{\pi,(A,B,D,C)}
.
31
31

31

One may notice that the marginal correlation

p(a,b|x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llcll}\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}&\text{if}\ x=y=0&\ ;&\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{a,0}&\text{if}\ x=0,y=1\\
\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{b,0}&\text{if}\ x=1,y=0&\ ;&\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{a,b}&\text{if}\ x=y=1\end{array}\right.

(which does not depend on
z,t
) is non-signalling—and in fact, even Bell-local
Bell 1964
(as is any non-signalling correlation in the “lazy” scenario, where each party has nontrivial outputs for at most one input):
p(a,b|x,y)=\sum_{\lambda=0,1}\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a,\lambda x}\delta_{b,\lambda y}
—so one could also decompose this correlation onto different orders for
A
and
B
.

For such a decomposition, it is then easy to see that
p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big|x,y,z,t\big)=p\big(\pi=(A,B,D,C)\big|x,y,z,t\big)=\frac{1}{2}
does not depend on the inputs
x,y,z,t
, which indeed proves that the correlation is in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
—and not in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, since it violates the bound
I_{4}\underset{\text{convFO}}{\leq}\frac{15}{16}
.

C.3
For correlations with non-influenceable coarse-grained causal orders:
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}\frac{47}{48}

Let us now relax the NIO assumption, and just consider the “non-influenceable coarse-grained causal orders” (NIO
′
) assumption of Eq. (
16
), that
p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})\big|\vec{x}\big)=p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})\big)
does not depend on
\vec{x}
.
Note that it implies in particular, here, that

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptp\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)

\displaystyle=p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},D)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,D\},B)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{B,D\},A)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,D\},C)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{B,D\},C)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{1},k_{2})=(\{D\},C)\big|\cancel{x=y=0,t=1}\big)\ =\ p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)

(118)

does not depend on the inputs
x,y,t
.

Coming back to Eq. (
107
)—which, as we emphasised, holds for all causal correlations—we can now write (instead of Eq. (
108
))

\displaystyle J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)\geq\frac{1}{3}J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{1}{3}\Big[p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big|x=1,y=0\big)+p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big|x=0,y=1\big)+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},C)\big|\cancel{x=y=1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\big|\cancel{x=1}\big)-p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)\big|\cancel{y=1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\big|\cancel{x=1}\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,C)\big|\cancel{y=1}\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big|\cancel{z=1}\big)+\hskip-28.45274pt\underbrace{3\,p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)}_{\geq\,p(\pi{:}D{\prec}C)+2\,p((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},D))\hskip 16.38895pt}\hskip-28.45274pt\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{1}{3}\Bigg[\underbrace{p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},C)\big)+p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,C)\lor(B,C)\big)+p\big((k_{1})=(C)\big)+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big)}_{=\,1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},D)\big)-\Big(\underbrace{p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)\big)-p\big(\pi=(A,B,C,D)\big|x=1,y=0\big)}_{p(\pi=(A,B,D,C)|x=1,y=0)}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+p\big((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},D)\big)-\Big(\underbrace{p\big((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)\big)-p\big(\pi=(B,A,C,D)\big|x=0,y=1\big)}_{p(\pi=(B,A,D,C)|x=0,y=1)}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{3}\Big[1+p\big(\pi=(B,A,D,C)\big|x=1,y=0\big)+p\big(\pi=(A,B,D,C)\big|x=0,y=1\big)\Big]\geq\frac{1}{3}.

(119)

Recalling again Eq. (
106
), we then obtain, for correlations with non-influenceable coarse-grained causal orders:

\displaystyle I_{4}\leq 1-\frac{1}{16}\Big(J_{C\prec D}+p\big(\pi{:}D{\prec}C\big|x=y=0,t=1\big)\Big)\underset{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}{\leq}\frac{47}{48},

(120)

as in Eq. (
20
).

This bound can be reached for instance by the correlation

\displaystyle p\big(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t\big)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{2}{3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ x=0\\[2.84526pt]
\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{b,y}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ y=a\\
\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ y=\bar{a}\end{array}\right.&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.\,+\,\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{b,y}\,\delta_{a,xy}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ x=0\\[2.84526pt]
\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.,

which can easily be seen to be causal. To see that it is in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
, notice that one can decompose it as

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t\big)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2}),(A,B)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\,\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(C,D)}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ x=0\\[2.84526pt]
\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{b,y}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(C,D)}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ y=a\\
\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(D,C)}\,\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ y=\bar{a}\end{array}\right.&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2}),(B,A)}\,\delta_{b,y}\,\delta_{a,xy}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(D,C)}\,\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ x=0\\[2.84526pt]
\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(C,D)}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right.,

such that

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})|x,y,z,t\big)=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{2}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),(A,B,C,D)}+\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),(B,A,D,C)}&\text{if}\ x=0\\[2.84526pt]
\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),(A,B,C,D)}+\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),(A,B,D,C)}+\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),(B,A,C,D)}&\text{if}\ x=1\end{array}\right..

It is then easy to verify that
p((k_{1})=(A)|x,y,z,t)=p((k_{1},k_{2})=(A,B)|x,y,z,t)=\frac{2}{3}
,
p((k_{1})=(B)|x,y,z,t)=p((k_{1},k_{2})=(B,A)|x,y,z,t)=\frac{1}{3}
,
p((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},C)|x,y,z,t)=p((\mathcal{K}_{3},k_{4})=(\{A,B,C\},D)|x,y,z,t)=\frac{2}{3}
and
p((\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})=(\{A,B\},D)|x,y,z,t)=p((\mathcal{K}_{3},k_{4})=(\{A,B,D\},C)|x,y,z,t)=\frac{1}{3}
do not depend on
x,y,z,t
(with all other probabilities
p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})\big|\vec{x}\big)
being null), which indeed proves that the correlation is in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
—and not in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
, since it violates the bound
I_{4}\underset{\text{NIO}}{\leq}\frac{31}{32}
.

C.4
For general causal correlations:
I_{4}\leq_{\text{causal}}1

We conclude this appendix with the bound for general causal correlations, which is simply the trivial algebraic bound:

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=p\big(a=b=xy,d=zt\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy),c=zt\big)\leq p\big(a=b=xy\big)+p\big(\neg(a=b=xy)\big)=1.

(133)

An example of a causal correlation that indeed reaches this algebraic maximum is

\displaystyle p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)=\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=0\\[2.84526pt]
\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=1\end{array}\right.,

which can be decomposed as

p((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)=\delta_{(k_{1},k_{2}),(A,B)}\,\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\!\!\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(C,D)}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=0\\[2.84526pt]
\!\!\delta_{(k_{3},k_{4}),(D,C)}\,\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=1\end{array}\right.,

(136)

in which the causal order between
C
and
D
depends on the product of inputs
xy
(indeed the causal order has to be “influenceable” so as to violate the previous bounds).

Appendix D
Description and characterisation of our different classes of quantum circuits

In this appendix we provide more details on the description and characterisation of the different classes of quantum circuits with classical and quantum control of causal order considered in the paper. These are described within the wider framework of process matrices
Oreshkov et al. 2012
. We start by recalling, for completeness, the validity constraints for general process matrices that the characterisations of some of our classes refer to.

D.1
Validity constraints for general process matrices

The process matrix framework was first introduced to characterise the set of all possible correlations that can be established (in a consistent manner) by a number of parties implementing operations that are locally described by the quantum formalism (as quantum instruments
Davies and Lewis 1970
), but without assuming
a priori
that these are applied in any specific, well-defined causal order
Oreshkov et al. 2012
. Under some reasonable assumptions (in particular: dependency of the probabilities on the realised operations only rather than on the full quantum instruments; linearity of the probabilities to preserve the interpretation of probabilistic mixtures of operations and coarse-graining of outcomes), it was shown that all such correlations could be written in the form of the generalised Born rule of Eq. (
26
), for some “process matrix”
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}})
(where
\mathcal{N}=\{1,\ldots,N\}
refers to the set of parties, each having input/output spaces
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}/\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
, and with
\mathcal{H}^{A_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}}=\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{IO}}=\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
).

Requiring that the probabilities thus obtained are nonnegative, even when the parties share other auxiliary systems and their instruments can also act on these, imposes that the process matrix must be positive semidefinite (PSD):
W\geq 0
. Recalling that the elements of a quantum instrument must sum to a trace-preserving (TP) map, and that the TP condition for a map
\mathcal{M}_{k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}})
translates in terms of its Choi matrix into
\Tr_{A_{k}^{O}}M_{k}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k}^{I}}
, then the requirement that the probabilities must be normalised, on the other hand, can be phrased as follows:

\displaystyle\forall\,M_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{1}^{IO}}),\ldots,M_{N}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{N}^{IO}})\ \text{ such that }\ \Tr_{A_{1}^{O}}M_{1}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{1}^{I}},\ldots,\Tr_{A_{N}^{O}}M_{N}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{N}^{I}},\ \ \Tr[\Big(\bigotimes_{k}M_{k}\Big)^{\!T}\,W]=1.

(137)

This was shown to be equivalent to imposing some linear and affine constraints on
W
, which can be written as
Araújo et al. 2015
;
Wechs et al. 2019
32
32

32

We note that another equivalent formulation of these constraints was also given in terms of “allowed” and “forbidden” terms in a Hilbert-Schmidt basis decomposition of
W

Oreshkov et al. 2012
;
Oreshkov and Giarmatzi 2016
.

\displaystyle\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{N},\,{}_{\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}[1-A_{k}^{O}]}\Tr_{A_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}}^{IO}}W=0\quad\text{and}\quad\Tr W=d_{\mathcal{N}}^{O},

(138)

where we used the notation
{}_{[1-A_{k}^{O}]}W\coloneqq W-(\Tr_{A_{k}^{O}}W)\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k}^{O}}}{d_{k}^{O}}
(with
d_{k}^{O}
denoting the dimension of
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
), such that
{}_{\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}[1-A_{k}^{O}]}W={}_{[1-A_{k_{1}}^{O}]}({}_{[1-A_{k_{2}}^{O}]}(\cdots({}_{[1-A_{k_{|\mathcal{K}|}}^{O}]}W)))
for
\mathcal{K}=\{k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{|\mathcal{K}|}\}
, and where
d_{\mathcal{N}}^{O}\coloneqq\prod_{k\in\mathcal{N}}d_{k}^{O}
is the product of the dimensions of all parties’ output spaces.

As recalled in the main text, another version of the process matrix framework was introduced in Ref.
Araújo et al. 2017
, with some “open” past and future spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
. Here the process matrix acts as a “supermap”
Chiribella et al. 2008a
that transforms the parties’ local maps to a new quantum map from
\mathcal{H}^{P}
to
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, given by the further generalised Born rule of Eq. (
27
). Requiring that completely positive (CP) maps thereby get transformed to a CP map (even when involving auxiliary systems: the supermap must be “completely CP-preserving”) imposes again that
W
must be PSD. The requirement that TP maps (corresponding e.g. to the sum of the parties’ instrument elements) get transformed to a TP map (the supermap must be “TP-preserving”), on the other hand, can be written here as in Eq. (
137
), just replacing the last equality by
\Tr_{F}[(\bigotimes_{k}M_{k})^{T}\,W]=\mathbbm{1}^{P}
. It can be shown to be equivalent to the following linear and affine constraints:
33
33

33

These can be verified to be equivalent to the set of constraints obtained (after certain simplifications) from Eq. (
137
), when one attributes
\mathcal{H}^{P}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}
to two additional parties with trivial input and output spaces, respectively.
}

\displaystyle\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{N},\,{}_{\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}[1-A_{k}^{O}]}\Tr_{A_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}}^{IO}F}W=0\quad\text{and}\quad\Tr_{A_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}W=d_{\mathcal{N}}^{O}\,\mathbbm{1}^{P}.

(139)

Let us emphasise again that the validity constraints of Eqs. (
138
) and (
139
) correspond to the requirement that the generalised Born rule of Eq. (
26
) gives the constant value 1, or that the alternative version of Eq. (
27
) returns a trace-preserving global map
M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}}^{PF}
, whenever one plugs in TP external operations (obtained e.g. as the sum of the elements of the parties’ instruments).
When dealing with process matrices, one is often interested in the linear parts of the validity constraints, ignoring the affine part, i.e. ignoring the constraint
\Tr W=d_{\mathcal{N}}^{O}
in Eq. (
138
), or only imposing
\Tr_{A_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}W\propto\mathbbm{1}^{P}
in Eq. (
139
). One then refers to matrices that are valid process matrices
up to normalisation
only
34
34

34

Notice that this includes in particular the null matrix,
W=0
.
—as in the characterisations of our classes
QC-NICC
and
QC-NIQC
, see Eqs. (
III.2.3
) and (
III.3.3
).
Requiring validity up to normalisation only, for a given
W
, therefore has the following interpretation:
when plugging in TP external operations, the generalised Born rules of Eq. (
26
) or (
27
) must return a constant value, or a global induced map whose output state has a constant trace, respectively, whatever the choice of the TP external operations.

D.2
Quantum circuits with classical control of causal order (QC-CCs)

Let us now come to a more in-depth presentation of QC-CCs, and of the subclasses of QC-convFOs and QC-NICCs that we considered in this work. Secs.
D.2.1
(general QC-CCs) and
D.2.2
(QC-convFOs) below are essentially just reviews of previous work, to set up the stage for the presentation and characterisation of our new class of QC-NICCs in Sec.
D.2.3
.

QC-CCs were introduced and characterised in Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
, which we refer to for more details. One technical ingredient that we will use below is the so-called
link product

Chiribella et al. 2008b
;
Chiribella et al. 2009
. It is defined, for two matrices
M_{1}^{XY}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{XY})
and
M_{2}^{XY}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{YZ})
that share a common space
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
, as

\displaystyle M_{1}^{XY}*M_{2}^{YZ}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\big(\mathbbm{1}^{XZ}\otimes{\langle\!\langle\mathbbm{1}|}^{YY}\big)(M_{1}^{XY}\otimes M_{2}^{YZ})\big(\mathbbm{1}^{XZ}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YY}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr_{Y}\big[\big((M_{1}^{XY})^{T_{Y}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{Z}\big)\big(\mathbbm{1}^{X}\otimes M_{2}^{YZ}\big)\big]\quad\in\mathcal{L}\big(\mathcal{H}^{XZ}\big),

(140)

where
{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YY}\coloneqq\sum_{j}{|i\rangle}^{Y}\otimes{|i\rangle}^{Y}
denotes the unnormalised maximally entangled state (defined for two copies of the space
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
with computational basis
\{{|i\rangle}^{Y}\}_{i}
), and where
\Tr_{Y}
and
{}^{T_{Y}}
denote the partial trace and partial transpose over
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
, respectively. The link product corresponds in the Choi picture to the composition of maps: if
M_{1}^{XY}
and
M_{2}^{YZ}
are the Choi matrices of two linear maps
\mathcal{M}_{1}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y})
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Z})
, then
M_{1}^{XY}*M_{2}^{YZ}
is the Choi matrix of their composition
\mathcal{M}_{2}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Z})
. (The same interpretation in terms of composition also applies if the output and input spaces of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
do not match
Chiribella et al. 2008b
;
Chiribella et al. 2009
;
Wechs et al. 2021
.)

The link product is commutative (up to a re-ordering of the tensor products) and associative (provided each Hilbert space is involved at most twice in an
n
-fold link product—as will always be the case here). It reduces to a standard tensor product
M_{1}^{X}*M_{2}^{Z}=M_{1}^{X}\otimes M_{2}^{Z}
if the space
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
is trivial, and to a scalar product
M_{1}^{Y}*M_{2}^{Y}=\Tr[(M_{1}^{Y})^{T}M_{2}^{Y}]
if the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{X}
and
\mathcal{H}^{Z}
are trivial.
Noticing that the Choi matrix of the preparation of a state
\rho
coincides with the density matrix
\rho
itself, one can write the output state of a map
\mathcal{M}
(with Choi matrix
M
) applied to
\rho
as the composition
\mathcal{M}(\rho)=\rho*M
.
Notice also that the generalised Born rule of Eq. (
27
) can be written in a simple form as a link product, namely:
M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}}^{PF}=\big(\bigotimes_{k}M^{A_{k}}_{a_{k}|x_{k}}\big)*W
.

D.2.1
General QC-CCs

Construction of a QC-CC.

As briefly introduced in the main text, a QC-CC consists in the application of a number of “internal operations”, each of which determines which “external operation” (implemented by one of the
N
parties
A_{k}
) is to be applied next—with each of the latter being applied once and only once by the end of the protocol.

One way to understand this is to consider that each internal operation corresponds to a quantum instrument
\{\mathcal{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}\}_{k_{n+1}\notin\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
, whose classical output value
k_{n+1}
indicates the label of the next external operation to be applied, conditioned on the previous operations being those labelled
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, in that order.
The instrument corresponding to the first internal operation consists of CP maps
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{1}}^{I}\alpha_{1}})
that transform the input state in the “global past” space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
into a state in the input space
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}
of the first operation to be applied, as well as in some auxiliary “memory” space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}
.
35
35

35

The auxiliary “memory” systems are not touched by the external operations. Notice that these may just be trivial.

The subsequent internal operations, for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
, consist of CP maps
\mathcal{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}\alpha_{n}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}})
that transform (conditioned on the previous external operations being
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, in that order) the output state of the latest external operation, together with the memory system, into a state in the input space
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}
of the next operation to be applied, as well as in some new memory space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{n+1}}
. Finally, the last internal operation consists of just a single CPTP map
\mathcal{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}\alpha_{N}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F})
(since there is no choice of next operation to be made) that transforms (conditioned on all external operations having been applied in the order
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
) the output state of the last external operation, together with the memory system, into a state in the “global future” space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
.

Figure 9:
Graphical representation of a QC-CC: a more detailed version than Fig.
2
(clarifying here the internal structure of the circuit operations
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
), reproduced from Fig. 9 of Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
.

Another equivalent way to describe such a protocol—as depicted in Fig.
2
of the main text, or with more details in Fig.
9
here—is to encode the output of these instruments into a physical (effectively classical) system and consider the internal operations to also act on this system, which is used to control the subsequent operations. This can be done by introducing, for each time-slot
t_{n}
(
n=1,\ldots,N
) at which an external operation is to be applied, a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{C_{n}}
carrying orthogonal control states of the form

\displaystyle[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}}\coloneqq{|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\rangle\!\langle(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})|}^{C_{n}},

(141)

which keeps track of all previous external operations, as well as of the current one to be applied.

In order to define a controlled version of the external operations, it is assumed for simplicity that all input Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}
(all output Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
, respectively) of all external operations are isomorphic. One can then introduce, for each time-slot
t_{n}
, a “generic” input Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}}
(a “generic” output Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}}
, resp.), isomorphic to all
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}
’s (isomorphic to all
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
’s, resp.). In this way, when an external operation
36
36

36

Here for simplicity we allow for some double-use of notation, using the same
A_{k}
for the Choi matrix of an external operation and the party that implements it, as considered in the main text. The operations
A_{k}
(in the Choi picture) considered here correspond to the individual CP maps
M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}^{A_{k}}
considered in the main text to be part of each party’s instrument.

\mathcal{A}_{k_{n}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}})
(with Choi matrix
A_{k_{n}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n}}^{IO}})
) is applied at time-slot
t_{n}
, one can identify it formally with an operation of the type
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k_{n}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}})
(with Choi matrix
\tilde{A}_{k_{n}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{IO}})
). This allows one to write all the external operations that may be applied at time-slot
t_{n}
as acting in the same generic input and output spaces, and to embed these into some “larger” controlled operation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
, which applies the desired operation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k_{n}}
depending on the state of the control (more specifically, on its last label
k_{n}
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k_{n}}\otimes\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{C_{n}\to C_{n}^{\prime}}\ :\ \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}C_{n}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}C_{n}^{\prime}}),

(142)

where for later convenience we introduced a copy
\mathcal{H}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}
of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{C_{n}}
, and where
\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{C_{n}\to C_{n}^{\prime}}
is the (classical) map that projects the control system onto the state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}}
, while re-labelling the control system
C_{n}
to
C_{n}^{\prime}
.

Similarly, one can formally write the internal operations
\mathcal{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}\alpha_{n}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}})
(for each
n=1,\ldots,N-1
) as maps of the type
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}})
acting in the generic Hilbert space introduced above. These can then be embedded into some “larger” operation
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
that also involves the control system: upon receiving the latter in the state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}
(as outputted by the operation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
),
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
applies the operations
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and updates the control system into the corresponding state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n}}
. Formally:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}\otimes\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),k_{n+1}}^{C_{n}^{\prime}\to C_{n+1}}\ :\ \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}}),

(143)

where
\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),k_{n+1}}^{C_{n}^{\prime}\to C_{n+1}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{C_{n}^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{C_{n+1}})
is the (classical) map that projects the control system onto
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}
and updates it to
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n+1}}
.
For the first and last internal operations, one similary defines

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\otimes\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\emptyset\to C_{1}}\ :\ \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{1}^{I}\alpha_{1}C_{1}})

(144)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{N+1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\otimes\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),F}^{C_{N}^{\prime}\to\emptyset}\ :\ \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{N}^{O}\alpha_{N}C_{N}^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F}),

(145)

where
\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\emptyset\to C_{1}}
and
\scalebox{1.6}{$\pi$}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),F}^{C_{N}^{\prime}\to\emptyset}
are the maps that create the initial control states
[\![(k_{1})]\!]^{C_{1}}
and that project onto the final control state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})]\!]^{C_{N}^{\prime}}
, respectively.

In the Choi representation, all these embedding operations are written

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\tilde{A}_{k_{n}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}\ \in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}C_{n}\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}C_{n}^{\prime}}),

(146)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}}\tilde{M}_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\otimes[\![(k_{1})]\!]^{C_{1}}\ \in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P\tilde{A}_{1}^{I}\alpha_{1}C_{1}}),

(147)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{n+1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}\tilde{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n+1}}\ \in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}})

\displaystyle\hskip 312.9803pt(\text{for }n=1,\ldots,N-1),

(148)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{N+1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{M}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})]\!]^{C_{N}^{\prime}}\ \in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{N}^{O}\alpha_{N}C_{N}^{\prime}F}).

(149)

These allow one, in particular, to conveniently evaluate the state of all systems at any point in the circuit. Consider, for that, inputting some state
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})
in the global past of the circuit. The states
\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}
before and
\varrho_{(n+1)}
after each operation
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
are found (recursively) to be

\displaystyle\varrho_{(0)}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\rho\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P}),

(150)

\displaystyle\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\rho*\tilde{M}_{1}*\tilde{A}_{1}*\tilde{M}_{2}*\cdots*\tilde{M}_{n}*\tilde{A}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}*\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}}\big)\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\hskip 284.52756pt\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}})\quad\text{ for }n\geq 1

(151)

and

\displaystyle\varrho_{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\rho*\tilde{M}_{1}*\tilde{A}_{1}*\tilde{M}_{2}*\cdots*\tilde{M}_{n}*\tilde{A}_{n}*\tilde{M}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}*\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n+1}}\ \in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}})\quad\text{ for }n\leq N-1,

(152)

\displaystyle\varrho_{(N+1)}

\displaystyle=\rho*\tilde{M}_{1}*\tilde{A}_{1}*\tilde{M}_{2}*\cdots*\tilde{M}_{N}*\tilde{A}_{N}*\tilde{M}_{N+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{N}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\rho\otimes A_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{N}\big)*\Big(\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\Big)\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F}),

(153)

where
\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}}
and
\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}}
in Eqs. (
151
) and (
152
) simply represent some trivial mappings from the external operations’ output and input spaces
\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}}
to the corresponding generic output and input spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}}
(notice how the tildes were removed between the first and second lines in each equation).

Trace-preserving conditions.

In order to define a TP-preserving quantum circuit—such that when one plugs in TP external operations, the induced global map remains TP—one requires all internal operations
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
to preserve the trace of their input state (they must be TP on their
effective
input space, in which their input state
\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}
may effectively be found). In fact, a stronger condition is imposed here: the control system must evolve in a consistent way, so that for any
n\leq N-1
and any given sequence
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, the probabilities of finding the control in the state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n+1}}
after the operation
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
(and of the operations
A_{k_{1}},\ldots,A_{k_{n}}
to be applied, when these are part of quantum instruments) sum up, for all possible
k_{n+1}\notin\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}
, to the probability of finding the control in the state
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}}
before
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}
(and of the operations
A_{k_{1}},\ldots,A_{k_{n}}
to be applied).
From Eqs. (
151
) and (
152
), these probabilities are

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n+1}}\Tr[(\mathbbm{1}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})]\!]^{C_{n+1}})\varrho_{(n+1)}]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=\sum_{k_{n+1}}\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)^{T}\sum_{k_{n+1}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}})\big].

(154)

and

\displaystyle\Tr[(\mathbbm{1}^{\tilde{A}_{O}^{n}\alpha_{n}}\otimes[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{\prime}})\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}\big]=\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}\big)\big].

(155)

As these two expressions must be equal for all possible
\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n}}
, which span the whole space
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}^{IO}})
, then the second terms inside both final traces must themselves be equal:

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n+1}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}})=\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}.

(156)

For the limiting cases
n=0
and
n=N
, we correspondingly obtain the constraints:

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}\alpha_{1}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}})=\mathbbm{1}^{P}

(157)

and

\displaystyle\Tr_{F}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F})=\Tr_{\alpha_{N}}(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}.

(158)

Process matrix description and characterisation.

The expression for
\varrho_{N+1}
in Eq. (
153
) allows one to readily obtain the process matrix description of the QC-CC under consideration. Indeed, it follows from the generalised Born rule of Eq. (
27
) (or its form given on p.
D.2
after the introduction of the link product) that the final state in the global future should be
\varrho_{N+1}=(\rho\otimes A_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{N})*W
.
Since the
\rho\otimes A_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{N}
’s span the whole space
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}})
, there is only one matrix
W
that gives the correct output state through this expression. From Eq. (
153
) we can thus directly identify the process matrix of the QC-CC under consideration to be

\displaystyle W=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}).

(159)

Defining, for all
n=1,\ldots,N
and all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
,

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}\big)\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}}),

(160)

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}),

(161)

then it follows directly from Eq. (
159
) and the TP conditions of Eqs. (
156
)–(
158
) that the process matrix
W\,(\geq 0)
of a QC-CC has a characterisation as in Eq. (
III.2.1
).

Conversely, from any decomposition of a matrix
W
in terms of PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.1
), one can explicitly reconstruct (in a non-unique manner) the internal operations
M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and
M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}
of a QC-QC (satisfying the required TP conditions), whose composition reproduces
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
,
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W
as in Eqs. (
159
)–(
161
) above. This shows that Eq. (
III.2.1
) is also a sufficient condition for
W
to be a QC-CC.
An explicit procedure for such a reconstruction was detailed in Appendix B of Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
.

D.2.2
Convex mixtures of quantum circuits with fixed causal order (QC-convFOs)

Quantum circuits with fixed causal order (QC-FOs).

QC-FOs have been described and characterised previously, under the alternative names of channels with memory
Kretschmann and Werner 2005
, quantum strategies
Gutoski and Watrous 2007
, or quantum combs
Chiribella et al. 2008b
;
Chiribella et al. 2009
. One may describe them in a similar and in fact much simpler way to QC-CCs above, without the need to introduce the control system. For a given fixed order
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
, the internal operations would simply be of the form
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
. The TP conditions they satisfy are as in Eqs. (
156
)–(
158
), just without the sums
\sum_{k_{n+1}},\sum_{k_{1}}
. One can identify their process matrix representation in the same way as we did above, and one simply gets

\displaystyle W=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}=M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{\to k_{N}}*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}).

(162)

From the TP conditions, and defining
37
37

37

Notice that in contrast to the general QC-CC case, the matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
defined here are all valid process matrices (indeed, one can readily verify explicitly that the constraints of Eq. (
139
) are satisfied). This implies in particular that the matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
that appear in the QC-convFO decomposition of Eq. (
III.2.2
), defined below as
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\coloneqq q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
, are also valid process matrices up to normalisation.

W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
as in Eq. (
160
) above, one can readily see that the process matrix
W=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}\,(\geq 0)
of a quantum circuit with fixed causal order
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
is necessarily such that for all
n=1,\ldots,N
, there exist PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Tr_{F}W=\Tr_{F}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,n=1,\ldots,N-1,\ \ \Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1})}=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1})}=\mathbbm{1}^{P}.\end{array}\right.

Conversely, for any PSD matrix
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})
satisfying the above constraint, one can construct (in a similar way to the case of general QC-CCs) internal circuit operations
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
, whose composition as in Eq. (
162
) gives precisely
W

Gutoski and Watrous 2007
;
Chiribella et al. 2009
;
Wechs et al. 2021
. This shows that Eq. (
D.2.2
) is also a sufficient characterisation of the class of QC-FOs.

Convex mixtures of QC-FOs (QC-convFOs).

The process matrix representation of a convex mixture of QC-FOs is then of the form
W=\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}q_{\pi}\,\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
, with
q_{\pi}\geq 0
,
\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}=1
, and each
\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
satisfying the constraints of Eq. (
D.2.2
).
By defining the subnormalised process matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}\coloneqq q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\coloneqq q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
(with
\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
obtained from the QC-FO decomposition of
\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
),
one readily gets the characterisation of Eq. (
III.2.2
) for the class of QC-convFOs.
The fact that Eq. (
III.2.2
) is a sufficient condition for
W
to be a QC-convFO also follows straightforwardly from the sufficiency of Eq. (
D.2.2
) above.

An alternative description of QC-convFOs.

We note that one could present an alternative description of QC-convFOs, by encoding each order in the mixture into a classical system that is then used to control all operations and their order—as in a general QC-CC, except that the complete order
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
would be decided, and encoded in the control system, from the very beginning (and independently of the global input state of the circuit).

This approach is the one we will follow to define the class of quantum circuits with superposition of fixed causal orders (QC-supFO), after turning the classical control system into a quantum control, whose initial state will encode the complete order; see Sec.
D.3.2
below.

D.2.3
Quantum circuits with non-influenceable classical control of causal order (QC-NICCs)

Let us now come to our new class of QC-NICCs, defined in Sec.
III.2.3
of the main text as follows:

QC-NICCs are the QC-CCs for which at any intermediate time step, the state of the control system is independent of the choice of previously applied external operations and of the potential initial state preparation in the global past,

with here the external operations referring to the parties’ whole instruments (or simply a quantum channel they apply) rather than the individual CP maps that comprise them, and the state of the control system being considered after summing over all these individual CP maps.

The states
\varrho_{(n)}
,
\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}
of all systems at any intermediate time step
t_{n}
(for
n=1,\ldots,N
, just before or just after the operation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
) were given in Eqs. (
151
) and (
152
). Considering the case where all external operations
A_{k}
are TP (equivalently, summing over the CP maps that constitute the parties’ instruments), the reduced state of the control system is obtained as
38
38

38

In Eq. (
166
) we used the TP property
\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}A_{k_{n}}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}
to remove
A_{k_{n}}
from the expression of
\Tr_{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}}\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}
. Since we formally get the same reduced state of the control before and after the operation
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
, we ignore here the distinction between systems
C_{n}
and
C_{n}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\Tr_{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}\alpha_{n}}\varrho_{(n)}=\Tr_{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}}\varrho_{(n)}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\!\!\Tr[\big(\rho\!\otimes\!A_{k_{1}}\!\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\!A_{k_{n-1}}\big)*\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\!*\!M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}\!*\!\cdots\!*\!M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}\big)][\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n-1}}\big)*W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}]\,[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}},

(166)

with
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
as defined in Eq. (
160
), and contributing to the decomposition of the process matrix
W
of the QC-CC under consideration, as in Eq. (
III.2.1
).

The condition that the state of the control system should be independent of
\rho
and of the previously applied external operations translates into the constraint that for all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
,

\displaystyle\Tr[\big(\rho\otimes A_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{k_{n-1}}\big)*W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}]

\displaystyle\text{ does not depend on }\rho,A_{k_{1}},\ldots,A_{k_{n-1}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ whenever }A_{k_{1}},\ldots,A_{k_{n-1}}\text{ are TP (and $\Tr\rho=1$)}.

(167)

Recalling the discussion at the end of Sec.
D.1
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\text{all}\ W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\text{'s are valid process matrices (up to normalisation)},

(168)

as in Eq. (
III.2.3
).

Conversely, as we recalled above, from a decomposition of
W
in terms of
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.1
), one can reconstruct some internal operations
M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
(and
M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}
) of a QC-QC such that
\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}\big(M_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}*M_{(k_{1})}^{\to k_{2}}*\cdots*M_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}^{\to k_{n}}\big)=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
.
If each
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
is proportional to a valid process matrix, then the reduced state of the control system at time step
t_{n}
, as given in Eq. (
166
) above, does not depend on the operations
A_{k}
when these are TP. It follows that
W
is the process matrix of a QC-NICC.

D.2.4
Inclusion relations

It is quite clear, from the definition of the
QC-NICC
class just above, that
\textup{{QC-NICC}}\subset\textup{{QC-CC}}
.
To see that
\textup{{QC-convFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-NICC}}
, consider a process matrix
W\in\textup{{QC-convFO}}
, with a decomposition in terms of PSD matrices (and even valid process matrices, up to normalisation; see Footnote
37
)
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.2
); defining, for each
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
,
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, one can easily see that these satisfy the QC-CC decomposition of Eq. (
III.2.1
), and that these are themselves valid process matrices (up to normalisation), which proves that
W\in\textup{{QC-NICC}}
.
Hence, we obtain the general inclusion relations of Eq. (
41
):

\displaystyle\text{{{QC-convFO}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-NICC}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-CC}}}.

(169)

It is straightforward to see that all these classes reduce to the same one (which also matches the whole class of valid process matrices, see Sec.
D.1
) for
N=1
.

For
N=2
already, with a nontrivial global past space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, one finds a strict separation between
QC-NICC
and
QC-CC
: an example of a process matrix in
\text{{{QC-CC}}}\backslash\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
was given by the “classical switch”
W_{\textup{CS}}
, Eq. (
44
) of the main text. If
\mathcal{H}^{P}
is trivial on the other hand,
QC-NICC
and
QC-CC
coincide for
N=2
(see the proof below), and one needs to go to
N\geq 3
to see a strict separation.

As for
QC-convFO
vs
QC-NICC
, these can be seen to coincide for
N\leq 3
(proof below). A strict separation can be found for
N\geq 4
: an example of a process matrix in
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\backslash\text{{{QC-convFO}}}
, for
N=4
with trivial global past and future spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
, was given by
W_{\textup{NICC}}
from Eq. (
43
).

•

Proof that
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}=\text{{{QC-CC}}}
for
N=2
and a trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P}
:
Given the general inclusion
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-CC}}}
, it remains to prove the inclusion in the reverse direction.

Consider for that the process matrix
W
of a QC-CC in that scenario. It decomposes in terms of PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},F)}
,
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
and
W_{(k_{1})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.1
)—satisfying in particular
\Tr_{A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=W_{(k_{1})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{1}}^{O}}
. Now, according to Eq. (
139
), the validity constraints, up to normalisation, for
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{1}}^{IO}A_{k_{2}}^{I}})
(with a trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P}
) reduce to
{}_{[1-A_{k_{1}}^{O}]}\Tr_{A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=0
, which is indeed implied by
\Tr_{A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=W_{(k_{1})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{1}}^{O}}
. Hence, the
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
’s are valid process matrices up to normalisation, which implies that
W
is a QC-NICC, and therefore that
\text{{{QC-CC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
in the scenario under consideration. (Recall that it is enough here to check the validity for the
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
’s; in any case, the validity constraints for
W_{(k_{1})}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k_{1}}^{I}})
, with no
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, are trivially satisfied.)

•

Proof that
\text{{{QC-convFO}}}=\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
for
N\leq 3
:
Given the general inclusion
\text{{{QC-convFO}}}\subset\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
, it remains to prove the inclusion in the reverse direction; furthermore, it is enough to consider
N=3
, since any scenario with fewer parties can be considered as a 3-partite scenario where some party(-ies) have trivial input and output spaces.

Consider, therefore, the process matrix
W
of a tripartite (
N=3
) QC-NICC. It decomposes in terms of PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},F)}
,
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}
,
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
and
W_{(k_{1})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.1
), with all of these being valid process matrices (up to normalisation). Defining
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}^{\emptyset}\coloneqq W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}\geq 0
and
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3})}\coloneqq W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}\geq 0
(for
k_{3}\neq k_{1},k_{2}
), one can see that these (together with
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},F)}
) satisfy the first two lines of Eq. (
III.2.2
), as well as the third constraint thereof, for
n=2
. Furthermore, the validity of
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{k_{1}}^{IO}A_{k_{2}}^{I}})
implies (according to Eq. (
139
)) that
{}_{[1-A_{k_{1}}^{O}]}\Tr_{A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=0
, i.e.,
\Tr_{A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3})}=\frac{1}{d_{k_{1}}^{O}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{O}A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{1}}^{O}}
, so that by defining
W_{(k_{1})}^{(k_{2},k_{3})}\coloneqq\frac{1}{d_{k_{1}}^{O}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{O}A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3})}\geq 0
, the third constraint of Eq. (
III.2.2
) gets also satisfied for
n=1
. The validity of
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
also implies that
{}_{[1-P]}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{IO}A_{k_{2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=0
, i.e.,
\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1})}^{(k_{2},k_{3})}=(\frac{1}{d^{P}}\Tr W_{(k_{1})}^{(k_{2},k_{3})})\mathbbm{1}^{P}
, so that final constraint of Eq. (
III.2.2
) is also satisfied, with
q_{\pi=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}=\frac{1}{d^{P}}\Tr W_{(k_{1})}^{(k_{2},k_{3})}\geq 0
, such that
\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}=\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}\frac{1}{d^{P}}\Tr W_{(k_{1})}^{(k_{2},k_{3})}=\sum_{k_{1}}\frac{1}{d^{P}d_{k_{1}}^{O}}\sum_{k_{2}}\Tr W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=\frac{1}{d^{P}}\sum_{k_{1}}\Tr W_{(k_{1})}=1
as required (where we used
\sum_{k_{2}}\Tr W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}=\Tr\,(W_{(k_{1})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{1}}^{O}})
and
\sum_{k_{1}}\Tr W_{(k_{1})}=d^{P}
, which follow from Eq. (
III.2.1
)).

We thus obtained a decomposition of
W
that satisfies all constraints of Eq. (
III.2.2
), which shows that
W
is a QC-convFO, and therefore that
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-convFO}}}
, for the (at most) tripartite case considered here.

D.2.5
Explicit decompositions of our specific examples of QC-CCs

To close this section on QC-CCs, we now provide explicit decompositions of our examples of QC-CCs from Sec.
III.2.4
, in the appropriate form so as to match the characterisation of the class that they belong to.

Our first example,
W_{\textup{convFO}}=\frac{1}{2}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{B^{O}}+\frac{1}{2}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A^{O}}
from Eq. (
42
), is clearly of the QC-convFO form of Eq. (
III.2.2
), with

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}W_{(A,B)}^{\emptyset}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}},&&W_{(B,A)}^{\emptyset}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}},\\[5.69054pt]

\lx@intercol\hfil q_{(A,B)}\lx@intercol&=&\lx@intercol q_{(B,A)}\,=\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(and with
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},F)}=W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{\emptyset}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{2}}^{O}}
, since
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is trivial).

Our second example,
W_{\textup{NICC}}=\frac{1}{2}W_{+\alpha}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{C^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{C^{O}D^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D^{O}}+\frac{1}{2}W_{-\alpha}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{D^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{D^{O}C^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C^{O}}

’s are valid process matrices, the fact that they are causally nonseparable prevents one from obtaining a QC-convFO decomposition of
