(and indeed no such decomposition exists, as can be checked via SDP).

Our third example,
W_{\textup{CS}}={|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{B^{O}}+{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A^{O}}

), can be decomposed in the general QC-CC form of Eq. (

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}W_{(A,B)}&=&{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}},&&W_{(B,A)}&=&{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}},\\[5.69054pt]

(and once again with

, since

is trivial).
Notice that none of the matrices in the above decomposition are valid process matrices; indeed it can be shown via SDP that no decomposition of the form of Eq. (
III.2.1
) exists for
W_{\textup{CS}}
with only valid
W_{(k_{1}(,k_{2}))}
’s, which implies that
W_{\textup{CS}}
is not a QC-NICC.

D.3
Quantum circuits with quantum control of causal order (QC-QCs)

We now move to the more in-depth presentation of QC-QCs and of our new subclasses of QC-supFOs and QC-NIQC.

QC-QCs were introduced and characterised in Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
. The basic idea was to turn the classical control of QC-CCs into a quantum control, so as to allow for coherently controlled superpositions of different causal orders.

To describe such coherent superpositions, it is more convenient to work in the “pure” picture of quantum theory: we will deal here with pure quantum states described by ket vectors of the form
{|\psi\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}
(rather than with density matrices
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
), that evolve through pure linear operators of the form
V:\mathcal{H}^{X}\to\mathcal{H}^{Y}
(rather than through CP maps
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y})
). Correspondingly, we will use the “pure” version of the Choi–Jamiołkowski isomorphism and of the link product. Namely, for any given operator
V:\mathcal{H}^{X}\to\mathcal{H}^{Y}
, we define its Choi vector as the “double-ket” vector

\displaystyle{|V\rangle\!\rangle}^{XY}\coloneqq(\mathbbm{1}^{X}\otimes V){|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XX}=\sum_{i}{|i\rangle}^{X}\otimes V{|i\rangle}^{X}\ \in\mathcal{H}^{XY}

(179)

(with
\{{|i\rangle}^{X}\}_{i}
denoting the computational basis of
\mathcal{H}^{X}
,
{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XX}\coloneqq\sum_{i}{|i\rangle}^{X}\otimes{|i\rangle}^{X}
); and for two vectors
{|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{XY}
and
{|V_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{YZ}
that share a common space
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
, we define their pure link product as
Wechs et al. 2021

\displaystyle{|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XY}*{|V_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YZ}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\big(\mathbbm{1}^{XZ}\otimes{\langle\!\langle\mathbbm{1}|}^{YY}\big)\big({|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XY}\otimes{|V_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YZ}\big)=\big(({|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XY})^{T_{Y}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{Z}\big){|V_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YZ}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{XZ}.

(180)

The pure link product has the same commutativity and associativity properties as in the mixed state, and similarly reduces to a tensor product if
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
is trivial, or to a scalar product if
\mathcal{H}^{X}
and
\mathcal{H}^{Z}
are trivial.
As in the mixed case, when
{|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{XY}
and
{|V_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}^{YZ}
are the Choi vectors of two linear operators
V_{1}:\mathcal{H}^{X}\to\mathcal{H}^{Y}
and
V_{2}:\mathcal{H}^{Y}\to\mathcal{H}^{Z}
, then their link product is the Choi vector of their composition (or simply their product, here).

D.3.1
General QC-QCs

Construction of a QC-QC.

Figure 10:
Graphical representation of a QC-QC: a more detailed version than Fig.
5
(clarifying here the internal structure of the circuit operations
\tilde{V}_{n}
), reproduced from Fig. 10 of Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
.

The general construction of a QC-QC, depicted in Fig.
5
of the main text or with more details in Fig.
10
here, is quite similar to that of a QC-CC; we will simply highlight here the essential differences, referring again to Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
for more details.

As just mentioned, here we work in the “pure” picture, so we now describe both internal and external operations as linear operators
\tilde{V}_{1}:\mathcal{H}^{P}\to\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{1}^{I}\alpha_{1}C_{1}}
,
\tilde{V}_{n+1}:\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}}
(for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
),
\tilde{V}_{N+1}:\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{N}^{O}\alpha_{N}C_{N}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}^{F\alpha_{F}}
(instead of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n+1}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}})
etc.), and
A_{k}:\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
(instead of
\mathcal{A}_{k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}})
).
40
40

40

We again introduce “generic” input (respectively output) spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}}
) for the external operations to be applied at time-slot
t_{n}
, assuming that all their input (resp. output) spaces are isomorphic.

Notice also that we introduce an additional auxiliary system
\alpha_{F}
in the global future. This system will ultimately be traced out (see Fig.
5
) so as to still allow for some mixtures in the general definition of QC-QCs.

And we again allow for a slight conflict of notation with
A_{k}
. Here in the pure picture,
A_{k}:\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
(with Choi vector
{|A_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{IO}}
) can be understood as a Kraus operator of the operation applied by party
A_{k}
(rather than the Choi matrix of their CP map, as we wrote in the context of QC-CCs).

The structure of the control system is somewhat different here. While in the QC-CC case the (effectively classical) control states were keeping track of the whole order of the previous operations, for QC-QCs the control system at each time-slot
t_{n}
only encodes the unordered set
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
of the
n-1
previous operations, as well as the label
k_{n}(\notin\mathcal{K}_{n-1})
of the current operation. Thus, the (orthogonal) basis states of the control system take the form

\displaystyle{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}}

(181)

(instead of
[\![(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})]\!]^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}}\coloneqq{|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\rangle\!\langle(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})|}^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}}
as in Eq. (
141
)).
Such states control which external operation
A_{k_{n}}
is to be applied at time-slot
t_{n}
by embedding these into the larger controlled operation

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{n}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}\tilde{A}_{k_{n}}\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}|}^{C_{n}}:\ \mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{I}C_{n}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}C_{n}^{\prime}}

(182)

(instead of Eq. (
142
)).
They similarly control the internal operations through
41
41

41

We use the short-hand notations
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}=\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup\{k_{n}\}
,
\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}=\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{N}\}
(and, further below,
{\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n}={\cal K}_{n}\backslash\{k_{n}\}
).

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k_{1}}\tilde{V}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\otimes{|\emptyset,k_{1}\rangle}^{C_{1}}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{P}\to\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{1}^{I}\alpha_{1}C_{1}},

(183)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
k_{n},k_{n+1}\end{subarray}}\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1}}\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n},k_{n+1}\rangle}^{C_{n+1}}{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}|}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}:\hskip-14.22636pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}},

(184)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{N+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k_{N}}\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}}^{\to F}\otimes{\langle\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}|}^{C_{N}^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{N}^{O}\alpha_{N}C_{N}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}^{F\alpha_{F}}

(185)

(instead of Eqs. (
143
)–(
145
)), where the operators
\tilde{V}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}
,
\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and
\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}}^{\to F}
act on the target and auxiliary memory systems in an analogous (now coherently) controlled manner to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}^{\to F}
in the QC-CC case.
Notice in particular how the specific position of
k_{n}
gets erased in Eq. (
184
), by merging (in the control state)
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
and
k_{n}
into the unordered set
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}
; forgetting about the specific order of the past operations will allow different orders to interfere.

The state of all systems at any point in the circuit is simply obtained by composing the above internal and external operations—which is conveniently done in terms of their Choi vectors, using the pure link product. Inputting some initial state
{|\psi\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{P}
in the global past of the circuit, and defining (for any subset
\mathcal{K}_{n}
of
\mathcal{N}
) the vectors
{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}\coloneqq{|\psi\rangle}\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}{|A_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}^{IO}}
, the states
{|\varphi^{\prime}_{(n)}\rangle}
before and
{|\varphi_{(n+1)}\rangle}
after each operation
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
are found to be

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(0)}^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{P},

(186)

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|\tilde{V}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\big({|\psi\rangle}\otimes{|A_{k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{|A_{k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}\big)*\big({|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2}}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}^{O}_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 355.65944pt\otimes{|\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n},\,k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}^{O}_{n}}\big)\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}C_{n}^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for }n\geq 1

(187)

and

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(n+1)}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|\tilde{V}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{n+1}\rangle\!\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}\big({|\psi\rangle}\otimes{|A_{k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{|A_{k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}\big)*\big({|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}^{I}_{n+1}}\big)\otimes{|\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\},k_{n+1}\rangle}^{C_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n},\,k_{n+1}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n}}\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}^{I}_{n+1}}\big)\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1}\rangle}^{C_{n+1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\in\mathcal{H}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}C_{n+1}}\quad\text{ for }n\leq N-1,

(188)

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{F\alpha_{F}}

(189)

(instead of Eqs. (
150
)–(
153
)), with

\displaystyle{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\rangle}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}{|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2}}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}\alpha_{n}},

(190)

\displaystyle{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}{|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2}}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-2}\},k_{N-1}}^{\to k_{N}}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\},k_{N}}^{\to F}\rangle\!\rangle}\quad\in\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F\alpha_{F}}.

(191)

Trace-preserving conditions.

The TP conditions correspond here to the requirement that all internal operations
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
preserve the norm of their input state
{|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}
: they must act as isometries on their effective input space.
The squared norms of
{|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}
and
{|\varphi_{(n+1)}\rangle}
(for
1\leq n\leq N-1
) can be obtained from Eqs. (
187
)–(
188
) above as

\displaystyle\innerproduct*{\varphi_{(n)}'}{\varphi_{(n)}'}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n},\,k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr[{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}|}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr[\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}\big)^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big)\Big],

(192)

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\varphi_{(n+1)}}{\varphi_{(n+1)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n},\,k_{n+1}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr[{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}|}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr[\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}\big)^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n+1}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}|}\Big)\Big].

(193)

Equality between these two expressions, for any possible choice of
{|\psi\rangle}
and of the
A_{k}
’s, implies that the last terms in round brackets must themselves be equal, for any
\mathcal{K}_{n}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n+1}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}|}=\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}.

(194)

For the extremal cases
n=0
and
n=N
, the corresponding constraints are

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathcal{N}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}\alpha_{1}}{|w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}|}=\mathbbm{1}^{P}

(195)

and

\displaystyle\Tr_{F\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}=\sum_{k_{N}\in\mathcal{N}}\Tr_{\alpha_{N}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}.

(196)

Process matrix description and characterisation.

From the expression for
{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle}
in Eq. (
189
), one gets the (in general, mixed) global output state of the whole QC-QC, after tracing out
\alpha_{F}
:

\displaystyle\varrho_{N+1}=\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{(N+1)}|}={|\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}|}*\big(\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}\big)\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F}).

(197)

With this, one can readily identify the process matrix that describes the QC-QC under consideration (as we did in the QC-CC case), namely

\displaystyle W=\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}),

(198)

with
{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}
defined in Eq. (
191
) above, as a coherent superposition of terms corresponding to different causal orders.

Defining, for all
n=1,\ldots,N
, all strict subsets
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
of
\mathcal{N}
and all
k_{n}\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
,

\displaystyle W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}|}\quad\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}}),

(199)

it then follows directly from Eq. (
198
) and the TP conditions of Eqs. (
194
)–(
196
) that the process matrix
W\,(\geq 0)
of a QC-QC has a characterisation as in Eq. (
III.3.1
).

Conversely, from any decomposition of a process matrix
W
in terms of PSD matrices
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
satisfying Eq. (
III.3.1
), one can explicitly reconstruct (in a non-unique manner) the internal operations
V_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and
V_{\mathcal{N}\setminus k_{N},k_{N}}^{\to F}
of a QC-QC (satisfying the required TP conditions), whose composition reproduces
{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}
and, subsequently,
W
as in Eqs. (
191
) and (
198
) above.
This shows that Eq. (
III.3.1
) is also a sufficient condition for
W
to be a QC-QC. An explicit procedure for such a reconstruction was detailed in Appendix B of
Wechs et al. 2021
.

D.3.2
Quantum circuits with superposition of fixed causal orders (QC-supFO)

As succinctly introduced in the main text, we define the class of quantum circuits with superposition of fixed causal orders as a modification of the general QC-QC construction above, in which the control system at any time-slot
t_{n}
now encodes (in addition to the past and current operations) the full causal order
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})
of the upcoming external operations that are still to be applied. The weight of each total order
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
is fixed from the very beginning to some value
q_{\pi}
(such that
q_{\pi}\geq 0
,
\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}=1
). When updating the control system, the internal operations may again erase the order of previous operations, but they should not modify the weight of each remaining “future” order
(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})
.

The construction, the derivation of the TP conditions, the process matrix description and the characterisation of QC-supFOs align quite closely to the case of general QC-QCs recalled above. We will just point out the differences.

Construction of a QC-supFO.

As just said, the control system should now also encode the order of future operations; we thus take its (orthogonal) basis states to be of the form

\displaystyle{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}},

(200)

with
k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N}\not\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
, instead of just
{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}^{(\prime)}}
as in Eq. (
181
).

These states control the external and internal operations through

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{n}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
(k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\end{subarray}}\tilde{A}_{k_{n}}\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})|}^{C_{n}}

(201)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{V}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes{|\emptyset,k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{1}},

(202)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
(k_{n},k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n},k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n+1}}{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n};(k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})|}^{C_{n}^{\prime}},

(203)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{N+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ \sum_{k_{N}}\ \tilde{V}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}}^{\to F}\otimes{\langle\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N};\emptyset|}^{C_{N}^{\prime}},

(204)

instead of Eqs. (
182
)–(
185
) for the general QC-QC case. Notice that the operators
\tilde{V}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}
and
\tilde{V}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}
that act on the target and auxiliary memory systems can depend in general on the order of future operations, as encoded in the system
C_{n(+1)}^{(\prime)}
that controls them.

Composing the above internal and external operations, we obtain the state of all systems before and after each operation
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
:

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(0)}^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle},

(205)

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|\tilde{V}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\big({|\psi\rangle}\otimes{|A_{k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{|A_{k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt*\big({|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2};(k_{3},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}^{O}_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 284.52756pt\otimes{|\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n},\,k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n},\\
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{n}\end{subarray}}\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}\tilde{A}^{O}_{n}}\big)\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}

(206)

and

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(n+1)}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|\tilde{V}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{A}_{n}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\tilde{V}_{n+1}\rangle\!\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\big({|\psi\rangle}\otimes{|A_{k_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{|A_{k_{n}}\rangle\!\rangle}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt*\big({|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}^{I}_{n+1}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 270.30118pt\otimes{|\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\},k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n},\\
(k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{n}\end{subarray}}\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}*{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}\tilde{A}^{I}_{n+1}}\big)\otimes{|\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{n+1}},

(207)

\displaystyle{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle}

\displaystyle={|\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}

(208)

with

\displaystyle{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}{|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2};(k_{3},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1}}^{\to k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle},

(209)

\displaystyle{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}{|V_{\emptyset,\emptyset}^{\to k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\emptyset,k_{1}}^{\to k_{2};(k_{3},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\rangle}*\cdots*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-2}\},k_{N-1}}^{\to k_{N};\emptyset}\rangle\!\rangle}*{|V_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\},k_{N}}^{\to F}\rangle\!\rangle}

(210)

instead of Eqs. (
186
)–(
191
).

Trace-preserving conditions.

As specified above, the weight of each total order
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
of all parties, which is encoded in the state
{|\emptyset,k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})\rangle}^{C_{1}}
of the control system right after
\tilde{V}_{1}
, is fixed to some predefined value
q_{\pi}
. This weight—which can be interpreted as the probability that the control system is found in the state corresponding to that order (if it were measured in the appropriate basis)—can be obtained from the expression of
\varphi_{(1)}
in Eq. (
207
):

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\mathbbm{1}^{\tilde{A}_{1}^{I}\alpha_{1}}\otimes{\langle\emptyset,k_{1};(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})|}^{C_{1}}\big){|\varphi_{(1)}\rangle}\Big|^{2}=\Big|{|\psi\rangle}*{|w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\Tr[\big({|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}\big)^{T}\Big(\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}\alpha_{1}}{|w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}|}\Big)\Big]=q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}.

(211)

Imposing that this equality holds for all possible (normalised) input states
{|\psi\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{P}
requires that

\displaystyle\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}\alpha_{1}}{|w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}|}=q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\,\mathbbm{1}^{P},

(212)

instead of the TP constraint of Eq. (
195
) in the general QC-QC case.

Additionally, we impose that the internal operations
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
preserve the weight of each future order
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})
.
The total weight attached to that order before
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
is obtained as the sum over
k_{n}
of the weights corresponding to
(k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})
in the state
{|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}
. Namely, for
\mathcal{K}_{n}=\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N}\}
(using Eq. (
206
)):

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Big|\big(\mathbbm{1}^{\tilde{A}_{n}^{O}\alpha_{n}}\otimes{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n};(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})|}^{C_{n}^{\prime}}\big){|\varphi_{(n)}^{\prime}\rangle}\Big|^{2}=\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Big|{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\Tr[\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}\big)^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big)\Big].

(213)

The weight of
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})
after
\tilde{V}_{n+1}
on the other hand is simply (using Eq. (
207
))

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\mathbbm{1}^{\tilde{A}_{n+1}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}\otimes{\langle\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1};(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})|}^{C_{n+1}}\big){|\varphi_{(n+1)}\rangle}\Big|^{2}=\Big|{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\Tr[\big({|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}|}\big)^{T}\Big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}|}\Big)\Big].

(214)

Imposing that these two expressions are equal, for any
{|\psi\rangle}
and any
A_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}
, implies that one must have (for any
\mathcal{K}_{n}
, any
k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n}
)

\displaystyle\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}\alpha_{n+1}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}|}=\sum_{k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}}\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}},

(215)

instead of Eq. (
194
).
For the final case of
n=N
, the corresponding constraint is

\displaystyle\Tr_{F\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}=\sum_{k_{N}\in\mathcal{N}}\Tr_{\alpha_{N}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}|}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}.

(216)

instead of Eq. (
196
).

Process matrix description and characterisation.

As in the general QC-QC case, from the expression for
{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle}
in Eq. (
208
), one gets the global output state of the whole QC-supFO, after tracing out
\alpha_{F}
:

\displaystyle\varrho_{N+1}=\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|\varphi_{(N+1)}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{(N+1)}|}={|\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}\rangle\!\langle\psi,A_{\mathcal{N}}|}*\big(\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}\big)\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F}).

(217)

One can then identify the process matrix that describes the QC-supFO under consideration as

\displaystyle W=\Tr_{\alpha_{F}}{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}|}\quad\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F}),

(218)

with
{|w_{(\mathcal{N},F)}\rangle}
defined in Eq. (
210
) above.

Defining, for all
n=1,\ldots,N
, all
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}
and all
(k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\notin\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
,

\displaystyle W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}|}\quad\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}}),

(219)

then it follows directly from Eq. (
218
) and the TP conditions of Eqs. (
212
), (
215
) and (
216
) that the process matrix
W\,(\geq 0)
of a QC-supFO has a characterisation as in Eq. (
III.3.2
).
Notice that this characterisation implies in particular that the
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
’s are valid process matrices, up to normalisation—as it was the case for the
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
’s in the decomposition of a QC-convFO (cf Footnote
37
).

Conversely, the procedure of Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
that allows one to reconstruct the internal operations
V_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}^{\to k_{n+1}}
and
V_{\mathcal{N}\setminus k_{N},k_{N}}^{\to F}
of a QC-QC
from its characterisation as in Eq. (
III.3.1
) can be adapted here, implying that Eq. (
III.3.2
) is also a sufficient condition for
W
to be a QC-supFO.

Another perspective:
QC-supFO
as a recursive generalisation of the
Sup
class from Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
.

Another subclass of QC-QCs was introduced in Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
, which could also have been seen as a potential candidate to define quantum circuits with superpositions of fixed orders—it was indeed simply called
Sup
by the authors of
Liu et al. 2023
. We will argue however that its definition has an undesirable feature that should rule it out as such a meaningful definition of a relevant class of circuits, and that by generalising the
Sup
class so as to avoid this unwanted feature, we naturally recover our
QC-supFO
class.

Formally, the
Sup
class was defined as follows:
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})
is in
Sup
iff
W\geq 0
, and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}\,\tilde{W}_{\pi},\\[5.69054pt]
\forall\,\pi,\tilde{W}_{\pi}\in\text{{{QC-FO}}}_{\pi},\ q_{\pi}\geq 0,\,\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}=1.\end{array}\right.

where
\text{{{QC-FO}}}_{\pi}
is the class of quantum circuits with fixed causal order
\pi
, i.e., for
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
, of PSD matrices
W
that admit a decomposition as in Eq. (
D.2.2
).
This class was meant to generalise the “static” quantum switch, in which the control system is initially prepared in some predefined quantum state corresponding to a superposition of fixed orders and ultimately sent to
\mathcal{H}^{F}
in the global future, so that tracing
\mathcal{H}^{F}
out leaves the rest of the parties in an incoherent mixture of processes with fixed causal order.

The unwanted feature we identify in the definition of
Sup
above is that it crucially depends on the labelling of
\mathcal{H}^{F}
. More specifically, recall first that considering the future space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
as “open” or attributing it to an additional party, with no output space, leads to formally equivalent frameworks
Araújo et al. 2017
;
Wechs et al. 2021
.
Now, if one were to rename
\mathcal{H}^{F}
and consider it as the input space of a new party, treated on equal footing with the other ones, then the definition of the

Sup
class above would change.

Concretely, it can be seen for example that the process matrix of the “static” quantum switch,
W_{\textup{supFO}}
from Eq. (
57
), is in
Sup
. However, if one were to attribute
\mathcal{H}^{F}
—or even just
\mathcal{H}^{F_{\text{c}}}
—to another party (call it
C
) and write
W_{\textup{supFO}}={|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{supFO}}|}
with
{|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{|\psi\rangle}^{A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|0\rangle}^{C_{I}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{|\psi\rangle}^{B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|1\rangle}^{C_{I}}
instead of Eq. (
57
), then one would conclude that
W_{\textup{supFO}}\notin\text{{{Sup}}}
—even though the situation is physically exactly the same as the one in which one concludes that
W_{\textup{supFO}}\in\text{{{Sup}}}
.

This observation leads us to argue that any physically meaningful definition of a class of quantum circuits should not depend on the labellings of the different parties, including (crucially) parties in the global past or future.
42
42

42

Unless the class one wishes to define refers explicitly to a certain labelling of the parties. E.g. the class
\text{{{QC-FO}}}_{\pi=(A,B)}
of quantum circuits with the fixed causal order
(A,B)
is of course different from the class
\text{{{QC-FO}}}_{\pi=(B,A)}
obtained by swapping the two parties’ labels.

In particular, if two parties have no output space, the class should not depend on whose input space if called
\mathcal{H}^{F}
; similarly if two parties have no input space, the class should not depend on whose output space if called
\mathcal{H}^{P}
.
One can verify that all our classes satisfy this requirement.
43
43

43

Except for
\text{{{QC-FO}}}_{\pi}
, see the previous footnote.

We will now see how one can modify the
Sup
class so as to also satisfy it.

For this, recall that the defining property of
Sup
is that if one traces out
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, one should be left with an incoherent mixture of fixed causal orders. If one attributes
\mathcal{H}^{F}
to another party, we would hence like that there is different party in the future, such that if one traces them out, the rest should be in an incoherent mixture of fixed orders.

The key insight we can take from the example of
W_{\textup{supFO}}
just discussed is that in order to conclude that
W_{\textup{supFO}}\in\text{{{Sup}}}
, it may not be enough to just trace one future party out; after tracing out one party, one may need to check whether one is left in a situation where another party can be considered to be in the future of the remaining ones, such that tracing it out leaves the other ones in an incoherent mixture of orders—or more generally, whether one is left in an (incoherent) mixture of such situations, with fixed weights. This suggests considering a recursive generalisation of the
Sup
class that would formalise this.

As it turns out, following this idea provides an alternative characterisation of our class
QC-supFO
. More formally: let us add subscripts and write
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
to specify the global past Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, the set of parties
\mathcal{N}
, and the global future Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
considered in the definition of
QC-supFO
. We show below that
\textup{{QC-supFO}}=\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
has the following recursive characterisation, which formalises precisely our suggestion above for generalising the
Sup
class:

\displaystyle\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}

\displaystyle=\left\{W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{c}W\geq 0,\,\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{k_{N}\in\mathcal{N}}\,q^{[k_{N}]}\,\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}},\\[5.69054pt]
q^{[k_{N}]}\geq 0,\ \sum_{k_{N}}q^{[k_{N}]}=1,\\[5.69054pt]
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},A_{k_{N}}^{I}}\end{array}\right\}\quad\text{for }|\mathcal{N}|\geq 1,

\displaystyle\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\emptyset,F}

\displaystyle=\Big\{W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PF})\,\Big|\,W\geq 0,\,\Tr_{F}W=\mathbbm{1}^{P}\Big\}.

(225)

This characterisation could also be taken as a definition of our class
QC-supFO
, which can therefore be seen, from this perspective, as a natural generalisation of the
Sup
class of Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
.
It can indeed be checked that if two parties
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
have no output Hilbert spaces, then
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\cup F_{1},F_{2}}=\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\cup F_{2},F_{1}}

: the above characterisation does not depend on which of
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
is labelled
F
, thus indeed resolving the issue with the
Sup
class that we pinpointed above.

Proof of Eq. (
225
):

Let us denote by
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}^{\prime}
the RHS of the first equation in (
225
) (adding the prime recursively, to
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},A_{k_{N}}^{I}}
and
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\emptyset,F}
as well)—so that our task here is to prove that
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}=\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}^{\prime}
, with
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
defined by Eq. (
III.3.2
).

•

For
|\mathcal{N}|=N=1
(
\mathcal{N}=\{A\}
), it is easy to see that the set
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\{A\},F}^{\prime}=\{W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA^{IO}F})|W\geq 0,\Tr_{F}W=\tilde{W}^{[A]}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A^{O}},\Tr_{A_{I}}\tilde{W}^{[A]}=\mathbbm{1}^{P}\}
coincides with
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\{A\},F}
as characterised by Eq. (
III.3.2
). (These in fact also coincide with the set of all valid process matrices, as characterised through Eq. (
139
).)

•

Assume that
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}^{\prime},F}=\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}^{\prime},F}^{\prime}
for all
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
such that
|\mathcal{N}^{\prime}|=N-1\geq 1
.

Consider then
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}^{\prime},F}^{\prime}
; by definition
\Tr_{F}W
can be decomposed as a convex mixture

\displaystyle\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{k_{N}\in\mathcal{N}}q^{[k_{N}]}\,\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}

(226)

with each
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},A_{k_{N}}^{I}}
, which therefore has a decomposition in terms of PSD matrices
\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
as in Eq. (
III.3.2
):

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Tr_{A_{k_{N}}^{I}}\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}=\sum_{k_{N-1}\in\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}\tilde{W}_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\},k_{N-1})}^{\emptyset;[k_{N}]}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N-1}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq{\cal K}_{n}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},\,\forall\,(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1})\in\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}\backslash{\cal K}_{n},\\[2.84526pt]
\qquad\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}=\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash\{k_{n}\},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,\pi^{\prime}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})\in{\cal N}\backslash k_{N},\ \Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}\tilde{W}_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}=q_{\pi^{\prime}}^{[k_{N}]}\,\mathbbm{1}^{P},\ q_{\pi^{\prime}}^{[k_{N}]}\geq 0,\,\sum_{\pi^{\prime}}q_{\pi^{\prime}}^{[k_{N}]}=1.\end{array}\right.

Defining
W_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N}\},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}\coloneqq q^{[k_{N}]}\,\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}
,
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1},k_{N})}\coloneqq q^{[k_{N}]}\,\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}
and
q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1},k_{N})}\coloneqq q^{[k_{N}]}\,q_{\pi^{\prime}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{[k_{N}]}
, one can see that Eqs. (
226
) and (
•
‣
D.3.2
) together provide a decomposition of
W
as in Eq. (
III.3.2
), which proves that
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
.

Conversely, consider
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
, with a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.3.2
).
Defining, for each
k_{N}
,
q^{[k_{N}]}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})\in\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1},k_{N})}
and
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\coloneqq W_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N}\},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}/q^{[k_{N}]}
(when
q^{[k_{N}]}>0
; otherwise,
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\coloneqq 0
), then one obtains a decomposition of
\Tr_{F}W
as in Eq. (
226
) above, with
q^{[k_{N}]}\geq 0
,
\sum_{k_{N}}q^{[k_{N}]}=1
, and one can further see that each
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}
admits itself a decomposition of the form of Eq. (
III.3.2
), in terms of PSD matrices
\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1});[k_{N}]}\coloneqq W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N-1},k_{N})}/q^{[k_{N}]}
and with weights
q_{\pi^{\prime}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1})}^{[k_{N}]}\coloneqq q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1},k_{N})}/q^{[k_{N}]}\,
in the final constraint. This implies that each
\tilde{W}^{[k_{N}]}\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},F}
, and hence that
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}^{\prime}
.

We thus have that
\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}^{\prime}=\textup{{QC-supFO}}_{P,\mathcal{N},F}
. This, by recursion, concludes the proof of Eq. (
225
).

D.3.3
Quantum circuits with non-influenceable quantum control of causal order (QC-NIQCs)

The last class we introduced is that of QC-NIQCs, defined in Sec.
III.3.3
of the main text as follows:

QC-NIQCs are the QC-QCs for which at any intermediate time step, the classical weights of each term in the basis
\{{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}}\}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}
that defines the controllisation—i.e. the diagonal elements of the state of the control system in that basis—are independent of the choice of previously applied external operations and of the potential initial state preparation in the global past.

(with, as for QC-NICCs, the external operations referring to the parties’ whole instruments rather than the individual CP maps that compose them, and the state of the control system being considered after summing over all these individual CP maps—and over all their Kraus operators when one starts from a description in the “pure” picture).

The state
{|\varphi_{(n)}\rangle}
of all systems at any intermediate time step
t_{n}
(for
n=1,\ldots,N
, just before
44
44

44

Considering the case where the external operations are TP, the reduced state of the control system after the operator
\tilde{A}_{n}
(and after summing over the corresponding Kraus operators) will be the same; as in Eq. (
166
) (see also Footnote
38
).

applying the operator
\tilde{A}_{n}
) is given by Eq. (
188
). From this, the weight of each component
{|\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}\rangle}^{C_{n}}
of the control system is obtained as

\displaystyle\Big\|\,{|\psi,A_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\rangle}*{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\rangle}\,\Big\|^{2}=\Tr[\Big({|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\mathinner{|{A_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{A_{k}}|}\Big)*W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\Big],

(231)

with
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}=\Tr_{\alpha_{n}}{|w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\rangle\!\langle w_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}|}
as defined in Eq. (
199
).
Summing over the Kraus operators
A_{k}
so as to obtain a CPTP map for each party—whose Choi matrix, abusing the notation slightly, we still denote by
A_{k}
(see Footnote
40
)—the requirement expressed above translates into the constraint that for all
\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}
,

\displaystyle\Tr[\Big({|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}A_{k}\Big)*W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\Big]

does not depend on
\rho
nor on the
A_{k}
’s,

\displaystyle\quad\text{ whenever all }A_{k}\text{'s are TP (and ${|\psi\rangle}$ is normalised)}.

(232)

Recalling the discussion at the end of Sec.
D.1
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\text{all}\ W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\text{'s are valid process matrices (up to normalisation)},

(233)

as in Eq. (
III.3.3
).

Conversely, as recalled above, from the characterisation of a QC-QC as in Eq. (
III.3.1
), one can reconstruct internal operations that realise it; one can verify that the additional validity constraint above implies indeed that these internal operations are such that the diagonal elements of the state of the control system are independent of the external TP operations—implying that the QC-QC under consideration is then a QC-NIQC.

D.3.4
Inclusion relations

Let us start here by noting that each of the three classes of quantum circuits with quantum control considered above contains the corresponding class with classical control as a subclass:
\text{{{QC-convFO}}}\subset\text{{{QC-supFO}}}
,
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}
and
\text{{{QC-CC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-QC}}}
.
In the latter case for instance, this can be seen by starting from a QC-CC decomposition in terms of PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
and
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
as in Eq. (
III.2.1
), and defining
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
, which indeed readily provide a QC-QC decomposition as in Eq. (
III.3.1
). For
\text{{{QC-convFO}}}\subset\text{{{QC-supFO}}}
, a similar construction works, after just adding the appropriate superscripts
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})
. For
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}\subset\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}
, we further note that if
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
’s are valid process matrices (up to normalisation), then so are the
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
’s just defined.

Quite trivially, for
N=1
all six classes reduce to the same one—which is also the whole class of all valid process matrices. For
N=2
, with a nontrivial global future space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, one already obtains a strict separation between the three QC-QC classes and their QC-CC counterparts: indeed the “static quantum switch”,
W_{\textup{supFO}}
from Eq. (
57
), provides in that scenario an example in
QC-supFO
(hence also in
QC-NIQC
and
QC-QC
, see below) but not in
QC-CC
(nor in any of its subclasses).
If
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is trivial on the other hand, one can easily see that for
N=2
each of the three QC-QC classes reduces to its QC-CC counterpart; one then needs to go to
N\geq 3
to see a strict separation.

Let us now compare the three (sub)classes of QC-QCs.

It is again quite clear, from the definition of the
QC-NIQC
class just above, that
\textup{{QC-NIQC}}\subset\textup{{QC-QC}}
. To see that
\textup{{QC-supFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-NIQC}}
, consider a process matrix
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}
, with a decomposition in terms of PSD matrices (and even valid process matrices, up to normalisation)
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
as in Eq. (
III.3.2
); defining, for each
(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})
,
W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, one can easily see that these satisfy the QC-QC decomposition of Eq. (
III.3.1
), and that these are themselves valid process matrices (up to normalisation), which proves that
W\in\textup{{QC-NIQC}}
.
Hence, we obtain the general inclusion relations of Eq. (
56
):

\displaystyle\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\ \subset\ \text{{{QC-QC}}}.

(234)

For
N=2
already, with a nontrivial global past space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
, one finds a strict separation between
QC-NIQC
and
QC-QC
. An example of a process matrix in
\text{{{QC-QC}}}\backslash\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}
was given by the quantum switch
W_{\textup{QS}}
, Eq. (
61
) of the main text (for
N=2
indeed, with a nontrivial space
\mathcal{H}^{P}
but also a nontrivial space
\mathcal{H}^{F}
to avoid falling back into the
QC-CC
class; see above). If
\mathcal{H}^{P}
is trivial on the other hand,
QC-NIQC
and
QC-QC
coincide for
N=2
(the proof being quite similar to that for
\text{{{QC-NICC}}}=\text{{{QC-CC}}}
in the same scenario, see p.
•
‣
D.2.4
), and one needs to go to
N\geq 3
to see a strict separation.

As for
QC-supFO
vs
QC-NIQC
, these can be seen to coincide for
N\leq 3
(again, the proof is quite similar to that for
\text{{{QC-convFO}}}=\text{{{QC-NICC}}}
in the same
N\leq 3
case, on p.
•
‣
D.2.4
). A strict separation can be found for
N\geq 4
: an example of a process matrix in
\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\backslash\text{{{QC-supFO}}}
(which was also not in
QC-CC
), for
N=4
with trivial global past and future spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
, was given by
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
from Eq. (
43
).

D.3.5
Explicit decompositions of our specific examples of QC-QCs

We finish this section on QC-QCs by providing explicit decompositions of our examples of QC-QCs from Sec.
III.3.4
, in the appropriate form so as to match the characterisation of the class that they belong to.

The process matrix
W_{\textup{supFO}}={|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{supFO}}|}
, with
{|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{|\psi\rangle}^{A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|0\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{|\psi\rangle}^{B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|1\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}
of the “static”, or “non-dynamical” quantum switch, Eq. (
57
), can be decomposed in the QC-supFO form of Eq. (
III.3.2
), with

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}W_{(\{A\},B)}^{\emptyset}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}},&&W_{(\{B\},A)}^{\emptyset}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}},\\[5.69054pt]
W_{(\emptyset,A)}^{(B)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}},&&W_{(\emptyset,B)}^{(A)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}},\\[5.69054pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil q_{(A,B)}\lx@intercol&=&\lx@intercol q_{(B,A)}\,=\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

Notice that this is (up to very slightly different notations) the same decomposition as that given for
W_{\textup{convFO}}
in Eq. (
D.2.5
). This is because
\Tr_{F}W_{\textup{supFO}}=W_{\textup{convFO}}
, and because the QC-supFO constraints for
\Tr_{F}W_{\textup{supFO}}
reduce (in the
N=2
case, with no more
\mathcal{H}^{F}
) to the QC-convFO constraints. Besides, it is also well-known—and easy to see—that
W_{\textup{supFO}}
is not a QC-CC (noting that
W_{\textup{supFO}}={|w_{\textup{supFO}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{supFO}}|}
is rank-1, so that any decomposition of
W_{\textup{supFO}}
directly into PSD matrices can only contain terms that are proportional to itself).

Our example of a QC-NIQC,
W_{\textup{NIQC}}=\frac{1}{2}W_{0}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{C^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{C^{O}D^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D^{O}}+\frac{1}{2}W_{1}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{D^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{D^{O}C^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C^{O}}
from Eq. (
60
),
with
W_{0}
and
W_{1}
two (valid) process matrices defined in Eq. (
59
) and satisfying
{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{0}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{1}=\frac{1}{8}(2\,\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 4}+2\,Z\mathbbm{1}Z\mathbbm{1}+XZX\mathbbm{1}-YZY\mathbbm{1}+X\mathbbm{1}XZ-Y\mathbbm{1}YZ)^{A^{IO}B^{IO}}
,
admits a QC-NIQC decomposition of the form of Eq. (
III.3.1
), with the valid process matrices (up to normalisation, as required the QC-NIQC condition of Eq. (
III.3.3
))

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}W_{(\{A,B,C\},D)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{0}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{C^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{C^{O}D^{I}},&W_{(\{A,B,D\},C)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{1}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{D^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{D^{O}C^{I}},\\[8.53581pt]
W_{(\{A,B\},C)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{0}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{C^{I}},&W_{(\{A,B\},D)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}W_{1}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{D^{I}},\\[8.53581pt]
W_{(\{A\},B)}&=&\frac{1}{8}(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 3}+Z\mathbbm{1}Z+XZX-YZY)^{A^{IO}B^{I}},&W_{(\{B\},A)}&=&\frac{1}{8}(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 3}+ZZ\mathbbm{1}+XXZ-YYZ)^{A^{I}B^{IO}},\\[8.53581pt]
W_{(\emptyset,A)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathbbm{1}^{A^{I}},&W_{(\emptyset,B)}&=&{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathbbm{1}^{B^{I}},\end{array}\right.

and all other
W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}=0
.

Finally, the process matrix
W_{\textup{QS}}={|w_{\textup{QS}}\rangle\!\langle w_{\textup{QS}}|}
, with
{|w_{\textup{QS}}\rangle}={|0\rangle}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle}^{A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|0\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}+{|1\rangle}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle}^{B^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{B^{O}A^{I}}\otimes{|\mathbbm{1}\rangle\!\rangle}^{A^{O}F_{\text{t}}}\otimes{|1\rangle}^{F_{\text{c}}}
of the “dynamical” quantum switch, Eq. (
61
), can be decomposed in the general QC-QC form of Eq. (
III.3.1
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}W_{(\{A\},B)}&=&{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{A^{O}B^{I}},&&W_{(\{B\},A)}&=&{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{B^{O}A^{I}},\\[5.69054pt]
W_{(\emptyset,A)}&=&{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{A^{I}},&&W_{(\emptyset,B)}&=&{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}^{P}\otimes{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|}^{B^{I}}.\end{array}\right.

Notice that this is again essentially the same decomposition as that given for the process matrix of the classical switch
W_{\textup{CS}}
in Eq. (
D.2.5
). This is because
\Tr_{F}W_{\textup{QS}}=W_{\textup{CS}}
, and because the QC-QC constraints for
\Tr_{F}W_{\textup{QS}}
reduce (in the
N=2
case, with no more
\mathcal{H}^{F}
) to the QC-CC constraints.
As in the case of
W_{\textup{CS}}
, none of the matrices in the above decomposition are valid process matrices; indeed it can be shown via SDP that no decomposition of the form of Eq. (
III.3.1
) exists for
W_{\textup{QS}}
with only valid
W_{(\{k_{1}\},k_{2})}
’s and
W_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}
’s, which implies that
W_{\textup{QS}}
is not a QC-NIQC.
Besides, it is also well-known—and again easy to see—that
W_{\textup{QS}}
is not a QC-CC (e.g., by contradiction: otherwise, feeding the state
{|+\rangle}=\frac{{|0\rangle}+{|1\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}
into
\mathcal{H}^{P}
would otherwise imply that
W_{\textup{supFO}}
is a QC-CC).

D.4
A graphical representation of the SDP constraints for our various classes of circuits

Looking at the SDP constraints that characterise each of the classes we considered in this work (see Eqs. (
III.2.1
), (
III.2.2
), (
III.3.1
), and (
III.3.2
)), one may notice some similar structure in the different decompositions.
Here we propose a graphical representation of these constraints that exploits their common features and helps visualise the structure of the different classes.

Each of the decompositions under consideration involves PSD matrices in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
(for some subset
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}
and some
k_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
) and in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}F})
, as well as the identity matrix
\mathbbm{1}^{P}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})
. The idea of the representation we propose is to organise these PSD matrices in a graph, defined as follows.

•

The nodes are the various PSD matrices that appear in the decomposition. These are organised in
N+2
layers: matrices in some
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
appear on the
n^{\text{th}}
layer, for each
n=1,\ldots,N
(from the bottom up), matrices in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}F})
appear on the
(n=N+1)^{\text{th}}
(top) layer, while the
\mathbbm{1}
’s (i.e. the
\mathbbm{1}^{P}
’s; we will drop the superscript
P
) appear on the
(n=0)^{\text{th}}
(bottom) layer.

•

For any SDP constraint of the general form
\big(\sum_{k_{n+1}}\big)\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(\cdots,k_{n+1})}=\big(\sum_{k_{n}}\big)W_{(\cdots,k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}
, with
1\leq n\leq N-1
, all matrices
W_{(\cdots,k_{n+1})}
appearing on the LHS of the equality (and on the
(n+1)^{\text{th}}
layer in the graph) are connected to all matrices
W_{(\cdots,k_{n})}
appearing on the RHS of the equality (and on the
n^{\text{th}}
layer in the graph).

•

For any SDP constraint of the form
\Tr_{F}W_{(\cdots)}=\big(\sum_{k_{N}}\big)W_{(\cdots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}
, for some
W_{(\cdots)}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}F})
, the matrix
W_{(\cdots)}
(that appears on the top layer in the graph) is connected to all matrices
W_{(\cdots,k_{N})}
appearing on the RHS of the equality (and on the
N^{\text{th}}
layer in the graph).

•

For any SDP constraint of the form
\big(\sum_{k_{1}}\big)\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}W_{(\cdots,k_{1})}=q_{(\cdots)}\mathbbm{1}^{P}
(possibly with
q_{(\cdots)}=1
), all matrices
W_{(\cdots,k_{1})}
appearing on the LHS of the equality (and on the
(n=1)^{\text{th}}
layer in the graph) are connected to
q_{(\cdots)}\mathbbm{1}
(that appears on the bottom layer in the graph).

For concreteness, let us start by illustrating this for the
QC-CC
class, characterised in Eq. (
III.2.1
). From the bottom to the top: the matrix
\mathbbm{1}
(
=\mathbbm{1}^{P}
, on the bottom layer) is connected to all
W_{(k_{1})}
’s (on the
(n=1)^{\text{th}}
layer); then each
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
(for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
, on the
n^{\text{th}}
layer) is connected to all
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}
’s (with the same first labels
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, on the
(n+1)^{\text{th}}
layer); finally, each
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}
(on the
N^{\text{th}}
layer) is connected to
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}
(on the top layer). We thus obtain a branch-graph structure, as illustrated in Fig.
11
for the
N=3
case.

Figure 11:
Graphical representation of the SDP constraints of Eq. (
III.2.1
), for the
QC-CC
class with
N=3
. For the
QC-NICC
class, all matrices that appear in the graph must be valid process matrices (up to normalisation), see Eq. (
III.2.3
).

Following the above construction, we can similarly build the graphs that represent the SDP constraints for our other classes: these are shown in Fig.
12
for the
QC-convFO
class (with
N=3
), in Fig.
13
for the
QC-QC
class (with
N=3
and
4
), and in Fig.
14
for the
QC-supFO
class (with
N=3
). These representations help visualise certain features of each class: e.g., that the
QC-convFO
class involves independent orders, in parallel; that the graph of the
QC-QC
class has an “upside-down symmetry”; that the graph of the
QC-supFO
class is the “upside-down version” of that of the
QC-CC
class (reflecting the comment made a couple of paragraphs below Eq. (
III.3.2
)).

Figure 12:
Graphical representation of the SDP constraints of Eq. (
III.2.2
), for the
QC-convFO
class with
N=3
.

Figure 13:
Graphical representation of the SDP constraints of Eq. (
III.3.1
), for the
QC-QC
class with
N=3
(top) and
N=4
(bottom, where we drew the edges between the middle two layers with colours to help visualise which matrices are connected together, in the different “branches”). For the
QC-NIQC
class, all matrices that appear in the graph must be valid process matrices (up to normalisation), see Eq. (
III.3.3
).

Notice the upside-down symmetry of the graph: flipping the graph upside-down and relabelling the nodes according to
W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\leftrightarrow W_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}
,
W\leftrightarrow\mathbbm{1}
gives the same graph.

Figure 14:
Graphical representation of the SDP constraints of Eq. (
III.3.2
), for the
QC-supFO
class with
N=3
.

Notice that this graph is the upside-down version of that of the
QC-CC
class (that one obtains by flipping the graph here and relabelling the nodes according to
W\to\mathbbm{1}
,
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\to W_{(k_{N},\ldots,k_{n})}
,
q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\mathbbm{1}\to W_{(k_{N},\ldots,k_{1},F)}
).

Conversely, rather than building the graph corresponding to a given set of SDP constraints, one can take a given graph to define a set of SDP constraints, and thereby a class of quantum circuits.

An “SDP-constraints-defining graph” must be of a certain form:

•

It must be a layered graph, with
N+2
layers for a class of
N
-slot circuits, each being referred to by an integer
n=0,\ldots,N+1
.

•

To each node on a layer
n=1,\ldots,N
, one attaches an
(n-1)-
partite set of parties
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}
, a party labelled by
k_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
(these could be the same for two different nodes on the same layer), and a PSD matrix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
. As a general convention these matrices are denoted
W_{\cdots}^{\cdots}
, where the subscript contains
n
parties: the first
n-1
parties altogether form
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
, while the last party in the subscript is
k_{n}
(so that the notations themselves define
\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}
unambiguously).

•

To each node on layer
n=N+1
one attaches a PSD matrix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})
.

•

To each node on layer
n=0
one attaches a PSD matrix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})
—typically,
\mathbbm{1}
or
q_{\cdots}\mathbbm{1}
, for some
q_{\cdots}\geq 0
.

•

Edges only connect nodes in subsequent layers. The edges between any two subsequent layers
n,n+1
(for
n=0,\ldots,N
) define a so-called “branched relation”
Vanrietvelde et al. 2022
: the sets
{\cal S}_{n},{\cal S}_{n+1}
of matrices in the lower and upper layers are partitioned into
{\cal S}_{n}=\sqcup_{j}{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}
and
{\cal S}_{n+1}=\sqcup_{j}{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}
, with the
{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}
’s and the
{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}
’s being in one-to-one correspondence, such that for each
j
, all matrices in
{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}
are connected to all matrices in
{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}
.
45
45

45

Each layer (each set of matrices
{\cal S}_{n}
), for
n=1,\ldots,N
, is thus partitioned in two different ways,
{\cal S}_{n}=\sqcup_{j}{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}=\sqcup_{k}{\cal S}_{n,k}^{\downarrow}
. The number of subsets in both partitions can be different.

Each pair
{\cal B}_{n,j}=({\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow},{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow})
defines a so-called “branch”.

Furthermore, for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
, it is required that in any branch
{\cal B}_{n,j}=({\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow},{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow})
, the sets
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}
attached to all matrices in
{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}
are the same, and are the same as all sets
\mathcal{K}_{n}
attached to all matrices in
{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}
.
46
46

46

Recall from the second bullet point above that a subset
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
and a party
k_{n}
are attached to each node in
{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}
; similarly, a subset
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and a party
k_{n+1}
are attached to each node in
{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}
.

Any graph of the above form then defines a set of linear constraints (hence, SDP constraints when one further requires that all matrices are PSD) as follows:

•

for all matrices
W_{i}
of the top (
n=N+1
) layer:
W=\sum_{i}W_{i}
;

•

for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
: for any branch
{\cal B}_{n,j}=({\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow},{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow})
,
\sum_{W_{(\cdots,k_{n+1})}^{\cdots}\in{\cal S}_{n+1,j}^{\downarrow}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(\cdots,k_{n+1})}^{\cdots}=\sum_{W_{(\cdots,k_{n})}^{\cdots}\in{\cal S}_{n,j}^{\uparrow}}W_{(\cdots,k_{n})}^{\cdots}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}
;
47
47

47

The last requirement on the general form of an “SDP-constraints-defining graph”, as stated above, ensures that all terms (on both sides of the equality) live in the same space.

•

for all matrices
W_{i}
of the bottom (
n=0
) layer:
\sum_{i}W_{i}=\mathbbm{1}^{P}
.

The set of all
W
’s that satisfy these constraints forms a certain class of process matrices, thus directly defined from the graph under consideration.

One can verify that the graphs in Figs.
11
to
14
are indeed of the required form specified above, and that starting from these graphs, one obtains the constraints that characterise the
QC-CC
,
QC-convFO
,
QC-QC
and
QC-supFO
classes, as given in Eqs. (
III.2.1
), (
III.2.2
), (
III.3.1
), and (
III.3.2
), respectively (for the specified values of
N
). An example of a class only defined through its graph representation will be given in Appendix
E.2.4
(see Fig.
16
); this will illustrate how convenient the graph representation is, compared to the explicit specification of all constraints separately, and how it allows one to nicely visualise their structure.
It would be interesting to explore which further insights can be gained, or which properties of the process matrices can be read, directly from such a graphical representation.

Appendix E
Correlations induced by quantum circuits with classical or quantum control of causal order

In this appendix, we prove the claims that each of the classes of quantum circuits with classical or quantum control of causal order considered in the paper generate correlations in a given set.

Recall that these are given by the generalised Born rule of Eq. (
26
) (considering here trivial global past and future spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
):
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}})^{T}\,W\big]
with
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
,
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
, where for convenience we introduced here the notation
M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}
(not to be confused with the resulting global map
M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}}^{PF}
of Eq. (
27
), in the presence of
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
), and where each
\{M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}\}_{a_{k}}
, for each
x_{k}
, denotes a quantum instrument (in the Choi picture).

E.1
Correlations induced by QC-CCs

Let us start with the correlations induced by QC-CCs. As described in the previous appendix, in these circuits one can identify some control system that encodes in a classical manner the causal order (which may be established dynamically). It is then rather straightforward to see the connection between the (sub)class of QC-CCs under consideration and the type of correlations that can be generated, and to construct the causal decompositions
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
explicitly.

E.1.1
Correlations induced by QC-CCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{causal}}

A process matrix
W\in\textup{{QC-CC}}
has a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.2.1
); recalling that we consider here some trivial global past (
\mathcal{H}^{P}
) and future (
\mathcal{H}^{F}
) spaces, we have in particular that
W=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}
.

Let us then define

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}})^{T}(W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}})],\ \text{such that}\ p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big).

(244)

From Eq. (
III.2.1
) and using the trace-preserving property that
\sum_{a_{k}}\Tr_{A_{k}^{O}}M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k}^{I}}
for each instrument, it is easy to see, recursively, that (for all
n=0,\ldots,N-1
and all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})
)

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})\\
\vec{a}_{k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N}}\end{subarray}}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1})}\big)].

(245)

Indeed, for
n=N-1
,

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{k_{N}}}\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}})^{T}(W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}})]

\displaystyle=\Tr\,\!\Big[\Big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}}}\otimes\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{a_{k_{N}}}}\Tr_{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}M_{a_{k_{N}}|x_{k_{N}}}}_{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{I}}}\Big)^{T}\,W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{N}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\big)].

(246)

Assuming then that Eq. (
245
) holds for some value
n=1,\ldots,N-1
, we have, for
n^{\prime}=n-1
,

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}},k_{n^{\prime}+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}},k_{n^{\prime}+2}}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}},k_{n^{\prime}+1},k_{n^{\prime}+2}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}},k_{n^{\prime}+1}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}},k_{n^{\prime}+2}}\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1}}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+2}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1},k_{n^{\prime}+2})}\big)]

\displaystyle=\Tr\,\!\Big[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}}\!\!\otimes\!\!\!\sum_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}}\!\!\!M_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}|x_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}}\big)^{T}\Big(\underbrace{\!\sum_{k_{n^{\prime}+2}}\!\Tr_{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+2}}^{I}}\!\!W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1},k_{n^{\prime}+2})}\!\!}_{W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}^{O}}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\Tr\,\!\Big[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}}\otimes\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}^{O}}M_{a_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}|x_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}}}_{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}^{I}}}\big)^{T}\,W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}}}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n^{\prime}+1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{\prime}+1})}\big)],

(247)

which shows that Eq. (
245
) holds for the value
n^{\prime}=n-1
, and indeed recursively proves that it holds for all
n=0,\ldots,N-1
.

Eq. (
245
) implies that
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)
does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
, so that the decomposition
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)
from Eq. (
244
) satisfies Eq. (
3
)—which formally proves that the correlations
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
obtained from any QC-CC are causal.

E.1.2
Correlations induced by QC-convFOs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}

By definition a quantum circuit with fixed causal order can only generate correlations that are compatible with the same fixed causal order.

It then straightforwardly follows, by linearity, that any quantum circuit in
QC-convFO
can only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}
.

E.1.3
Correlations induced by QC-NICCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}

Recall that according to Eq. (
III.2.3
), process matrices in
QC-NICC
are QC-CCs with a decomposition in which all
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}
’s (and hence all
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}
’s) are proportional to valid process matrices; let us write
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\,\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, for some fixed weights
q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\geq 0)
and some valid (now properly normalised) process matrices
\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}
.

Recall also that valid process matrices
\tilde{W}
satisfy
\sum_{\vec{a}}\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}}\big)^{T}\,\tilde{W}]=1
, independently of
\vec{x}
.
Hence, for a process matrix in
QC-NICC
and its appropriate decomposition,
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}})^{T}(W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}})]
as defined in Eq. (
244
) above (and which, as we showed already, provides a causal decomposition for
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
satisfying Eq. (
3
)) is then such that
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{\vec{a}}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{\vec{a}}\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}|\vec{x}})^{T}(W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}})]=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, independently of
\vec{x}
(notice that this implies that
\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}=1
). This shows (recalling Eq. (
15
)) that the correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is indeed in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
.

E.2
Correlations induced by QC-QCs

Although QC-QCs can be causally nonseparable
Oreshkov et al. 2012
, and therefore feature some form of indefinite causal orders, it was shown in Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
that they can only generate causal correlations.
This result is less trivial than for QC-CCs. We will present below a new way to write its proof, which provides a causal decomposition of the form
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
, as in Eq. (
3
), and thereby makes more explicit (and may perhaps provide a better intuition of) how the causal order arises in the induced correlations.

E.2.1
Correlations induced by QC-QCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{causal}}

Consider the process matrix
W
of a QC-QC, which has a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.3.1
) (once again, considering here trivial global past and future spaces
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
), and let us define,
\forall\,n=1,\ldots,N,\ \forall\,\mathcal{K}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{N},\ \forall\,k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}
,

\displaystyle s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\big(W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big)]\geq 0

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad f_{{\cal K}_{n}}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\!\!\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})=\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big)\Big]\geq 0.

(248)

With these,
48
48

48

The watchful reader may notice that we introduce here the same definitions for
s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})
and
f_{{\cal K}_{n}}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})
as in the proof of Ref.
Wechs et al. 2021
. Since it is always clear what the arguments
(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})
of
s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}
and
f_{{\cal K}_{n}}
must be, to lighten the notations we shall sometimes just write
s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)
and
f_{{\cal K}_{n}}(\cdot|\cdot)
, as e.g. in Eq. (
249
).

For simplicity we assume in the upcoming equations that all
f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)>0
; otherwise the corresponding summands in Eq. (
249
), i.e.
p((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}|\vec{x})
, are simply 0.

we easily obtain, iteratively, that

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{N}}s_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{k_{N}}f_{{\cal N}\backslash k_{N}}(\cdot|\cdot)\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash k_{N}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{(k_{N-1},k_{N})}\!\!\!\!\!s_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\},k_{N-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash k_{N}}(\cdot|\cdot)}=\!\!\sum_{k_{N-1},k_{N}}\!\!\!\!f_{{\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\},k_{N-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash k_{N}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{(k_{N-2},k_{N-1},k_{N})}\!\!\!\!s_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-2},k_{N-1},k_{N}\},k_{N-2})}(\cdot|\cdot)\,\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\},k_{N-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash\{k_{N-1},k_{N}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\,\frac{s_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{{\cal N}\backslash k_{N}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle=\ \cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\underbrace{s_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}_{\tilde{p}((k_{1}),a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})}\ \underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1}\},k_{2})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\tilde{p}((k_{2}),a_{k_{2}}|(k_{1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},k_{2}},a_{k_{1}})}\hskip-28.45274pt\underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},k_{2}\},k_{3})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{p}((k_{3}),a_{k_{3}}|(k_{1},k_{2}),\\
\hskip 71.13188pt\vec{x}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},k_{2}})\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.53581pt\cdots\hskip-14.22636pt\underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\},k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{p}((k_{N}),a_{k_{N}}|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}),\\
\hskip 85.35826pt\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}})\end{subarray}}
