\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big),

(249)

where we defined, for
n=1,\ldots,N
(and with
f_{\emptyset}(\vec{a}_{\emptyset}|\vec{x}_{\emptyset})\coloneqq 1
),

\displaystyle\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\coloneqq\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}})}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}\geq 0

and

\displaystyle p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\geq 0.

(250)

Using the trace-preserving property (
\sum_{a_{k}}\Tr_{A_{k}^{O}}M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k}^{I}}
), we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{p}\big((k_{n})\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\coloneqq\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt=\frac{\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}})^{T}(W_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}})]}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt=\frac{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}}\otimes\overbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}M_{a_{k_{n}}|x_{k_{n}}}}^{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}}\,)^{T}\,W_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}\Big]}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt=\frac{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}})^{T}\,\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}W_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}\big)\Big]}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}

(251)

does not depend on
x_{k_{n}}
.
Using the constraints of Eq. (
III.3.1
), Eq. (
251
) further leads, for
n\geq 2
and with
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\coloneqq\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}
, to

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n},a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt=\frac{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}})^{\,T}\big(\sum_{k_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\!W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\big)\Big]}{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}})^{\,T}\big(\sum_{k_{n-1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}W_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash k_{n-1}^{\prime},k_{n-1}^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n-1}^{\prime}}^{O}}\big)\Big]}=1,

(252)

and similarly for
n=1
,
\sum_{k_{1},a_{k_{1}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{1}),a_{k_{1}}\big|x_{k_{1}}\big)=\sum_{k_{1}}\Tr[W_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}]=1
.

Hence Eq. (
249
) provides a decomposition of
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
as in Eq. (
81
) of Appendix
A.3
, with each
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)
satisfying the necessary constraints, which indeed proves that the correlations induced by a QC-QC are causal.

E.2.2
Correlations induced by QC-supFOs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}

Consider now a process matrix
W\in\textup{{QC-supFO}}
, which has a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.3.2
) (still with some trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
), and let us now define,
\forall\,n=1,\ldots,N,\ \forall\,\mathcal{K}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{N},\ \forall\,k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n},\ \forall\,(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{n}
,

\displaystyle s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\big(W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big)]\geq 0

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad f_{{\cal K}_{n}}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\!\!\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})=\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big)\Big]\geq 0.

(253)

Following similar calculations as in the first few lines of Eq. (
249
) (just adding the superscripts
{}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
wherever we need them), we can write here
49
49

49

In Eq. (
254
) we again assume, for simplicity, that all
f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{(k_{n},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)>0
; otherwise, the corresponding summands are simply 0. In the first line of Eq. (
255
) we assume that
q_{\pi}>0
; otherwise
\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})
can be taken to be any valid conditional probability distribution.

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\underbrace{s_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}_{q_{\pi}\,\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})}\ \underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1}\},k_{2})}^{(k_{3},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1}\}}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{2}}(a_{k_{2}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},k_{2}},a_{k_{1}})}\ \underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},k_{2}\},k_{3})}^{(k_{4},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}^{(k_{3},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{3}}(a_{k_{3}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}},\\
\hskip 51.21495pt\vec{a}_{k_{1},k_{2}})\end{subarray}}\ \cdots\underbrace{\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}\}}^{(k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{N}}(a_{k_{N}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}},\\
\hskip 56.9055pt\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}})\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}q_{\pi}\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})=\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}q_{\pi}\,p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x}),

(254)

with, for
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})\coloneqq\frac{s_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})}{q_{\pi}}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\coloneqq\frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}})}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{(k_{n},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}\geq 0\ \ \ \text{for}\ n=2,\ldots,N,

and

\displaystyle p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})\coloneqq\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\geq 0.

(255)

Now, using again the trace-preserving property together with the constraints of Eq. (
III.3.2
), we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{k_{1}}}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{1}}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})=\frac{\Tr[(\overbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{a_{k_{1}}}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{O}}M_{a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}}}}^{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}})^{T}\,W_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\Big]}{q_{\pi}}=\frac{\Tr[W_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}^{(k_{2},\ldots,k_{N})}\big]}{q_{\pi}}=1

(256)

and for
n\geq 2
, with
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\coloneqq\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}
(similarly to Eqs. (
251
)–(
252
) before)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}}\!\otimes\!\overbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}}\!\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\!M_{a_{k_{n}}|x_{k_{n}}}}^{\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}})^{T}\,W_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\Big]}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{(k_{n},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}})^{\,T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\big)\Big]}{\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}})^{\,T}\big(\sum_{k_{n-1}^{\prime}\in{\cal K}_{n-1}}\!W_{\!({\cal K}_{n-1}\backslash k_{n-1}^{\prime},k_{n-1}^{\prime})}^{(k_{n},\ldots,k_{N})}\!\otimes\!\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n-1}^{\prime}}^{O}}\big)]}=1.

(257)

Hence for each
n=1,\ldots,N
,
\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})
defines a valid (normalised) probability distribution for party
A_{k_{n}}
’s output, which only depends on the inputs and outputs of the parties in the past of
A_{k_{n}}
, according to the order
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
, and of
A_{k_{n}}
’s own input.
The definition of
p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
in Eq. (
255
) then implies that
p_{\pi}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{j}}(a_{k_{j}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}})
does not depend on
\vec{x}_{k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N}}
, i.e., that
p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
is compatible with the fixed causal order
\pi
.

From the decomposition of Eq. (
254
), we then conclude that process matrices in
QC-supFO
only give correlations that are convex mixtures of correlations with fixed orders, i.e., correlations in
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}
.

E.2.3
Correlations induced by QC-NIQCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}

Consider a process matrix
W
in
QC-NIQC
(still with some trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P},\mathcal{H}^{F}
), i.e., according to Eq. (
III.3.3
), a QC-QC with a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.3.1
), in which all
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
’s are proportional to valid process matrices.

As we have seen in Appendix
E.2.1
above, the correlations induced by such a process matrix are causal, and an explicit causal decomposition for these can be constructed as in Eq. (
249
).
For this decomposition, let us calculate (for any
n=1,\ldots,N
, any
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}
and any
k_{n}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
, using in particular Eq. (
83
)):

\displaystyle p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\!\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\!\!p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},k_{n}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\!\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{p}\big((k_{j}),a_{k_{j}}\big|\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\cancel{s_{(\emptyset,k_{1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \frac{s_{(\{k_{1}\},k_{2})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{\cancel{f_{\{k_{1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}\ \frac{s_{(\{k_{1},k_{2}\},k_{3})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \cdots\ \frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-2}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \frac{s_{(\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\hskip-8.53581pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
(k_{3},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{2}\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt\Big(\cancel{{\textstyle\sum_{k_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}}}\,s_{(\mathcal{K}_{2}\backslash k_{2},k_{2})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\Big)\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{2},k_{3})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{\cancel{f_{\mathcal{K}_{2}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3}\},k_{4})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \cdots

\displaystyle\hskip 270.30118pt\cdots\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3},\ldots,k_{n-2}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{2}\cup\{k_{3},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
(k_{4},\ldots,k_{n-1})\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{3}\end{subarray}}\Big(\cancel{{\textstyle\sum_{k_{3}\in\mathcal{K}_{3}}}\,s_{(\mathcal{K}_{3}\backslash k_{3},k_{3})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\Big)\,\frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{3},k_{4})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{\cancel{f_{\mathcal{K}_{3}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}\cdots\frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{3}\cup\{k_{4},\ldots,k_{n-2}\},k_{n-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{3}\cup\{k_{4},\ldots,k_{n-2}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}\,\frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{3}\cup\{k_{4},\ldots,k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{3}\cup\{k_{4},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\ \ \cdots\ \ =\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-2}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{n-1},\\
k_{n-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{n-2}\end{subarray}}\Big(\cancel{{\textstyle\sum_{k_{n-2}\in\mathcal{K}_{n-2}}}\,s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-2}\backslash k_{n-2},k_{n-2})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\Big)\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-2},k_{n-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{\cancel{f_{\mathcal{K}_{n-2}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-2}\cup\{k_{n-1}\},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{K}_{n-2}\cup\{k_{n-1}\}}(\cdot|\cdot)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\Big(\cancel{\sum_{k_{n-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}\,s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\backslash k_{n-1},k_{n-1})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\Big)\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{\cancel{f_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}}(\cdot|\cdot)}}\ =\ s_{(\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})}(\cdot|\cdot)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n}}})^{T}\big(W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big)].

(258)

By assumption all
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
’s are valid up to normalisation, so that we can write
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}=q_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
, for some weights
q_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}(\geq 0)
and some (properly normalised) valid process matrices
\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}
, which by definition satisfy
\sum_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}}\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}}\big)^{T}\,\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}]=1
, independently of
\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}
. Hence we obtain that

\displaystyle p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n})\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}}p\big((\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}),\vec{a}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle=q_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}\sum_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}}\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}}})^{T}\,\tilde{W}_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}]=q_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}

(259)

does not depend on
\vec{x}
(notice that
\sum_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}q_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}=1
). This proves that the correlations generated by the process matrix
W
in
QC-NIQC
are indeed in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
.

E.2.4
Defining a new class whose correlations are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}

While we showed above that correlations induced by QC-NICCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
(as one may have expected), we could only show that correlations induced by QC-NIQCs are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
, an (in general) strict superset of
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
. This is due to the structure of the quantum control system, which does not encode the full order of past operations, but only their unordered list. It remains an open question, whether one can find correlations from QC-NIQCs that are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}\backslash{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
.

One may also wonder whether one could define any other subclass of QC-NIQCs, larger than that of QC-NICCs, for which we could prove that the correlations are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
. We propose here such a subclass, inspired directly from the structure of the proofs above, by introducing precisely what was missing to prove that the correlations are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
rather than just in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
. The basic intuition is that in order to prove that the correlations are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
, we need each full order
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
to appear “somewhere in the decomposition”, and to have a valid process matrix (up to normalisation) attached to it (so as to generate a probability for that order that does not depend on the instruments).

Specifically, let us first define, for some fixed value of
n^{*}=1,\ldots,N
, the class
\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
to be the set of
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\mathcal{N}}^{IO}F})
,
W\geq 0
such that there exist PSD matrices
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
(for all
n=1,\ldots,n^{*}-1
and all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
),
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}-1}\}}^{IO}A_{k_{n^{*}}}^{I}})
(for all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})
),
W_{({\cal K}_{n-1},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\in{\cal L}(\mathcal{H}^{PA_{{\cal K}_{n-1}}^{IO}A_{k_{n}}^{I}})
(for all strict subsets
{\cal K}_{n-1}
of
{\cal N}
with
n>n^{*}
and all
(k_{n},k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\in{\cal N}\backslash{\cal K}_{n-1}
) such that, defining
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
and
W_{({\cal K}_{n^{*}}\backslash k_{n^{*}},k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}-1})\in{\cal K}_{n^{*}}\backslash k_{n^{*}}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}-1},k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{k_{N}\in{\cal N}}W_{({\cal N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,n=n^{*},\ldots,N-1,\ \forall\,{\cal K}_{n}\subsetneq{\cal N},\,\forall\,(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\in{\cal N}\backslash{\cal K}_{n},\\[2.84526pt]
\hskip 42.67912pt\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{({\cal K}_{n},k_{n+1})}^{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}=\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\forall\,n=1,\ldots,n^{*}-1,\ \forall\,(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\\[2.84526pt]
\hskip 42.67912pt\sum_{k_{n+1}}\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})}=W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}},\\[8.53581pt]
\sum_{k_{1}\in{\cal N}}\Tr_{A_{k_{1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1})}=\mathbbm{1}^{P},\end{array}\right.

and such that

\displaystyle\text{all}\ W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\text{'s are valid process matrices (up to normalisation).}

(266)

49
49
footnotetext:
Notice that this, together with the constraints of Eq. (
E.2.4
), implies that all
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}
’s, for
n=1,\ldots,n^{*}
, are also valid process matrices, up to normalisation.

As an illustration, the above set of constraints is depicted in Fig.
15
for the case of
N=4,n^{*}=2
, using the graphical representation introduced in Appendix
D.4
.

Figure 15:
Graphical representation (as introduced in Appendix
D.4
) of the SDP constraints of Eq. (
E.2.4
), for the
\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
class with
N=4
and
n^{*}=2
. All matrices of the form
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3},k_{4})}
, in the layer corresponding to
n^{*}=2
, are assumed to be valid process matrices (up to normalisation)—which, here, implies the same for the matrices
W_{(k_{1})}
in the lower layer. Compared to the graph for the general
QC-QC
class (for
N=4
as well, shown in Fig.
13
, bottom), one can see that the matrices on the
n^{*}=2
layer split in two matrices each (corresponding to the two possible orders of the future parties)—to help visualise the changes, we kept the same colours as in Fig.
13
(bottom).

For
N=4,n^{*}=3
the structure of the graph would look symmetric to the present one, just upside-down: it is the matrices on the
n^{*}=3
layer that would split in two matrices each (corresponding to the two possible orders of the past parties).

One can see that these classes
\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
interpolate between
QC-NICC
(obtained
50
50

50

More precisely: with a trivial
\mathcal{H}^{F}
(as we consider when only looking at correlations) we get exactly
QC-NICC
for
n^{*}=N
. With a nontrivial
\mathcal{H}^{F}
, for
n^{*}=N
we get in fact a larger class than
QC-NICC
, for which the first two lines of Eq. (
III.2.1
) are replaced by
\Tr_{F}W=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{N}}^{O}}
. (This larger class includes in particular the class
Sup
from Ref.
Liu et al. 2023
, characterised as in Eq. (
D.3.2
); it thus contains QC-QCs that are not QC-CCs.)

for
n^{*}=N
) and
QC-supFO
(obtained for
n^{*}=1
).
51
51

51

Notice also that for
N=3
, the intermediate value
n^{*}=2
recovers the

QC-QC
class.

From these we define the class
QC-NIO
to be their convex hull:

\displaystyle\textup{{QC-NIO}}=\textup{conv}\Big(\bigcup_{n^{*}=1}^{N}\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}\Big),

(267)

which thus includes both
QC-NICC
and
QC-supFO
.
We will now show that process matrices in
QC-NIO
only generate correlations in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
.

By convexity, it suffices to prove that the same claim holds true for process matrices in
\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
, for any fixed value of
n^{*}
.
52
52

52

Since the claim is already known to be true for
n^{*}=N
(the
QC-NICC
class) and for
n^{*}=1
(the
QC-supFO
class), we can restrict in the proof to
1<n^{*}<N
, to avoid bothering with these limit cases.

Consider therefore a process matrix
W\in\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
with a decomposition as in Eq. (
E.2.4
) with the
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
’s being proportional to valid process matrices, and with now some trivial
\mathcal{H}^{P}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}
.
Similarly to what we did before, let us start by defining here, for all subsets
{\cal K}_{n}
of
{\cal N}
with
n\geq n^{*}
, all
k_{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{n}
and all
(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})\in{\cal N}\backslash{\cal K}_{n}
,

\displaystyle s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\big(W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big)]\geq 0

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad f_{{\cal K}_{n}}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\!\!\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!s_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}})=\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{{\cal K}_{n}}|\vec{x}_{{\cal K}_{n}}})^{T}\Big(\sum_{k_{n}\in{\cal K}_{n}}\!\!W_{({\cal K}_{n}\backslash k_{n},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big)\Big]\geq 0.

(268)

Following the first steps of Eq. (
249
), while adding the superscripts
{}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
as in Eq. (
254
), we can write
53
53

53

Still assuming, for simplicity, that all denominators are nonzero. Cf. Footnotes
48
and
49
.

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})}s_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N}\},k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N}\},k_{n^{*}+1})}^{(k_{n^{*}+2},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N}\}}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\ \cdots\ \frac{s_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N})}^{\emptyset}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N}}^{(k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}.

(269)

Let us then further define, for all
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}(\pi,\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}})^{T}\big(W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n^{*}}}^{O}}\big)]\geq 0,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{s_{(\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n},\ldots,k_{N}\},k_{n})}^{(k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}{f_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{n},\ldots,k_{N}\}}^{(k_{n},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)}\geq 0\quad\text{for}\ n=n^{*}+1,\ldots,N.

(270)

From the definition of
W_{({\cal K}_{n^{*}}\backslash k_{n^{*}},k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
just before Eq. (
E.2.4
), it follows that
s_{({\cal K}_{n^{*}}\backslash k_{n^{*}},k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\cdot|\cdot)=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}-1})\in{\cal K}_{n^{*}}\backslash k_{n^{*}}}\tilde{p}(\pi,\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}})
, so that from Eq. (
269
) we can write

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{p}(\pi,\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}})\prod_{n=n^{*}+1}^{N}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\ =\ \sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with}\qquad p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)\coloneqq\tilde{p}(\pi,\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}})\prod_{n=n^{*}+1}^{N}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}).

(271)

Now, it can be shown as in Eq. (
257
) that
\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}_{\pi,k_{n}}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})=1
. Hence for
n\geq n^{*}
and any
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})
,
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{N}}}p\big(\pi{=}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{p}\big(\pi{=}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big)\prod_{j=n^{*}+1}^{n}\tilde{p}_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),k_{j}}(a_{k_{j}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}})
does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
.
For
n<n^{*}
on the other hand, and still any
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1})
, it can be shown recursively from the second to last constraint in Eq. (
E.2.4
) (in a similar way to Eqs. (
245
)–(
247
), and using the definition of
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}
just before Eq. (
E.2.4
) for the case
n=n^{*}-1
) that
p\big((k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\sum_{(k_{n+2},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{n+1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}}\tilde{p}\big(\pi{=}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big)=\Tr[\big(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}}\big)^{T}\big(\Tr_{A_{k_{n+1}}^{I}}W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1})}\big)\big]
, which also does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
.

All in all, we thus find that Eq. (
271
) provides a causal decomposition of
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})
that satisfies Eq. (
3
).
Recalling that each
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
is assumed to be proportional to a valid process matrix, we can write
W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})}\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
, for some fixed weight
q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})}
and some now properly normalised process matrix
\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}
. We then have, for all
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})
,

\displaystyle p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})\big|\vec{x}\big)\,=\,\sum_{\vec{a}}p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}}\tilde{p}\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})}\underbrace{{\textstyle\sum_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}}}\Tr[(M_{\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}}}})^{T}\big(\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{A_{k_{n^{*}}}^{O}}\big)\big]}_{=1\ (\text{validity and normalisation of }\tilde{W}_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}})}^{(k_{n^{*}+1},\ldots,k_{N})})}=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n^{*}},\ldots,k_{N})},

(272)

which does not depend on
\vec{x}
and concludes the proof that the correlations are in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
.

Beyond the
QC-NIO
class introduced here, one may also construct even larger classes whose correlations are restricted to be in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
. E.g., one can conceive classes of a similar type as
\textup{{QC-NIO}}_{n^{*}}
, but allowing for different critical values
n^{*}
attached to different orders
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
(and then considering convex combinations of such classes). Formalising this properly becomes rather tedious—we only illustrate it with a specific example, depicted in Fig.
16
using the graphical representation of Appendix
D.4
.

Figure 16:
An example of a class of quantum circuits with
N=4
, defined through the graphical representation of Appendix
D.4
. Assuming that the matrices of the form
W_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{(k_{3},k_{4})}
(in the left part of the graph) and of the form
W_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})}^{(k_{4})}
(in the right part) are valid process matrices (up to normalisation), one can show that this class only generates correlations in
{\cal P}_{\textup{NIO}}
.

E.3
Obtaining any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
or
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
with a QC-convFO or a QC-CC

Here we show, for any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
, how to construct a convex mixture of quantum—and even classical—circuits with fixed causal order that generates it; and similarly for any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
, how it can be obtained from a quantum—and even classical—circuit with classical control of causal order.

The basic idea of the construction is that the correlation one wishes to reproduce is established “inside” the process matrix. The process just indicates to each party what they should output, for each of their possible inputs; the party then does as instructed and indicate to the process which was their input.

To formalise this, we attribute to each party
A_{k}
an input Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}
whose basis states describe the list of all possible outputs for the party, for all of its possible inputs: these are taken to be of the form
{|\vec{\alpha}_{k}\rangle}^{A_{k}^{I}}
, with
\vec{\alpha}_{k}=(\alpha_{k,x_{k}})_{x_{k}}
and with the understanding that each
\alpha_{k,x_{k}}
corresponds to the output that the party should return when their input is
x_{k}
. As for the output Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
, its basis states are taken to be of the form
{|x_{k}\rangle}^{A_{k}^{O}}
, so as to encode the party’s input. The (classical) instrument consisting in reading off from
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{I}}
the value
a_{k}
to be outputted for input
x_{k}
, and sending out in
\mathcal{H}^{A_{k}^{O}}
the value of that input, is then described in the Choi picture by

\displaystyle M_{a_{k}|x_{k}}=\sum_{\vec{\alpha}_{k}}\,\delta_{a_{k},\alpha_{k,x_{k}}}\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k}]\!]^{A_{k}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k}]\!]^{A_{k}^{O}},

(273)

where we again used the notation
[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k}]\!]^{A_{k}^{I}}\coloneqq{|\vec{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\!\langle\vec{\alpha}_{k}|}^{A_{k}^{I}}
and
[\![x_{k}]\!]^{A_{k}^{O}}\coloneqq{|x_{k}\rangle\!\langle x_{k}|}^{A_{k}^{O}}
for effectively classical states.

E.3.1
Any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\textup{convFO}}
can be obtained from a QC-convFO

Consider a correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
, i.e. a mixture of correlations
p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
compatible with the fixed causal order
\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})
.
Recalling from Eq. (
2
) that (for
n=1,\ldots,N-1
)
p_{\pi}(\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}}|\vec{x})
does not depend on
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}}
, one can thus decompose
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
as

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}q_{\pi}\,p_{\pi}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})\quad

\displaystyle\text{with }q_{\pi}\geq 0,\ {\textstyle\sum}_{\pi}q_{\pi}=1,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad p_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}(\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle=p_{\pi}(a_{k_{1}}|x_{k_{1}})\,p_{\pi}(a_{k_{2}}|x_{k_{1}},a_{k_{1}},x_{k_{2}})\cdots p_{\pi}(a_{k_{N}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N-1}},x_{k_{N}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=1}^{N}p_{\pi}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},x_{k_{n}})\,.

(274)

Let us then define, for any
n=1,\ldots,N
, with
\vec{\alpha}_{k_{j}}=(\alpha_{k_{j},x_{k_{j}}})_{x_{k_{j}}}
as above, and with the shorthand notation
\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\coloneqq(\alpha_{k_{1},x_{k_{1}}},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{n-1},x_{k_{n-1}}})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\coloneqq\prod_{x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}}p_{\pi}(\alpha_{k_{n},x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}),

(275)

such that (for some
a_{k_{n}},x_{k_{n}}
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}}\delta_{a_{k_{n}},\alpha_{k_{n},x_{k_{n}}}}\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})=p_{\pi}(a_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},x_{k_{n}}),

(276)

and

\displaystyle W\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}q_{\pi}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{N}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{N}}\end{subarray}}\ \bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\Big(\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big).

(277)

It can be verified that
W
thus defined is the process matrix of a QC-convFO,
54
54

54

Indeed, it has a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.2.2
), with

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}

\displaystyle=q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}{\textstyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{N}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{N}}\end{subarray}}}\ {\textstyle\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}}\big(\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big),

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}

\displaystyle=q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}{\textstyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{n-1}}\end{subarray}}}\big({\textstyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n-1}}\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{j}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{j}}]\!]^{A_{k_{j}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{j}}]\!]^{A_{k_{j}}^{O}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\otimes\big(\tilde{q}(\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\big),

where
q_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}\coloneqq\sum_{(k_{n+1}\ldots,k_{N})}q_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},k_{n+1}\ldots,k_{N})}
.

and that with the choice of instruments of Eq. (
273
) for each of the parties, it reproduces the correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
under consideration, as desired.

E.3.2
Any correlation in
{\cal P}_{\textup{causal}}
can be obtained from a QC-CC

Consider now a correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
, which can be decomposed as in Eq. (
81
):

\displaystyle p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})=\sum_{\pi}p(\pi,\vec{a}|\vec{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with}\quad p\big(\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}),\vec{a}\big|\vec{x}\big)=\prod_{n=1}^{N}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big),

(278)

such that (for all
n
)
\sum_{a_{k_{n}}}\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)
does not depend on
x_{k_{n}}
.

The latter requirement allows us to write
\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)=\tilde{p}\big((k_{n})\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\,\tilde{p}\big(a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\vec{a}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},x_{k_{n}}\big)
.
Following a similar construction as above, let us then define

\displaystyle\tilde{q}\big((k_{n}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{p}\big((k_{n})\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\prod_{x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}}\tilde{p}\big(\alpha_{k_{n},x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},k_{n}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},x_{k_{n}}^{\prime}\big),

(279)

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}}\delta_{a_{k_{n}},\alpha_{k_{n},x_{k_{n}}}}\tilde{q}\big((k_{n}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)=\tilde{p}\big((k_{n}),a_{k_{n}}\big|(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}),\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big),

(280)

and

\displaystyle W\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N})}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{N}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{N}}\end{subarray}}\ \bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\Big(\tilde{q}\big((k_{n}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}\big|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\big)\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\Big).

(281)

It can be verified here that
W
thus defined is the process matrix of a QC-CC,
55
55

55

Indeed, it has a decomposition as in Eq. (
III.2.1
), with

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{N},F)}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{N}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{N}}\end{subarray}}}\ {\textstyle\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}}\big(\tilde{q}((k_{n}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{O}}\big),

\displaystyle W_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}_{k_{1}},\ldots,\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}},\\
x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{n-1}}\end{subarray}}}\ \big({\textstyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n-1}}\tilde{q}((k_{j}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{j}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{j}}]\!]^{A_{k_{j}}^{I}}\otimes[\![x_{k_{j}}]\!]^{A_{k_{j}}^{O}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\otimes\big(\tilde{q}((k_{n}),\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}|\vec{x}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}},\overrightarrow{[\alpha_{x}]}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}})\,[\![\vec{\alpha}_{k_{n}}]\!]^{A_{k_{n}}^{I}}\big).

and that with the choice of instruments of Eq. (
273
) for each of the parties, it reproduces the correlation
p(\vec{a}|\vec{x})
under consideration, as desired.

E.3.3
Saturating the bounds (
18
)–(
21
) on
I_{4}
with quantum circuits

While the constructions above are quite general and allow one to find process matrices reproducing any desired correlation in
{\cal P}_{\text{convFO}}
or
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
, in some cases (in particular, if some party does not signal to any other party, or if only limited communication is required) there also exist simpler process matrix realisations.

For instance, the correlation
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)=\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\,\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}
(given below Eq. (
18
)), compatible with any causal order where
C\prec D
and which saturates
I_{4}\leq_{\text{convFO}}\frac{15}{16}
, can be rather trivially obtained from the QC-FO
W=[\![\mathbbm{1}]\!]^{C_{O}D_{I}}\coloneqq\sum_{z=0,1}[\![z]\!]^{C_{O}}\otimes[\![z]\!]^{D_{I}}\in\textup{{QC-convFO}}\subset\textup{{QC-supFO}}
, representing a classical identity (or quantum dephasing) channel between
\mathcal{H}^{C_{O}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{D_{I}}
(with trivial systems for
A
,
B
,
C_{I}
and
D_{O}
), and with the instruments
M_{a|x}^{A}=\delta_{a,0}
,
M_{b|y}^{B}=\delta_{b,0}
,
M_{c|z}^{C}=\delta_{c,0}[\![z]\!]^{C_{O}}
,
M_{0|t=0}^{D}=\mathbbm{1}^{D_{I}}
and
M_{d|t=1}^{D}=[\![d]\!]^{D_{I}}
.

Similarly, one can obtain the correlation
p(a,b,c,d|x,y,z,t)=\delta_{a,0}\,\delta_{b,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{c,0}\,\delta_{d,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=0\\
\delta_{d,0}\,\delta_{c,zt}&\text{if}\ xy=1\end{array}\right.
of Eq. (
•
‣
II.4
), which saturates the trivial bound
I_{4}\leq_{\text{causal}}1
, with
W=([\![0,0]\!]+[\![0,1]\!]+[\![1,0]\!])^{A_{O}B_{O}}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{C_{I}}\otimes[\![\mathbbm{1}]\!]^{C_{O}D_{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D_{O}}+[\![1,1]\!]^{A_{O}B_{O}}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{D_{I}}\otimes[\![\mathbbm{1}]\!]^{D_{O}C_{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C_{O}}\in\textup{{QC-CC}}\subset\textup{{QC-QC}}
(with trivial systems for
A_{I},B_{I}
) and the instruments
M_{a|x}^{A}=\delta_{a,0}{|x\rangle\!\langle x|}^{A_{O}}
,
M_{b|y}^{B}=\delta_{b,0}{|y\rangle\!\langle y|}^{B_{O}}
,
M_{0|z=0}^{C}=\mathbbm{1}^{C_{I}}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{C_{O}}
,
M_{c|z=1}^{C}=[\![c]\!]^{C_{I}}\otimes[\![1]\!]^{C_{O}}
,
M_{0|t=0}^{D}=\mathbbm{1}^{D_{I}}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{D_{O}}
and
M_{d|t=1}^{D}=[\![d]\!]^{D_{I}}\otimes[\![1]\!]^{D_{O}}
.

Let us now turn to the correlation with non-influenceable causal order of Eq. (
C.2
), which saturates the
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}
bound.
Applying our construction above for correlations in
{\cal P}_{\text{causal}}
, we find that it can be obtained from the process matrix

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{\rightarrow}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{C_{I}}\otimes[\![\mathbbm{1}]\!]^{C_{O}D_{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D_{O}}+W_{\leftarrow}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{D_{I}}\otimes[\![\mathbbm{1}]\!]^{D_{O}C_{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C_{O}}

(282)

with

\displaystyle W_{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\mathbbm{1}^{A_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{O}}\mathbbm{1}^{B_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathbbm{1}^{A_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{O}},

\displaystyle W_{\leftarrow}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{8}}\mathbbm{1}^{A_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{O}}\mathbbm{1}^{B_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathbbm{1}^{A_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{O}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}[\![0]\!]^{A_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{A_{O}}[\![0]\!]^{B_{I}}[\![1]\!]^{B_{O}}

(283)

(with implicit tensor products), together with Alice’s instruments
M_{0|x=0}^{A}=\mathbbm{1}^{A_{I}}\otimes[\![0]\!]^{A_{O}}
and
M_{a|x=1}^{D}=[\![a]\!]^{A_{I}}\otimes[\![1]\!]^{A_{O}}
, and with the three other parties’ instruments defined in the same way.
Noting in particular that
(W_{\rightarrow}+W_{\leftarrow})
defines a process matrix compatible with the fixed order
A\prec B
, it can be seen that
W\in\textup{{QC-CC}}
, with Eq. (
282
) readily providing a QC-CC decomposition as in Eq. (
III.2.1
). However,
W_{\rightarrow}
and
W_{\leftarrow}
, individually, are
not
valid process matrices, and Eq. (
III.2.1
) does not give a QC-NICC decomposition. In fact
W
above can generate correlations with influenceable causal order, hence
W\notin\textup{{QC-NICC}}
.
As we explain in the main text, we were not able to saturate the
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}}\frac{31}{32}
bound with process matrices in
QC-NICC
.

The same holds for the
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}\frac{47}{48}
bound: one can construct the process matrix of a QC-QC that reproduces the correlation with non-influenceable coarse-grained order of Eq. (
C.3
), which saturates this bound. However that process matrix is not a QC-NIQC; we were also not able to saturate the
I_{4}\leq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}\frac{47}{48}
bound with process matrices in
QC-NIQC
.

E.4
Dynamical correlations from quantum circuits with non-influenceable control of causal order

As mentioned in the main text, we could not find any violation of our inequality (
18
),
I_{4}\underset{\text{convFO}}{\leq}\frac{15}{16}
, with the explicit examples of process matrices
W_{\textup{NICC}}
and
W_{\textup{NIQC}}
that we presented in the main text (Eqs. (
43
) and (
60
)). The violations reported in Fig.
8
were instead obtained numerically, with process matrices and instruments that are difficult to interpret.
Nonetheless, we were able to come up with another inequality that also bounds correlations in
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, and find explicit analytical violations with
W_{\textup{NICC}}
as well as with another example
W_{\textup{NIQC}}^{\prime}\in\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\setminus(\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\cup\text{{{QC-NICC}}})
that is defined below.

We recall that given a convex polytope
\mathcal{P}
, which can be characterised in terms of its
n_{\mathcal{P}}
vertices
\{\vec{p}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\mathcal{P}}}
, one can use the following linear program to test whether a correlation vector
\vec{p}
is inside or outside the polytope:

\displaystyle\min_{r,\vec{\lambda}}r\quad\text{s.t.}\quad

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{\lambda}=\vec{p}+r\vec{u},\ \vec{\lambda}\geq 0,\ r\geq 0

(284)

where
\vec{u}=\frac{1}{n_{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathcal{P}}}\vec{p}_{i}
, and
M_{\mathcal{P}}
is the matrix formed by the vectors
\{\vec{p}_{i}\}_{i}
in column. If the optimal value
r^{*}
is zero, then the correlation vector
\vec{p}
belongs to the polytope, while if
r^{*}>0
, then the correlation vector
\vec{p}
is outside of the polytope, and one can consider the dual optimisation problem to Eq. (
284
) to derive an inequality satisfied by any point in
\mathcal{P}
and violated by the correlation of interest
\vec{p}
.

To produce such an inequality and show that a correlation created by
W_{\textup{NICC}}
in the fourpartite lazy scenario is outside
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, we considered the following quantum instruments for each party
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
:

\displaystyle M^{A}_{0|0}=M^{B}_{0|0}=\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|},\quad M^{A}_{0|1}=M^{B}_{0|1}={|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|},\quad M^{A}_{1|1}=M^{B}_{1|1}={|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|},

\displaystyle M^{C}_{0|0}=M^{D}_{0|0}=\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|},\quad M^{C}_{0|1}=M^{D}_{0|1}={|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}\otimes{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|},\quad M^{C}_{1|1}=M^{D}_{1|1}={|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}\otimes{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}.

(285)

The instruments for parties
A
and
B
correspond to the ones chosen in Ref.
Branciard et al. 2015
to show that
W_{+\alpha}
violates the LGYNI inequality: on input
0
,
A
and
B
output
0
(as required here in the lazy scenario), and simply transmit the physical system they received, untouched; while on input
1
, they perform a measurement in the
Z
basis, output its classical outcome, and send out a physical system in the state
{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}
. The parties
C
and
D
act in a similar way to
A
and
B
, except that on input
1
they send out a physical system in the state
{|1\rangle\!\langle 1|}
. Solving the optimisation problem of Eq. (
284
) for the correlation vector
\vec{p}_{\textup{NICC}}=(p_{\textup{NICC}}(\vec{a}|\vec{x}))_{\vec{a},\vec{x}}
,
defined with the process
W_{\textup{NICC}}
and the instruments of Eq. (
285
) using generalised Born rule (see Eq. (
26
)), we obtained
r^{*}_{\textup{NICC}}\simeq 0.063>0
, proving that
\vec{p}_{\textup{NICC}}
is outside
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
. Solving the corresponding dual optimisation problem, we could derive the following inequality, which is satisfied by any correlation in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
but violated by
\vec{p}_{\textup{NICC}}
:

\displaystyle I_{4}^{\prime}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\ p(a{=}1,c{=}0|x{=}1,y{=}0,z{=}1,t{=}1)+p(a{=}1,c{=}1|x{=}1,y{=}0,z{=}1,t{=}0)

\displaystyle\ +p(b{=}1,c{=}0|x{=}0,y{=}1,z{=}1,t{=}1)+p(b{=}1,c{=}1|x{=}0,y{=}1,z{=}1,t{=}0)

\displaystyle\ +p(a{=}1,b{=}1,d{=}1|x{=}1,y{=}1,z{=}0,t{=}1)-p(a{=}1,b{=}1,c{=}0,d{=}1|x{=}1,y{=}1,z{=}1,t{=}1)\underset{\text{convFO}}{\geq}0.

(286)

This inequality can indeed be verified to hold for all the 481 776 (deterministic) vertices of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}

(and thus for any point in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
, since any correlation in this polytope can be expressed as a convex combination of its vertices).
One can then see that the correlation vector
\vec{p}_{\textup{NICC}}
violates this inequality, reaching
I_{4}^{\prime}=\frac{6-5\sqrt{2}}{16}\simeq-0.067<0
.

We could not find any analogous violation with
W_{\text{NIQC}}
, or any correlation outside of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
. Nevertheless, we could come up with another example of a fourpartite quantum circuit with dynamical but non-influenceable quantum control of causal order in
\text{{{QC-NIQC}}}\setminus(\text{{{QC-supFO}}}\penalty\ \cup\penalty\ \text{{{QC-NICC}}})
, and show that this process violates Eq. (
286
) with the same instruments as those considered in Eq. (
285
) above.
This process is defined analogously to
W_{\text{NICC}}
from Eq. (
43
), replacing
W_{-\alpha}
by
W_{+\beta}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 4}+\beta(ZZZ\mathbbm{1}+Z\mathbbm{1}XX))\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{{A^{IO}B^{IO}}})
with
\beta=1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
as
56
56

56

As already mentioned in the main text, one can construct an explicit “witness” to prove that this process is indeed not in
QC-NICC
, using the technique of causal witnesses
Araújo et al. 2015
;
Branciard 2016
.

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{\textup{NIQC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}W_{+\alpha}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{C^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{C^{O}D^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{D^{O}}+\frac{1}{2}W_{+\beta}\otimes{|0\rangle\!\langle 0|}^{D^{I}}\otimes\mathinner{|{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\mathbbm{1}}|}^{D^{O}C^{I}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{C^{O}}.

(287)

One also obtains
I_{4}^{\prime}=\frac{6-5\sqrt{2}}{16}\simeq-0.067<0
for the correlation thus generated, showing that it is indeed outside of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
.

Let us provide, for completeness, the other bounds on the expression
I_{4}^{\prime}
for the other polytopes considered in this work: we find
I_{4}^{\prime}{\geq}_{\text{NIO}}-\frac{1}{2}
,
I_{4}^{\prime}\geq_{\text{NIO}^{\prime}}-\frac{2}{3}
,
I_{4}^{\prime}\geq_{\text{causal}}-1
. While, by construction,
I_{4}^{\prime}\geq_{\text{convFO}}0
is a facet of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{convFO}}
, we do not expect these other bounds to define facets of the corresponding polytopes.

The technique we used above, consisting of cooking up some particular correlation and finding the facet of the
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
polytope “right below” it, could be exploited further to find other inequalities, possibly with some particular form, or some nice interpretation in terms of games. As explained in the main text, it is much more challenging to obtain facet inequalities for the
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
polytopes. Still, for some specific expression obtained as a facet for
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{convFO}}
, one can easily obtain the corresponding bound for
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{NIO}^{\prime}}
(and potentially test if, by any chance, it also corresponds to a facet of these polytopes).
